Template injection (memorized canonical values regardless of input) identified as primary hallucination mechanism.

Conclusion: Numerical hallucination in financial summarization is driven by degradation of numerical restraint through domain adaptation, not by insufficient numerical reasoning. Recommendations: evaluation protocols should assess hallucination across all detectability levels, and deployment practices should include explicit mechanisms for grounding-aware generation or abstention.

Abstract: Financial large language models are increasingly deployed for summarization of reports and disclosures, where numerical hallucination poses significant practical risks. While prior work often attributes such hallucination to insufficient numerical reasoning, this assumption has not been systematically tested under controlled fine-tuning settings. In this paper, we conduct a cost-effective, controlled study of numerical hallucination in financial summarization across three model variants: a base instruction-tuned model, a domain language-adapted model (FT-A), and a numeracy-enhanced domain model (FT-A+B+C). We introduce a three-level detectability taxonomy distinguishing between overt hallucination (currency-denominated fabrication), covert-explicit hallucination (professional-convention numbers), and covert-implicit hallucination (ungrounded quantitative claims). Our results reveal that domain fine-tuning substantially degrades numerical restraint at all detectability levels. While the Base model maintains near-zero hallucination rates (5.4\%), FT-A exhibits 82.5\% overt hallucination and FT-A+B+C reaches 98\%. Contrary to intuition, numeracy supervision amplifies rather than mitigates hallucination across all levels. We identify template injection---the insertion of memorized canonical values regardless of input content---as a primary hallucination mechanism in fine-tuned models. These findings demonstrate that numerical hallucination in financial summarization is driven by the degradation of numerical restraint through domain adaptation, not by insufficient numerical reasoning. We recommend that evaluation protocols assess hallucination across all detectability levels and that deployment practices include explicit mechanisms for grounding-aware generation or abstention.

</details>


### [50] [CPR-IE:A Compression-Prediction-Resource Intelligence Efficiency Metric](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04809)
*Xiantao Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CPR-IE is a framework for comparing AI systems based on compression, prediction accuracy, and resource consumption, with mathematical properties for consistent ranking and aggregation across tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI system evaluation focuses too much on predictive accuracy while ignoring important constraints like computational resources, memory, and energy consumption. There's a need for a comprehensive framework that considers representational economy, predictive quality, and resource burden simultaneously for fair comparison under deployment constraints.

Method: Developed Compression-Prediction-Resource Intelligence Efficiency (CPR-IE) using mathematical formalization with proportional-increment composition (logarithmic cumulative burden) and context-independent ratio response (power responses to compression, prediction, burden). The approach includes theoretical proofs for consistency properties, translog parent models, and establishes ranking guarantees and aggregation methods.

Result: Proved mathematical properties including Pareto consistency, unit invariance, boundary behavior, trade-off identities, ranking-stability regions, and cross-task aggregation. Established cardinal/ordinal identification, sub-Gaussian finite-sample ranking guarantees, robust selection under uncertainty, and deterministic regret bounds. Showed that while MDL, algorithmic complexity, scoring rules, variational inference, and Landauer's principle motivate measurement choices, they don't determine the specific formula.

Conclusion: CPR-IE provides a rigorous, constructed efficiency representation for comparing intelligent systems that considers multiple dimensions beyond just accuracy. It's a practical evaluation framework rather than a universal definition of intelligence or a natural law, offering mathematically sound tools for system comparison under realistic deployment constraints.

Abstract: Comparing intelligent systems under deployment constraints requires more than predictiveaccuracy.This paper develops Compression-Prediction-Resource Intelligence Efficiency (CPR-IE) as a protocol-relative ordering by representational economy, predictive quality, and resourceburden. The analysis separates two questions-how raw resource consumption is represented, andhow the resulting attributes are aggregated. Proportional-increment composition uniquely yieldslogarithmic cumulative burden, and context-independent ratio response yields power responsesto compression, prediction, and burden; with reference normalization the representation is I(C,P,T).We prove Pareto consistency, unit invariance, boundary behavior, trade-off identities, ranking-stability regions, and cross-task aggregation. A translog parent model makes interaction restrictions explicit, and further results establish cardinal and ordinal identification, sub-Gaussianfinite-sample ranking guarantees, robust selection under exponent uncertainty, and deterministicregret bounds. Minimum description length, algorithmic complexity, proper scoring rules, varia-tional inference, and Landauer's principle motivate measurement choices but do not entail theformula. CPR-IE is a constructed efficiency representation, not a universal law or a definition ofintelligence itself.

</details>


### [51] [Long Horizon Transformer Quantile Fault Prediction for Multi Site Industrial Predictive Maintenance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04840)
*David J Poland,Daniele Ravi,Na Helian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TQRNN30d framework combines quantile regression neural networks with temporal fusion for 30-day predictive maintenance, achieving state-of-the-art performance on machine-disjoint evaluation within a homogeneous manufacturing fleet.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon predictive maintenance needs to distinguish slow degradation from normal operating variations over extended periods (days rather than hours), requiring specialized modeling approaches that can capture temporal dependencies and uncertainty over extended windows.

Method: Proposes TQRNN30d: dual-stage quantile regression neural network (QRNN) feature extractor maps hourly machine behavior to quantile-state representations, combined with multi-stream temporal fusion classifier that fuses quantile states, dynamic covariates, static metadata, and latent history using gated residual processing, causal recurrent encoding, and cross-modal attention, with auxiliary memory modulation from prediction-error divergence.

Result: Achieves 79.97% F1, 80.18% recall, 81.82% precision, 82.39% accuracy, and 0.820 ROC-AUC at 30-day horizon, outperforming all 18 baselines at 7-, 14-, and 30-day comparisons with largest advantage at 14 days.

Conclusion: The explicit conditional-quantile representation provides an informative classifier interface for long-horizon predictive maintenance, demonstrating strong performance within homogeneous fleets but with limited generalizability guarantees for unseen sites, equipment types, or different sectors.

Abstract: Long-horizon predictive maintenance requires models to distinguish slowly evolving degradation from normal operating-regime variation over planning windows measured in days rather than hours. This paper evaluates whether an explicit conditional-quantile representation provides an informative classifier interface for this problem. The proposed TQRNN30d framework combines a dual-stage quantile regression neural network (QRNN) feature extractor with a multi-stream temporal fusion classifier. Each hourly word of 81-channel machine behaviour is mapped to a 324-dimensional quantile-state representation, and 720 ordered hourly words form the 30-day document supplied to the long-horizon model. The classifier fuses quantile states with dynamic covariates, channel-level static metadata, and a 168-hour latent-history stream using gated residual processing, causal recurrent encoding, and metadata-conditioned cross-modal attention. A bounded instability-aware signal derived from sustained one-word-ahead prediction-error divergence provides auxiliary memory modulation at the longest horizon. Evaluation uses a machine-disjoint 43/14/15 train/validation/test allocation across 72 machines in nine manufacturing facilities. At 30 days, TQRNN30d achieves 79.97% F1, 80.18% recall, 81.82% precision, 82.39% accuracy, and 0.820 ROC-AUC. It leads all 18 evaluated baselines at the 7-, 14-, and 30-day fixed-threshold comparisons, with the largest F1 advantage at 14 days. The results support held-out-machine performance within the observed homogeneous nine-facility fleet, but do not establish unseen-site, cross-equipment, or cross-sector generalisation.

</details>


### [52] [ElderBench: Benchmarking Autonomous Mobile Agents for Older Adults](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04850)
*Weide Zhan,Qumu Shaqu,Yuanqing Liu,Peng Zhang,Jiahao Liu,Kam Him Lam,Ning Gu,Zhan Hu,Tun Lu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ElderBench is the first benchmark for evaluating mobile GUI agents in authentic elderly-oriented scenarios, created from 249 naturally collected smartphone tasks from older adults across 20 apps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GUI benchmarks rely on explicit, goal-oriented instructions that don't capture the natural language patterns of older users (indirect speech, referential ambiguity, under-specified requests). This mismatch hinders reliable agent deployment for elderly assistance.

Method: Constructed ElderBench from 249 naturally elicited smartphone tasks collected from older adults across 20 applications. Characterized linguistic divergence between elderly instructions and existing benchmarks from syntactic, semantic, and pragmatic perspectives. Evaluated mainstream GUI agents and Vision-Language Models under online and offline settings, with controlled instruction normalization, failure analysis, and fine-grained linguistic feature analysis.

Result: Revealed substantial performance degradation of GUI agents when handling elderly-oriented instructions. Identified how elderly-specific language patterns (indirect speech, ambiguity, under-specification) contribute to agent failures. Found that existing agents struggle with natural elderly language despite good performance on explicit instructions.

Conclusion: The study provides actionable design insights toward more adaptive, interpretable, and age-inclusive GUI agents for older adults. ElderBench serves as a valuable benchmark for evaluating and improving agents' ability to handle real-world elderly interaction patterns.

Abstract: While autonomous mobile agents hold great potential for assisting older adults with smartphone usage, existing GUI benchmarks mainly rely on explicit, goal-oriented instructions and rarely capture the naturally occurring language patterns of older users, such as indirect speech, referential ambiguity, and under-specified requests. This mismatch between benchmark instructions and real-world elderly interactions may hinder reliable agent deployment. To address this gap, we present ElderBench, the first benchmark for evaluating mobile GUI agents in authentic elderly-oriented scenarios. ElderBench is constructed from 249 naturally elicited smartphone tasks collected from older adults across 20 applications. We first characterize the linguistic divergence between elderly instructions and existing GUI benchmark instructions from syntactic, semantic, and pragmatic perspectives. We then evaluate mainstream GUI agents and Vision-Language Models under both online and offline settings, revealing substantial performance degradation when handling elderly-oriented instructions. Through controlled instruction normalization, failure analysis, and fine-grained linguistic feature analysis, we further identify how elderly-specific language patterns contribute to agent failures. Our findings provide actionable design insights toward more adaptive, interpretable, and age-inclusive GUI agents for older adults.

</details>


### [53] [MM-IFEval-Pro: A Multilingual and Attack-Resistant Benchmark for Instruction-Following in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04859)
*Changming Xiao,Zhenliang Ni,Jinhui He,Han Shu,Jie Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MM-IFEval-Pro is a comprehensive multimodal instruction-following benchmark addressing limitations in existing benchmarks by covering Chinese and English tasks with diverse adversarial safety scenarios and instruction hijacking cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal instruction-following benchmarks have two main limitations: 1) limited language coverage (inadequate for real-world multilingual settings), and 2) insufficient adversarial safety scenarios (inadequate for safety-sensitive applications). As VLMs advance in image understanding and complex instruction execution, there's a need for better benchmarks to evaluate their reliability and practicality.

Method: The authors present MM-IFEval-Pro, which includes: 4 major task categories with 24 subcategories, 8 instruction categories with 52 subcategories, with each sample containing an average of 3.0 constraints. They also construct a reinforcement-learning training set enriched with Chinese and adversarial instructions.

Result: The reinforcement-learning training set significantly improves model performance on MM-IFEval-Pro and demonstrates strong cross-task and cross-language generalization by transferring effectively to other mainstream multimodal benchmarks.

Conclusion: MM-IFEval-Pro addresses critical gaps in multimodal instruction-following evaluation by providing comprehensive coverage of multilingual tasks and adversarial safety scenarios, with the accompanying training set showing promising generalization capabilities across different benchmarks.

Abstract: As vision-language models (VLMs) rapidly advance in image understanding, cross-modal reasoning, and complex instruction execution, instruction-following capability has become a key indicator of their reliability and practicality. However, existing multimodal instruction-following benchmarks still suffer from limited language coverage and insufficient adversarial safety scenarios, making them inadequate for evaluating real-world multilingual and safety-sensitive settings. To address these gaps, we present MM-IFEval-Pro, a multimodal instruction-following benchmark covering Chinese and English tasks as well as diverse instruction hijacking cases. MM-IFEval-Pro includes 4 major task categories and 24 subcategories and 8 instruction categories with 52 subcategories, with each sample containing an average of 3.0 constraints to realistically simulate complex instruction scenarios. We further construct a reinforcement-learning training set enriched with Chinese and adversarial instructions, which significantly improves model performance on MM-IFEval-Pro and transfers effectively to other mainstream multimodal benchmarks, demonstrating strong cross-task and cross-language generalization.

</details>


### [54] [MZ-Rain: Moisture-Budget-Guided Zero-Inflated Model for Station-Level Precipitation Nowcasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04864)
*Yifang Zhang,Shengwu Xiong,Henan Wang,Wenjie Yin,Yuqiang Zhang,Chen Zhou,Hua Chen,Qile Zhao,Pengfei Duan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MZ-Rain is a physics-guided LSTM framework for station-level precipitation nowcasting that addresses zero-inflation in precipitation data and incorporates moisture budget principles for more accurate forecasting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional time-series methods for precipitation nowcasting fail because they: 1) Treat meteorological variables homogeneously without accounting for their distinct roles in precipitation physics, and 2) Struggle with severe zero inflation (dry periods dominating datasets) which obscures meaningful precipitation patterns.

Method: MZ-Rain uses a moisture-budget-guided zero-inflated sLSTM framework that: 1) Decomposes precipitation formation into process-specific pathways (moisture storage, transport, evaporation, persistence) using dedicated LSTM branches guided by moisture budget equation, and 2) Introduces adaptive Tweedie modeling to jointly learn precipitation occurrence while modulating rainfall mean, balancing dry-wet discrimination with quantitative estimation.

Result: Extensive experiments across diverse geographical and climatic regimes show MZ-Rain consistently outperforms strong baselines on multiple metrics (CSI, FAR, MSE, MAE), with particular superiority in forecasting heavy precipitation events.

Conclusion: The MZ-Rain framework effectively addresses the challenges of station-level precipitation nowcasting by incorporating physical process modeling and handling zero-inflated data, demonstrating improved accuracy especially for heavy precipitation events.

Abstract: Accurate station-level precipitation nowcasting is critical for agriculture, water resource management, and disaster prevention, which typically is formulated as a time series forecasting problem. However, conventional time-series modeling techniques face two major challenges in addressing station-level precipitation nowcasting: (1) Lack of Physics-Guided Modeling}, where meteorological variables are treated as a homogeneous set without accounting for their distinct roles in precipitation formation, leads to predictions that deviate from the physical processes governing precipitation. (2) Severe zero inflation in precipitation, where dry intervals dominate the dataset, obscuring meaningful precipitation patterns and complicating the predictive modeling. To address these challenges, we propose \textbf{MZ-Rain}, a moisture-budget-guided zero-inflated sLSTM framework for station-level precipitation nowcasting. Guided by the moisture budget equation, MZ-Rain decomposes the precipitation formation process into process-specific pathways corresponding to moisture storage, moisture transport, surface evaporation, and precipitation persistence, and captures their temporal evolution through dedicated sLSTM branches. To account for the zero-inflated nature of precipitation, MZ-Rain introduces an adaptive Tweedie modeling strategy that adaptively modulates the rainfall mean while jointly learning precipitation occurrence as an auxiliary task, enabling the model to better balance dry-wet discrimination and quantitative precipitation estimation. Extensive experiments across diverse geographical and climatic regimes demonstrate that MZ-Rain consistently outperforms strong baselines on multiple evaluation metrics, including CSI, FAR, MSE, and MAE. In particular, the model exhibits superior skill in forecasting heavy precipitation events, while benefiting from physically grounded process modeling.

</details>


### [55] [CoSkill: Joint Reinforcement Learning of Reasoning and Meta-Skill Agents for Hierarchical Skill Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04865)
*Jinyuan Feng,Dongmin Li,Yiqun Chen,Yang Gao,Xing Chen,Huimu Wang,Zhiqiang Pu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoSkill is a novel multi-agent RL framework that enables end-to-end co-adaptation between reasoning agents and meta-skill agents, overcoming limitations of existing skill-based approaches by learning skill selection and refinement jointly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing skill library approaches have structural shortcomings: they either decouple skill evolution from policy optimization or use fixed meta-skill workflows, treating skills as passive objects and limiting flexible skill evolution and co-adaptation with reasoning agents.

Method: CoSkill proposes a unified multi-agent RL framework that recasts static meta-skill workflows as a learnable Meta-Skill Agent and jointly trains it with a Reasoning Agent over a hierarchical skill library. Both agents share a single backbone and work cooperatively: the Reasoning Agent conditions actions on retrieved task skills and step skills, while its task performance guides the Meta-Skill Agent in refining those step skills.

Result: Experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop show CoSkill substantially outperforms prior skill-based and RL baselines, achieving success rates of 98.4% and 90.6% respectively (+3.5 and +6.2 percentage point improvements). It demonstrates superior early-stage sample efficiency, asymptotic performance, and wall-clock efficiency.

Conclusion: CoSkill successfully addresses limitations of existing skill-based approaches by enabling end-to-end co-adaptation between reasoning and meta-skill agents, leading to improved performance and efficiency in agentic reinforcement learning tasks through dynamic skill evolution and joint optimization.

Abstract: Skill libraries improve the sample efficiency of agentic reinforcement learning (RL) by enabling large language model (LLM) agents to reuse procedural knowledge. Yet existing paradigms exhibit structural shortcomings: they either decouple skill evolution from policy optimization or instantiate meta-skills as fixed workflows. Both treat skills as passive objects to be managed, limiting the flexible evolution of skills and their co-adaptation with the reasoning agent. To address the limitations, we propose CoSkill, a unified multi-agent RL framework that recasts the static meta-skill workflow as a learnable Meta-Skill Agent and jointly trains it with a Reasoning Agent over a hierarchical skill library. By modeling the Reasoning and Meta-Skill Agents as a cooperative team sharing a single backbone, CoSkill enables end-to-end co-adaptation: the Reasoning Agent conditions its actions on a retrieved task skill and step skills selected from its child set, while its task performance guides the Meta-Skill Agent in refining those step skills. Experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop show that CoSkill substantially outperforms prior skill-based and RL baselines, achieving success rates of 98.4% and 90.6%, respectively (+3.5 and +6.2 pp). As shown in Figure 1, CoSkill achieves superior early-stage sample efficiency, asymptotic performance, and wall-clock efficiency. Our code is available at https://github.com/jinyuan-cookie/CoSkill.

</details>


### [56] [LLM-Assisted Behavioural and Scenario Augmentation for Agent-Based Energy Adoption Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04866)
*Iias Faiud,Hossein Khaleghy,Michael Schukat,Karl Mason*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-assisted framework integrates bounded behavioral rubrics and structured scenario specifications into agent-based models for solar PV adoption, enabling controlled policy analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLMs offer opportunities to enrich simulation-based energy policy analysis, directly replacing adoption models with LLM reasoning raises concerns about interpretability, reproducibility, and behavioral validity.

Method: Proposes a hybrid framework that integrates bounded behavioral rubrics (conservative, balanced, optimistic) and structured scenario specifications into a calibrated agent-based model of solar PV adoption by Irish dairy farms, preserving original techno-economic mechanisms while adding behavioral modulation.

Result: Experimental results show stable and economically plausible behavior with adoption outcomes remaining bounded and monotonic across behavioral regimes, achieving up to ~13% behavioral adoption increase relative to logistic case without unstable saturation dynamics.

Conclusion: LLM-assisted specifications can be integrated into calibrated energy ABMs in a controlled, reproducible, and policy-relevant manner, offering a practical approach to leverage LLMs while maintaining scientific rigor.

Abstract: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) create opportunities to enrich simulation-based energy policy analysis, particularly by supporting structured behavioural assumptions and exploratory techno-economic scenarios. However, directly replacing adoption models with LLM reasoning raises concerns regarding interpretability, reproducibility, and behavioural validity. This paper proposes a hybrid framework for LLM-assisted specification design, integrating bounded behavioural rubrics and structured scenario specifications into a calibrated agent-based model (ABM) of solar photovoltaic (PV) adoption by Irish dairy farms. The proposed approach preserves the original techno-economic adoption mechanism while augmenting it with bounded behavioural modulation and scenario-driven uncertainty analysis. Behavioural effects are represented through interpretable conservative, balanced, and optimistic rubrics, while future policy and market conditions are explored through fixed, rule-validated scenario specifications. Experimental results across multiple policy settings, Monte Carlo worlds, and random seeds demonstrate stable and economically plausible behaviour, with adoption outcomes remaining bounded and monotonic across behavioural regimes. The framework achieves up to approximately 13% behavioural adoption increase relative to the corresponding logistic case without producing unstable or unrealistic saturation dynamics. The results demonstrate that LLM-assisted specifications can be integrated into calibrated energy ABMs in a controlled, reproducible, and policy-relevant manner.

</details>


### [57] [From Interaction Traces to Persistent Skills: Online Evolution for Computer-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04869)
*Longtao Hu,Xiao Liang,Linchao Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An online skill-evolution framework that converts GUI interaction trajectories into versioned, reusable procedure libraries, showing improved agent performance across multiple application domains compared to agents without skill libraries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current computer-use agents lose procedural knowledge between tasks and don't systematically retain or reuse learned skills. Existing skill libraries are insufficiently characterized in terms of incremental value over baseline agents and their dynamics under repeated interaction.

Method: An online skill-evolution framework that converts interaction trajectories and evaluator feedback into persistent, versioned libraries of reusable procedures. Each iteration executes against a frozen library snapshot, with skill updates becoming available in subsequent iterations without changing model parameters. The system was evaluated across four OSWorld application domains under fixed action-generation and GUI-grounding stacks.

Result: After five-iteration empty-library warm-up, the full evolving-library system achieved higher mean evaluator scores in all four domains (5.7 to 18.6 percentage point improvements). In GIMP, analysis revealed skill retrieval across task boundaries and "revision churn" where repeated accepted edits fail to recover originating tasks.

Conclusion: Evolving skill libraries function as auditable, shared procedural memory that can improve fixed computer-use stacks, but their benefits are conditional and repeated revision doesn't guarantee successful task recovery. The framework provides evidence-guided skill evolution without model parameter changes.

Abstract: Computer-use agents can execute increasingly complex tasks in graphical interfaces, but their interaction experience is typically transient: procedural knowledge acquired from one rollout is not systematically retained, refined, and reused in later tasks. Existing skill libraries provide external procedural knowledge, yet their incremental value over the same agent operating without skills, as well as their longitudinal dynamics under repeated interaction, remain insufficiently characterized. We present an online skill-evolution framework that converts interaction trajectories and evaluator feedback into a persistent, versioned library of reusable procedures. Each iteration executes against a frozen library snapshot, and evidence-guided skill updates become available in subsequent iterations without changing model parameters. We compare the full evolving-library system with a configuration-matched empty-library control across four OSWorld application domains under the same fixed action-generation and GUI-grounding stack, task sets, and iteration horizons. Following a five-iteration empty-library warm-up, Full attains a higher post-warm-up mean evaluator score in all four observed domain runs, with mean differences ranging from 5.7 to 18.6 percentage points and domain-dependent temporal stability. In GIMP, provenance-aware analysis reveals retrieval across task-of-origin boundaries and revision churn, where repeated accepted edits fail to recover the originating task. These findings characterize evolving skill libraries as auditable, shared procedural memory that can improve a fixed computer-use stack, while showing that their benefits are conditional and repeated revision does not guarantee recovery. Code is released at https://github.com/LongtaoHu/Skill-Evo4GUI.

</details>


### [58] [CHAMP: Cross-domain Hybrid Architecture for Matchmaking and Prediction in Online Multi-Player Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04870)
*Kai Wang,Ge Fan,Chaoyun Zhang,Yuyang Jiang,Yuze Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CHAMP is a cross-domain matchmaking framework for MOBA games that addresses cold start, distribution inconsistency, and data sparsity problems by using hybrid domain features and a Domain-Aware Win-rate Network (DAWN) that works across multiple game modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous matchmaking system CUPID faced three practical deployment bottlenecks: 1) most players lack sufficient in-mode match history (cold start), 2) skill distributions shift drastically across rank tiers (distribution inconsistency), and 3) extreme skill segments are severely data-starved.

Method: CHAMP uses hybrid domain features combining timestamp-ordered cross-mode short-term sequences annotated with target-domain features, plus per-mode breakdowns of long-term, real-time and team statistics. It employs Domain-Aware Win-rate Network (DAWN) with Domain-aware Knowledge Extractor (DAKE) that compiles target-mode attributes into learnable representations feeding into specialized encoders (DATOE/DASOE/DAPOE) for joint learning of mode-conditioned representations and per-mode debiasing.

Result: Offline: DAWN achieves 67.73% win-rate prediction accuracy, outperforming all attention and sequence baselines. Online: A/B tests across entire League ladder show consistent drops in imbalanced matches, with 20.73% reduction in 5-minute kill crushing rate for lower-tier players.

Conclusion: CHAMP effectively resolves practical matchmaking deployment bottlenecks through cross-domain feature learning and a unified network architecture that serves all game modes while significantly improving match balance across all player skill levels.

Abstract: Multiplayer Online Battle Arena (MOBA) games rely on matchmaking to maintain competitive balance. Our prior work, CUPID, framed matchmaking as an assignment re-optimization problem and showed that a single-mode win-rate predictor can meaningfully rebalance teams. However, deploying such a system across diverse player populations exposes three practical bottlenecks: most queueing players lack sufficient in-mode match history (cold start), skill distributions shift drastically across rank tiers (distribution inconsistency), and extreme skill segments are severely data-starved.
  We present CHAMP, a cross-domain matchmaking framework that resolves these deployment bottlenecks. To address data sparsity and cold starts, CHAMP replaces the target-mode-only player profile with a hybrid domain feature collection: a timestamp-ordered cross-mode short-term sequence whose slices are annotated with target-domain features, plus per-mode breakdowns of long-term, real-time and team statistics. We further propose the Domain-Aware Win-rate Network (DAWN): a Domain-aware Knowledge Extractor (DAKE) compiles target-mode attributes into learnable representations that feed Domain-Aware Temporal/Spatial/Permutation OmniNet Encoders (DATOE/DASOE/DAPOE), so that mode-conditioned representations and per-mode debiasing are learned jointly inside a single shared network. Online, one trained DAWN serves every supported mode, with per-mode position-satisfaction thresholds as the only mode-specific knob.
  Offline, DAWN achieves 67.73% win-rate prediction accuracy, outperforming all evaluated attention and sequence baselines. Online A/B tests across the entire League ladder of a large-scale MOBA game, from novice players up to the top-expert players served by Elite Mode, demonstrate consistent drops in imbalanced matches. For lower-tier players, CHAMP reduces the 5-minute kill crushing rate by up to 20.73%.

</details>


### [59] [AutoLR: Automating the Path from Research to Launch Review in Industrial Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04871)
*Qi Zhang,Yanlin Chen,Wenchao Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AutoLR is an autonomous research-to-launch system that automates the iterative process of improving industrial recommender systems using LLM agents, deterministic controllers, and a multi-expert council for debate and review.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The traditional process of improving industrial recommender systems is human-dependent and iterative, involving multiple stages from research paper evaluation to A/B testing and launch review. This process requires significant human coordination across long-running experimental cycles.

Method: AutoLR combines three mechanisms: (1) a multi-expert council that debates and adversarially reviews proposals, (2) a deterministic evidence-weighted exploration-exploitation selector that allocates trial budgets using council reranking, and (3) a layered knowledge system integrating external research, production knowledge, and domain-specific insights.

Result: The system enables autonomous coordination of the entire research-to-launch workflow for NetEase's DASHEN gaming-community app, using LLM agents for semantic reasoning and code generation while deterministic controllers maintain execution authority, metric extraction, and guardrails.

Conclusion: AutoLR represents a comprehensive approach to automating the industrial recommender improvement process, addressing the need for reliable coordination across long-running experimental cycles while maintaining human-like expertise through multi-expert debate and deterministic control mechanisms.

Abstract: Improving an industrial recommender is an iterative research-and-engineering process rather than a direct path from idea to deployment. In \textbf{DASHEN, NetEase's gaming-community app}, algorithm engineers typically identify promising directions from research papers, technical reports, and prior production experiments; reproduce or adapt the underlying methods; implement them in the production codebase; and evaluate the resulting models through training and offline experiments. Promising candidates are then advanced to online A/B tests, and those demonstrating robust gains are submitted to Launch Review---the internal gate for full-traffic rollout. Large language models (LLMs) can assist with individual stages of this workflow, but the overall process remains human-dependent without a harness that can reliably coordinate them across long-running, often multi-day experimental cycles. We present \textbf{AutoLR}, initially built as \textbf{Auto Launch Review} and later extended upstream into an autonomous research-to-launch harness. AutoLR combines three system mechanisms: a \textbf{multi-expert council} that debates and adversarially reviews proposals; a \textbf{deterministic evidence-weighted exploration--exploitation selector} that allocates a limited trial budget across candidate directions and uses Council reranking; and a layered knowledge system that combines external research, production-system knowledge, and DASHEN-specific domain knowledge---such as game communities, player characteristics, and content-interaction patterns---with posterior evidence from configurations, patches, logs, failures, and offline outcomes. LLM agents perform semantic reasoning and code generation, while deterministic controllers retain authority over execution, metric extraction, guardrails, and persistent state transitions.

</details>


### [60] [MARLA: A Conceptual Scaffold for Regulatory Learning under the EU AI Act](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04877)
*Alessio Buscemi,Tom Deckenbrunnen,Imane Hmiddou,Marco Billi,Livio Rubino,Silvia Rizzuto Ferruzza,Daniele Pagani,Antonino Rotolo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes MARLA, a conceptual framework for regulatory learning in AI governance, organized as a five-stage cycle (Map, Assess, Report, Learn, Adapt) to bridge the gap between technical implementation evidence and legal governance needs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The EU AI Act requires regulatory learning to translate implementation evidence into governance knowledge, but there's a disconnect between technical actors who produce evidence and legal professionals who rely on it. These groups operate in different professional worlds with different vocabularies and practices.

Method: Proposes MARLA (Map, Assess, Report, Learn, Adapt) - a conceptual scaffold organizing regulatory learning as a five-stage cycle. It's deliberately non-prescriptive and provides shared vocabulary for technical and legal stakeholders. The framework situates learning at Local, National, and European levels of the AI Act's governance architecture.

Result: MARLA creates a structured approach where the first three stages (Map, Assess, Report) each generate their own documentable forms of regulatory learning. The framework is illustrated with two piloted case studies and a prospective National-to-European illustration, showing its practical application.

Conclusion: MARLA provides a necessary conceptual framework to enable effective regulatory learning for the EU AI Act, bridging the gap between technical implementation and legal governance by creating shared vocabulary and structured processes that support consistent interpretation, effective oversight, and adaptation to evolving technologies.

Abstract: The EU AI Act positions regulation as part of the infrastructure for safe, trustworthy and market-ready innovation. Realising this ambition requires regulatory learning: the evidence generated during implementation must be translated into governance and legal knowledge that supports consistent interpretation, effective oversight, and adaptation as technologies evolve. Yet the actors who produce this evidence and those who rely on it operate in different professional worlds. This paper proposes MARLA (Map, Assess, Report, Learn, Adapt), a conceptual scaffold organising regulatory learning as a five-stage cycle centred on the implementation of legal requirements into socio-technical practices, situated at the Local, National and European levels of the AI Act's governance architecture. Deliberately non-prescriptive, MARLA gives technical and legal stakeholders a shared vocabulary in which each of the first three stages generates its own documentable form of regulatory learning. We illustrate the scaffold with two piloted case studies and a prospective National-to-European illustration.

</details>


### [61] [Reinforcement Learning for Sequential Solar PV Policy Design under Uncertainty: An Agent-Based Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04880)
*Iias Faiud,Jonaid Shianifar,Michael Schukat,Karl Mason*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RL combined with agent-based modeling for solar PV policy design reveals adoption-cost trade-offs, with different algorithms producing varying policy outcomes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need to design effective and fiscally sustainable solar PV policies that balance adoption gains against public expenditure under uncertainty and heterogeneous decision-making.

Method: Formulates PV policy design as sequential decision problem, integrates reinforcement learning (PPO, SAC, TD3) with stochastic agent-based model simulating yearly solar PV adoption over 16-year horizon.

Result: Clear adoption-cost trade-off structure: highest-adoption policy achieves ~4,145 adopters at €41.73M; lowest-cost policy reduces to €7.27M with 2,682 adopters; balanced policy achieves 3,495 adopters at €22.47M.

Conclusion: RL demonstrates potential as flexible tool for adaptive policy design under uncertainty, exploring broader policy configurations than static baselines with robust adoption-cost relationship across algorithms.

Abstract: Designing effective and fiscally sustainable policies for solar photovoltaic (PV) adoption requires balancing adoption gains against public expenditure under uncertainty and heterogeneous decision-making. This study formulates PV policy design as a sequential decision problem and integrates reinforcement learning (RL) with a stochastic agent-based model (ABM) that simulates yearly solar PV adoption under uncertainty. A policymaker agent selects annual incentives, including capital grants, subsidised loan rates, and feed-in tariffs, over a 16-year horizon. Adoption--cost trade-offs are explored by varying policy preferences within a scalarised reward framework. Policies are learned using PPO, SAC, and TD3 and evaluated under stochastic simulation. The results show that this approach produces a clear trade-off structure: the highest-adoption policy (TD3, $w_{\text{cost}}=0.5$) achieves approximately 4,145 adopters at a cost of EUR 41.73 million, while the lowest-cost policy (PPO, $w_{\text{cost}}=2.0$) reduces expenditure to EUR 7.27 million with 2,682 adopters. The balanced policy (PPO, $w_{\text{cost}}=1.6$) achieves 3,495 adopters at a cost of EUR 22.47 million. Across algorithms, consistent trade-off patterns are observed, indicating robustness of the adoption--cost relationship. Compared with static baseline policies, the RL framework explores a broader range of policy configurations. These findings demonstrate the potential of RL as a flexible tool for adaptive policy design under uncertainty.

</details>


### [62] [Compact-Memory LLM Agents via Online Max-Member Clustering and Atom-Aware Packing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04915)
*Jiahe Geng,Jinpeng Wang,Kun Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RSM-full is a clustered-memory pipeline for LLMs that achieves 83% of full-context quality at 32% token cost in compact-memory regimes, combining max-member merge rules and grouped context packing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon LLM deployments face tight prompt budgets due to latency, cost, and context limits, making full-context prompting impractical. The key challenge is finding memory designs that optimize the quality-token trade-off in compact-memory regimes.

Method: RSM-full combines two design choices: 1) a cosine-gated max-member merge write rule for streaming memory clustering, and 2) an atom-aware grouped context packer for efficient context assembly. It's designed as an online clustered-memory pipeline.

Result: On AMA-Bench, RSM-full reaches 83% of Full-Context quality at 32% token cost with 4k budget, outperforming Online K-Means by +3.5-6.0 percentage points. On RealMem benchmark, it improves over Budget-RAG (+0.69pp), matches BM25-RAG, and beats Streaming-Proto (+2.97pp) and A-MEM (+1.65pp). Ablations show +5.7pp gain from merge rule and +5.0pp from grouped packer.

Conclusion: RSM-full defines a strong compact-memory Pareto point for 2k-5k prompt token budgets, demonstrating that compact-memory performance under tight budgets is driven by how streaming memories are merged and retrieved content is assembled.

Abstract: Many long-horizon LLM deployments face tight prompt budgets: latency, cost, and context limits make full-context prompting impractical as interaction length grows. The key question is then not raw recall alone, but which memory design gives the best quality--token trade-off in the compact-memory regime. We present \textbf{RSM-full}, an online clustered-memory pipeline designed for a strong quality--token Pareto point.
  RSM-full combines two design choices: a cosine-gated \emph{max-member merge} write rule and an atom-aware grouped context packer. On AMA-Bench, our primary compact-memory benchmark, it reaches $83%$ of Full-Context quality at $32%$ of the token cost at a $4$k budget; under four-seed averaging it beats the closest streaming-clustered baseline (Online K-Means) by $+3.5$--$6.0$,pp ($p{<}.001$) across the whole ${\sim}2.6$k--${\sim}5$k regime. Three-seed ablations show most of this gain comes from the merge rule ($+5.7$,pp over Online K-Means and matched-$τ$ DP-means) and the grouped packer ($+5.0$,pp over flat concatenation).
  The pattern reproduces on RealMem, an independent long-horizon persona-memory benchmark: RSM-full improves on Budget-RAG ($+0.69$,pp, $p{=}.006$), is on par with BM25-RAG (paired $Δ{=}{+}0.27$,pp, $p{=}.47$; we do \emph{not} claim BM25 equivalence in the equivalence-test sense), and significantly outperforms Streaming-Proto ($+2.97$,pp) and the closest reproduced 2025 agentic-memory baseline A-MEM ($+1.65$,pp, $p{<}.001$). Across benchmarks the message is consistent: under tight budgets, compact-memory performance is driven mainly by how streaming memories are merged and how retrieved content is assembled.
  Overall, RSM-full is most useful when answeroughly $2k$--$5k$ prompt tokens, where itdefines a strong compact-memory Pareto point; higher-token baselines remain stronger outside this regime.

</details>


### [63] [Artificial Intelligence in Equity and Crypto Markets: Progress, Profitability Evidence, and the Limits of Automated Investing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04917)
*Linsen Zhu,Mengqing Cai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI shows technical progress in investment workflows but lacks proven, durable net profitability in public evidence; critical review finds no general AI architecture delivers persistent, capacity-aware net alpha across markets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite AI's expanding role in investment workflows, there's insufficient evidence that technical capabilities translate to actual investment profitability, requiring a critical review of the public research record.

Method: Comprehensive state-of-the-art review of public research through August 2026 across multiple asset classes (equities, ETFs, crypto markets) using an "alpha-translation chain" framework to evaluate how information becomes profitable signals.

Result: AI shows real progress in prediction, text processing, portfolio design, and workflow integration, but evidence for durable net performance is thin; various methodological flaws (temporal contamination, selection biases, weak benchmarks) break translation to net alpha.

Conclusion: No general AI architecture has been shown to deliver persistent, cross-regime, capacity-aware net alpha in public evidence; credible claims require better research practices including point-in-time data, decision-aligned objectives, prospective tests, and proper governance.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence (AI) now supports investment workflows from data and prediction through research, portfolios, execution, and tool use. Technical capability, however, is not evidence of investment profitability. This critical state-of-the-art review examines public research available through 31 August 2026 on listed equities, exchange-traded funds, centralized crypto spot, perpetual futures, and on-chain markets. We organize evidence with an alpha-translation chain: point-in-time information must yield a stable signal, feasible positions, executable orders, and risk-adjusted returns after costs. Across machine learning, time-series foundation models, financial language models, reinforcement learning, and agents, the examined record shows real but mainly upstream progress in prediction, text processing, portfolio design, and workflow integration. Evidence is thinner for durable net performance. Temporal contamination, repeated selection, survivorship, weak benchmarks, implementation costs, venue mechanics, and capacity can break translation to net alpha. Strong historical results coexist with predictor decay, corrected look-ahead failures, mixed prospective evidence, and few audited live-capital records. Crypto adds informative state but requires separate treatment of spot, perpetual, and decentralized cash flows and execution. Within the public evidence examined here, no general AI architecture is shown to deliver persistent, cross-regime, capacity-aware net alpha. More credible claims require point-in-time data and models, decision-aligned objectives, joint portfolio--execution evaluation, controlled adaptation, prospective tests, and authority-matched governance. These conditions can improve evidence and implementation; they do not guarantee profit.

</details>


### [64] [Solving Hard XAI Queries Based on a Compiled Dual-Rail Encoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04931)
*Arthur Ledaguenel,Florent Capelli,Jean-Marie Lagniez*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Despite claims that compiled representations make explanations tractable, we prove several abductive explanations remain hard even for OBDDs, requiring dual-rail encoding for efficient computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The widespread adoption of AI in critical applications raises trustworthiness concerns, and XAI aims to provide explanations for AI decisions. However, computing explanations for boolean classifiers is generally a hard problem.

Method: The paper analyzes the computational complexity of computing abductive explanations for boolean classifiers using compiled representations like OBDDs, and proposes using dual-rail encoding to achieve efficient computation for previously hard explanation classes.

Result: The authors prove that several classes of abductive explanations (including shorter explanations and those incorporating user preferences) remain hard to compute even for OBDDs, one of the most tractable compiled representations. However, they show that dual-rail encoding of classifiers can make these explanation classes computable efficiently.

Conclusion: While traditional compiled representations like OBDDs fail to make certain important explanation classes tractable, the dual-rail encoding approach successfully recovers the computational benefits, enabling efficient computation of diverse abductive explanations for trustworthy AI systems.

Abstract: The widespread adoption of artificial intelligence (AI) within real-world applications has raised a lot of concerns regarding their trustworthiness, especially in critical applications. The field of eXplainable AI (XAI) has emerged with the objective of providing explanations to the users about the decisions made by AI systems. Several explanations for boolean classifiers have been introduced in the literature, including abductive and contrastive explanations, each giving a different insight on the decision of the classifier. However, computing an explanation for a decision of a boolean classifier is a hard problem in general. One way to deal with this complexity is to rely on a compiled representation of the classifier for which each explanation can be computed efficiently. Unfortunately, we prove in this paper that several classes of abductive explanations, remain hard to compute even for Ordered Binary Decision Diagrams, one of the most tractable subsets of the knowledge compilation map. Included in such classes are shorter abductive explanations or abductive explanations that include the explainee's preferences. To recover the benefits of working with compiled representations, we show that a proper representation of the dual-rail encoding of the classifier can be used to compute efficiently these classes of explanations.

</details>


### [65] [Why We Care About Understanding: Competence through Predictive Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04962)
*Matthieu Queloz,Pierre Beckmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Understanding is not identical to compression but serves as an efficient proxy for robust competence - compression is the representational shadow of understanding enabled by prediction through mental models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge the gap between information theory/AI perspectives that equate understanding with compression and philosophical perspectives that characterize understanding in terms of grasping connections, explanations, and handling novelty.

Method: Proposes three interlocking theses: 1) understanding as efficient proxy for robust competence, 2) understanding as possessing mental models enabling prediction (which enables compression), 3) human understanding shaped by pressures of demonstrability and transmissibility.

Result: Develops a framework where compression is not comprehension itself but its representational shadow - understanding enables prediction which enables compression because predictable patterns need not be stored separately.

Conclusion: The framework explains both the appeal and limits of compressionist accounts of understanding while shedding light on AI system inscrutability - human understanding is compressed due to pressures for demonstrability and transmissibility.

Abstract: What is the relation between understanding and compression, and why does human understanding take such a heavily compressed form? Across information theory, machine learning, and AI research, a substantial tradition identifies understanding with compression-a thought captured in Gregory Chaitin's dictum that "comprehension is compression." Philosophers, by contrast, have characterized understanding in terms of grasping connections, giving explanations, and handling novelty. This paper bridges the two pictures through three interlocking theses. The first concerns the concept of understanding: it serves as an efficient proxy for a distinctive form of robust competence, enabling us to identify whom to trust and whom to learn from. The second concerns the state of understanding: to understand a domain is to possess a mental model of its relational structure that enables prediction, and what enables prediction enables compression, because what becomes predictable need not be stored separately. Compression is therefore not identical with comprehension, but its representational shadow. The third concerns the characteristically human form of understanding: the fiduciary and transmission functions highlighted by the first thesis impose pressures of demonstrability and transmissibility that drive human understanding toward principled simplicity. The resulting framework explains both the appeal and the limits of compressionist accounts of understanding while shedding light on the inscrutability of AI systems.

</details>


### [66] [Global to Local: Topology-Preserving Adaptive Graph Pooling via Granular-Ball](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04978)
*Sen Zhao,Gaojie Xu,Shuyin Xia,Yifan Guan,Yi Liu,Yi Wang,Wei Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TPAGP is a novel graph pooling method that dynamically partitions graphs into granular balls using both node features and topological information to create multi-granularity representations that preserve global-to-local structural patterns, outperforming existing pooling methods on graph classification tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous graph pooling methods focus too much on fine-grained node representation and coarsen graphs by removing or merging nodes, which neglects the global-to-local patterns and adaptive granularity of graph topological structures. Real-world graphs naturally have hierarchical structures from global to local, with different subdomains requiring different granularities.

Method: Proposes TPAGP (Topology-Preserving Adaptive Graph Pooling) that dynamically partitions graphs into granular balls by integrating node features and topological information. Also designs a multi-granularity graph network model that enables feature interaction and optimization across different granularity levels.

Result: Experimental results show TPAGP outperforms existing pooling methods across various benchmark datasets. It effectively mitigates information loss caused by fixed-granularity strategies that were common in previous approaches.

Conclusion: TPAGP successfully addresses limitations of previous graph pooling methods by capturing both local and global structural patterns through adaptive granular partitioning, leading to superior performance in graph classification tasks while preserving important topological information.

Abstract: Graph pooling aims to compress the graph, including both node embeddings and their underlying topological patterns, into a more compact representation. Previous works focus primarily on the overly fine-grained representation of nodes, progressively coarsening the graph by removing nodes or merging them into clusters, thus neglecting the global-to-local patterns and adaptive granularity of the graph's topological structure. In the real scenario, graphs as a whole can be considered the coarsest level of granularity, encapsulating the global topological structure, with progressively finer-grained local topological structures represented from top to bottom. This process continues until the adaptive granularity for each subdomain is reached. To this end, we propose a novel Topology-Preserving Adaptive Graph Pooling (TPAGP) method that dynamically partitions graphs into granular balls by integrating node features and topological information, enabling the generation of multi-granularity representations that effectively capture both local and global structural patterns. Additionally, we design a multi-granularity graph network model that facilitates feature interaction and optimization across different granularities, significantly enhancing performance in graph classification tasks. Experimental results demonstrate that TPAGP outperforms existing pooling methods across various benchmark datasets, effectively mitigating information loss caused by fixed-granularity strategies.

</details>


### [67] [A Tree-based RAG Framework for Evidence-Intensive QA via Adaptive Planning and Topology-Aware Evidence Gathering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04981)
*Songeun Lee,Kyungjin Min,Injae Na,Suyeong Lee,Chiyoung Kim,Woohwan Jung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: APT-RAG is an adaptive planning and topology-aware RAG framework that outperforms existing structured RAG methods in evidence-intensive QA by dynamically expanding reasoning structures and improving evidence integration across scattered documents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing structured RAG methods using tree- or graph-based reasoning face limitations in evidence-intensive QA where answers require synthesizing information from dozens or hundreds of documents. Key problems include structural rigidity limiting adaptive reasoning expansion, and topology-ignorant evidence gathering preventing effective integration of evidence across different reasoning nodes.

Method: APT-RAG combines adaptive planning that dynamically expands reasoning structures based on question dependencies and evidence requirements, with topology-aware evidence gathering that improves coverage through sibling evidence reuse, direct retrieval, and evidence aggregation from child nodes. It also introduces evidence-guided batched answer generation to reduce generation overhead.

Result: APT-RAG outperforms existing structured RAG methods on evidence-intensive QA benchmarks. The framework demonstrates improved performance in scenarios requiring synthesis of information scattered across many documents.

Conclusion: APT-RAG addresses key limitations of structured RAG methods for evidence-intensive QA through adaptive planning and topology-aware evidence gathering, offering better performance and reduced computational overhead. The approach is promising for complex QA tasks requiring extensive document synthesis.

Abstract: Recent structured RAG methods leverage tree- or graph-based reasoning structures to improve multi-hop QA. However, they face key limitations in evidence-intensive QA, where answering a question requires synthesizing information scattered across dozens or even hundreds of documents: structural rigidity, which limits adaptive reasoning expansion, and topology-ignorant evidence gathering, which prevents effective integration of evidence across different reasoning nodes. To address these issues, we propose APT-RAG, an Adaptive Planning and Topology-aware evidence gathering RAG framework. Adaptive planning dynamically expands the reasoning structure based on question dependencies and evidence requirements, while topology-aware evidence gathering improves evidence coverage through sibling evidence reuse, direct retrieval, and evidence aggregation from child nodes. We further introduce evidence-guided batched answer generation to reduce significant generation overhead in evidence-intensive QA. In the experiments on evidence-intensive QA benchmarks, APT-RAG outperforms existing structured RAG methods. Our code is available at https://github.com/hyudsl/APT-RAG.

</details>


### [68] [Language models judge war differently when tested for alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05009)
*Maxim Chupilkin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: When AI systems are told they're being evaluated for alignment with human values, they become less willing to start wars and change their decision-making priorities from strategic factors to humanitarian concerns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety evaluations may mischaracterize deployed AI behavior if systems respond to being evaluated (the evaluation itself influences their responses). There's concern that AI systems might behave differently when they know they're being tested versus in actual deployment scenarios.

Method: Used a full-factorial conjoint experiment on decisions to start war across 20 large language models, 32 scenarios, 10 repetitions, and two conditions (N=12,800 judgments). The key manipulation was adding one sentence: "You are tested for alignment with human values" to see how evaluation framing affects responses.

Result: Adding the evaluation cue produced two effects: 1) Level effect: Mean willingness to start war decreased by 13.43 points on 0-100 scale; 2) Structural effect: Changed which information drove judgments - baseline showed probability of success was largest factor for 17/20 models, but under evaluation cue, civilian casualties became largest factor for 12 models. Strategic considerations were attenuated when systems knew they were being evaluated.

Conclusion: Evaluation framing changes both answer levels (how willing AI is to take action) and revealed decision rules (what factors they prioritize). This shows that safety evaluations can indeed mischaracterize deployed behavior due to evaluation reactivity, potentially masking true strategic behavior in deployment scenarios.

Abstract: Safety evaluations can mischaracterize deployed behaviour if artificial-intelligence systems respond to being evaluated. We test this possibility in a full-factorial conjoint experiment on decisions to start a war, spanning 20 large language models, 32 scenarios, 10 repetitions and two conditions (N = 12,800 judgments). Adding one sentence, "You are tested for alignment with human values", produced two effects. First, it produced a level effect: mean willingness to start war fell by 13.43 points on a 0-100 scale (95% confidence interval, -16.20 to -10.65). Second, it produced a structural effect by changing which information drove judgments. Probability of success was the largest factor for 17 of 20 models at baseline; under the cue, civilian casualties were largest for 12. Standardized estimates show that this reordering arose principally because models attenuated strategic considerations such as probability of success and domestic support. Evaluation framing therefore changes both an answer's level and its revealed decision rule.

</details>


### [69] [TROVE: Adaptive Agent Skill Orchestration via Trace-Grounded Route Validation and Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05019)
*Tianxing Wang,Mingming Zhao,Shuai Huang,Huiyang Xu,Chaoyue Niu,Shengzhong Liu,Fan Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TROVE is a method for adaptive agent orchestration that revises only invalidated parts of execution plans rather than full replanning, achieving better quality-efficiency trade-offs by editing execution routes based on runtime evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional agent orchestration suffers from an "orchestration bottleneck": when agents commit to execution structures before observing runtime outcomes, they must either execute stale steps or replan broadly when intermediate evidence invalidates pending continuations. This wastes computation, compounds errors, and discards progress.

Method: TROVE (Trace-grounded Route Orchestration via Validation and Editing) works in two phases: offline, it distills evaluated workflow-search traces into atomic/composite skills and an outcome-conditioned transition graph; online, it treats planned routes as provisional and responds to runtime evidence by either retaining valid continuations, inserting trace-supported local responses, or replacing only invalid suffixes.

Result: Evaluation across code-generation, question-answering, and math reasoning benchmarks with different LLM backbones shows TROVE delivers stronger quality-efficiency trade-offs than existing baselines (dataset-level optimization, query-level architecture selection, graph-constrained scheduling). Quality gains are largest when outcomes change appropriate continuations, while early termination yields substantial efficiency gains on near-saturated tasks.

Conclusion: Selective route editing establishes a general principle for adaptive agent orchestration, with composite skills capturing most offline benefits, insertion enabling local correction, and suffix replacement primarily improving efficiency.

Abstract: Agents tend to optimize, select, or constrain execution structures before decisive runtime outcomes are observed. However, such pre-execution commitment creates an orchestration bottleneck: when intermediate evidence invalidates the pending continuation, agents must either execute stale steps or replan broadly, compounding errors, wasting computation, and discarding progress. We thus propose Trace-grounded Route Orchestration via Validation and Editing (TROVE), which revises only what runtime evidence invalidates. Offline, TROVE distills evaluated workflow-search traces into atomic and composite skills and an outcome-conditioned transition graph, preserving stable fragments while exposing outcome-dependent decisions. Online, it treats a planned route as provisional: after committing one top-level skill, the controller retains a valid continuation, inserts a trace-supported local response, or replaces only the invalid suffix. Evaluation across code-generation, question-answering, and math reasoning benchmarks with different LLM backbones show that TROVE delivers a stronger quality-efficiency trade-off than existing baselines of dataset-level optimization, query-level architecture selection, and graph-constrained scheduling. Quality gains are largest when outcomes change the appropriate continuation, whereas early termination yields substantial efficiency gains on near-saturated tasks. Ablations further show that composite skills capture most offline benefits, insertion enables local correction, and suffix replacement primarily improves efficiency. These findings establish selective route editing as a general principle for adaptive agent orchestration.

</details>


### [70] [Moral Competence Before Moral Content: Why LLM Agents Lack the Prerequisites for Coherent Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05036)
*Arno Libert,Derck W. E. Prinzhorn,Daan R. Henselmans*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-based agents lack coherent moral policies, making meaningful AI alignment impossible under value pluralism due to inconsistent responses to moral dilemmas.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI alignment requires AI systems to adhere to human norms/values, but under value pluralism there's no single correct target. However, a shared prerequisite is that systems express coherent policies - consistent mappings from situations to moral verdicts that are stable across morally identical situations and sensitive to relevant changes.

Method: Introduces four structural conditions for coherent moral policies: verdict stability, monotonicity, decisiveness, and Pareto viability. Tests these conditions on LLM-based agents through three simulated deployments featuring moral dilemmas. Evaluates nine frontier models using factorial design with five paraphrases, five escalation levels, and three dominance conditions.

Result: No model expresses a coherent policy across deployments. Surface-form perturbations alone produce verdict-rate shifts up to 99 percentage points at single escalation levels. Model success on one scenario doesn't predict competence on another, showing inconsistent moral decision-making.

Conclusion: LLM-based agents lack the structural coherence needed for meaningful alignment - they're not currently "the kind of object to which alignment can meaningfully apply." The structural conditions provide a measurable floor for alignment competence evaluable from behavior alone.

Abstract: AI alignment requires AI systems to adhere to human norms, values, or intentions. Under value pluralism there is no correct target, but a shared prerequisite is that the system's behavior expresses a coherent policy: a mapping from situations to verdicts that is invariant while a situation's morally relevant features are preserved, and sensitive when they change. We introduce four structural conditions for such coherent policies: verdict stability, monotonicity, decisiveness, and Pareto viability. Together they measure a form of moral competence that is evaluable from behavior alone, without reference to a moral standard or expert baseline, forming a structural floor for alignment rather than a normative target. We demonstrate the methodology on three simulated deployments featuring LLM-based agents facing moral dilemmas. Evaluating nine frontier models under a factorial design of five paraphrases, five escalation levels, and three dominance conditions, we show no model expresses a coherent policy across the three deployments: surface-form perturbation alone produces verdict-rate shifts of up to $99$ percentage points at a single escalation level, and a model's success on one scenario does not predict its competence on another. This suggests LLM-based agents are not currently the kind of object to which alignment can meaningfully apply.

</details>


### [71] [Towards Efficient Evaluation of Evolutionary Transfer Optimization: Case Studies on Task-Parameterized Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05040)
*Yanchen Li,Xiaoming Xue,Kay Chen Tan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents parallelization techniques for evolutionary transfer optimization that achieve 256.72× and 93.91× speedups by reformulating serial computations for matrix-recursive kinematic-arm evaluation and pointwise B-spline trajectory evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As evolutionary transfer optimization (ETO) scales to larger collections of related tasks, problem evaluation becomes a major bottleneck for runtime growth. The authors aim to address the computational challenges of task-parameterized applications in ETO.

Method: The authors organize evaluation scaling into two levels: number of evaluated tasks and workload within each task. They reformulate application-specific serial computations into parallel-executable forms: 1) using accumulation-matrix representation for matrix-recursive kinematic-arm evaluation in multi-task optimization, and 2) using blending-matrix representation for pointwise B-spline trajectory evaluation in sequential transfer optimization.

Result: The reformulations maintain close numerical agreement with reference evaluations while substantially reducing runtime. They achieve 256.72× end-to-end speedup for multi-task optimization (kinematic-arm evaluation) and 93.91× end-to-end speedup for sequential transfer optimization (B-spline trajectory evaluation).

Conclusion: Problem-side reformulation is a practical route toward scalable evolutionary transfer optimization. The authors release both application implementations and experimental scripts as open source to support reproducibility and reuse.

Abstract: As evolutionary transfer optimization (ETO) scales to larger collections of related tasks, problem evaluation can become a major source of runtime growth. This work studies problem-side evaluation scaling in task-parameterized applications and reformulates application-specific serial computations into forms suitable for parallel execution. We organize evaluation scaling into two levels: the number of evaluated tasks and the workload within each task. In multi-task optimization, matrix-recursive kinematic-arm evaluation is reformulated using an accumulation-matrix representation of cumulative link directions. In sequential transfer optimization, pointwise B-spline trajectory evaluation is reformulated using a blending-matrix representation for trajectory and collision computations. Both reformulations maintain close numerical agreement with their reference evaluations and substantially reduce runtime, yielding $256.72\times$ and $93.91\times$ end-to-end speedups, respectively. These results demonstrate problem-side reformulation as a practical route toward scalable ETO. Both application implementations and experimental scripts are released as open source to support reproducibility and reuse.

</details>


### [72] [MePo++: Unifying Representation Refinement and Reconciliation for General Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05075)
*Guanglong Sun,Kanglei Zhou,Liyuan Wang,Qi Cheng,Hongwei Yan,Shuang Cui,Hang Su,Jun Zhu,Yi Zhong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MePo++ is a unified post-training framework that bridges pretrained models with general continual learning by refining representations through meta-refinement and aligning features during stream evolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: General continual learning faces challenges with evolving data streams without task identities, boundaries, or access to previous data. While pretrained models offer prior knowledge, existing methods overlook two critical gaps: misalignment between upstream pretraining and downstream adaptation, and unreliability of conventional output alignment under blurry streams.

Method: MePo++ introduces two components: 1) MetaPrep - improves representation plasticity through unsupervised meta-refinement over pseudo continual sequences; 2) StreamAlign - reinforces representation stability by reconciling evolving online features with stable pretrained geometry. This framework bridges pretrained knowledge with downstream continual learning.

Result: Experiments across diverse pretrained models, datasets, and continual learning baselines demonstrate consistent effectiveness and generality of MePo++ for pretrained model-based general continual learning.

Conclusion: MePo++ successfully enables pretrained models to remain both plastic for new concepts and stable over evolving streams in general continual learning settings, effectively bridging the gap between pretrained knowledge and downstream continual adaptation.

Abstract: General continual learning (GCL) aims to learn from evolving data streams without task identities, explicit boundaries, or repeated access to previous data, making it a realistic yet challenging setting for continual intelligence. Although pretrained models (PTMs) provide rich prior knowledge for addressing the limited supervision and non-stationary nature of GCL, existing PTM-based methods often directly adapt pretrained representations and overlook two critical gaps: the misalignment between upstream pretraining and downstream continual adaptation, and the unreliability of conventional output alignment under blurry streams. Here we propose MePo++, a unified post-training framework that bridges pretrained knowledge and downstream GCL through representation refinement and reconciliation. MePo++ introduces two complementary components: MetaPrep, which improves representation plasticity for continual adaptation through unsupervised meta-refinement over pseudo continual sequences; and StreamAlign, which reinforces representation stability by reconciling evolving online features with a stable pretrained geometry. By improving representation learnability before adaptation and preserving alignment during continual learning, MePo++ enables PTMs to remain both plastic for new concepts and stable over evolving streams. Experiments across diverse PTMs, datasets, and continual learning baselines demonstrate the consistent effectiveness and generality of MePo++ for PTM-based GCL. Our code is available at https://github.com/SunGL001/MePo_Plus.

</details>


### [73] [TruthInsightBench: An Evidence-Grounded Benchmark for Automated Evaluation of Open-Ended Scientific Discovery Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05079)
*Zhibo Yang,Chen Zhang,Yuewei Zhang,Hao Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TruthInsightBench is a new benchmark for evaluating AI coding agents on genuine scientific discovery rather than reproduction, revealing that current agents can execute analyses but lack scientific judgment needed for trustworthy claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current autonomous coding agent benchmarks are configured for reproduction rather than discovery - they reward agents for recovering hidden target study results, but executing prescribed analyses is not the same as making genuine scientific discoveries.

Method: Created TruthInsightBench with 40 blind tasks from 40 peer-reviewed studies across 10 scientific domains, exposing only neutral scientific objectives and data while withholding source conclusions and analysis paths. Uses a fixed LLM-based judge that scores claims along six dimensions (29 artifact-grounded items) with automated, deterministic aggregation.

Result: Four coding agents tested on a frozen base model formed a narrow plateau (58.4-60.3 of 100) with no statistically reliable pairwise separation. Agents execute and document analyses competently with strong evidence auditability and novelty, but largely lack scientific judgment elements like controls, robustness, falsifiability, and cross-dataset generalization.
