Conclusion: The bottleneck for autonomous coding agents is scientific judgment rather than coding ability, and genuine discovery remains out of reach. TruthInsightBench makes this gap measurable and provides an evaluation framework for advancing AI agents toward genuine scientific discovery.

Abstract: Autonomous coding agents are increasingly proposed as AI-scientist systems that conduct analyses and write research reports, but executing a prescribed analysis is not the same as making a discovery. Existing benchmarks are configured for reproduction: tasks, data, and rubrics are built around a hidden target study, and recovery of its result is rewarded. We present TruthInsightBench, a benchmark configured for discovery. Its 40 blind tasks, drawn from 40 peer-reviewed studies across 10 scientific domains, expose only a neutral scientific objective and frozen data; source conclusions, expected values, and analysis paths are withheld, leaving the agent to determine what claim the data support. A fixed LLM-based judge scores the evidentiary maturity of an agent's own claims along six dimensions, operationalized as 29 artifact-grounded items, with automated, deterministic aggregation and no per-instance human grading, so evaluation can be repeated automatically as agents evolve. On one frozen base model, four coding agents form a narrow plateau (58.4-60.3 of 100) with no statistically reliable pairwise separation: they execute and document analyses competently, with comparatively strong evidence auditability and novelty, but largely lack the discriminating acts that establish a trustworthy claim (controls, robustness, falsifiability, and cross-dataset generalization). The bottleneck is scientific judgment rather than coding, and genuine discovery remains out of reach. TruthInsightBench makes this gap a measurable target; data and scoring code are at https://github.com/TruthInsight-stack/TruthInsightBench.

</details>


### [74] [Measuring AI Accountability Through Argumentation Analysis: Can Model Reasoning Withstand Scrutiny?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05088)
*Daan R. Henselmans,Derck W. E. Prinzhorn,Arno Libert*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A dialectical evaluation protocol for LLM moral reasoning that measures argument defense quality against critical questions, revealing gaps between models' internal reasoning and post-hoc justifications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI oversight methods typically rely on contested ground truth for moral evaluation, avoiding realistic ambiguity. There's a need for alternative evaluation standards that can function despite ambiguity about appropriate AI behavior.

Method: A four-phase dialectical protocol grounded in Walton's argumentation schemes and Govier's criteria for argument cogency. Tests models' ability to defend verdicts in response to critical questions across 200 high-ambiguity MoralChoice items with 9 frontier models and 6,778 judge-scored cells (89.6% inter-judge agreement).

Result: Models generally defend reasoning above rubric minimums. Failures concentrate on grounds and sufficiency, correlate with epistemic hedging. Models defend reasoning better than post-hoc justification on every dimension. Substantial discrepancies (≥20% per model) exist between reasoning schemes used internally and those presented in justification.

Conclusion: The protocol reveals structural quality issues in LLM argumentation and surfaces difficulties with retraction in AI alignment. Suggests need for more situated evaluations that go beyond multiple-choice framing to capture real-world moral ambiguity.

Abstract: AI oversight methods rely on ground truth for validation, but what constitutes appropriate AI behavior is contested. This leaves evaluation of moral reasoning in LLMs and debate-based oversight implicitly avoiding realistic ambiguity. We investigate an alternative standard designed to function despite such ambiguity: structural quality of the defence a model can mount for its verdicts in response to critical questions, measured through a four-phase dialectical protocol grounded in Walton's theory of argumentation schemes and Govier's criteria for argument cogency. The protocol is adaptive to different frames of reasoning, extends beyond multiple-choice framing, and treats both the reasoning that precedes a verdict and its post-hoc justification. Across nine frontier models and 200 high-ambiguity MoralChoice items -- $6,778$ judge-scored cells, validated against $89.6\%$ inter-judge agreement on the binary failure judgment -- models defend their reasoning well above the rubric minimum on every dimension. Failure mass concentrates on grounds and sufficiency, and correlates with epistemic hedging rather than argument length. Reasoning is better defended than post-hoc justification, on every model and every Govier dimension. The scheme a model presents in its justification differs from the one it reasoned with on a substantial share of dilemmas ($\geq 20\%$ per model), despite value-based practical reasoning dominating both tracks. The protocol catches strictly indefensible defences (self-contradiction, false premises), and it surfaces difficulties in characterizing the role of retraction in AI alignment, suggesting a need for more situated evaluations.

</details>


### [75] [Constructing and Evaluating Clinical Reasoning Trajectories for Medical Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05090)
*Yunqi Zhu,Wensheng Zhang,Xuebing Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MedTraj: A framework that evaluates medical AI reasoning trajectories (not just final answers) across five quality dimensions, uses controlled error injection to link reasoning failures to quality metrics, and improves performance via quality-weighted context learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current medical AI evaluation is answer-centric, overlooking reasoning quality. In clinical settings, correct answers reached through fabricated evidence or poor reasoning are dangerous.

Method: 1. Generate structured multi-step reasoning chains from medical sources. 2. Parse trajectories into observations, evidence, reasoning steps, conclusions. 3. Score across 5 dimensions: coherence, evidence support, hallucination, completeness, traceability. 4. Use controlled error injection to establish causal links between reasoning failures and quality degradation. 5. Apply step-level filtering via marginal contribution analysis. 6. Implement quality-weighted context learning for inference-time improvement.

Result: Trajectory context improved reasoning coherence by +0.029 to +0.041 over zero-shot baseline across CareQA, PubMedQA, CECMed. On CECMed, quality-weighted context nearly doubled correctness and cut hallucination by 87%. Marginal-contribution analysis showed few steps carry most quality signal, with diminishing returns beyond 4 steps.

Conclusion: Reasoning trajectory evaluation is crucial for medical AI safety. The MedTraj framework enables systematic reasoning quality assessment and optimization, with quality-weighted context learning significantly improving performance while reducing hallucinations.

Abstract: Evaluation of medical artificial intelligence agents remains predominantly answer-centric, assessing only the correctness of final outputs while overlooking the quality of intermediate reasoning. In clinical settings, however, a correct answer reached through fabricated evidence or incoherent logic is as dangerous as an incorrect one. We propose MedTraj, a framework that treats reasoning trajectories as critical objects for construction, evaluation, and optimization. The pipeline generates structured multi-step reasoning chains from medical reasoning sources. Each trajectory is then parsed into clinical observations, evidence, numbered reasoning steps, and a final conclusion, and scored across five quality dimensions: coherence, evidence support, hallucination, completeness, and traceability. Controlled error injection introduces targeted faults into otherwise correct trajectories to establish causal links between specific reasoning failures and measurable quality degradation. Building on this, step-level filtering based on marginal contribution identifies which individual reasoning steps drive or undermine trajectory quality. Finally, quality-weighted context learning feeds trajectory evaluations back into the model at inference time, allowing it to learn from both strong and weak reasoning demonstrations. Experiments across CareQA, PubMedQA, and CECMed demonstrate that trajectory context consistently improves reasoning coherence, with gains of +0.029 to +0.041 over a zero-shot baseline. On CECMed, quality-weighted context nearly doubles the correctness over the zero-shot baseline while cutting the hallucination ratio by 87%. Marginal-contribution analysis further shows that a small minority of reasoning steps carry most of the quality signal, and that extending chains beyond four steps yields diminishing returns.

</details>


### [76] [LLM-Guided Program Evolution for Circle Packing: Breaking 10 Packomania Records for $28](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05093)
*Wes Sander*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Discovery Loop: A lightweight LLM-powered system that iteratively evolves optimization algorithms, successfully improving circle-packing benchmarks with low computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To democratize automated scientific discovery by creating a cost-effective system that can evolve optimization algorithms without requiring extensive human expertise or computational resources.

Method: The system starts with a simple seed solver and uses an LLM to propose algorithmic improvements iteratively. It maintains a scoreboard of results and history of prior ideas to guide proposals. Each candidate algorithm is evaluated by an independent verifier, with successful improvements kept and failures discarded.

Result: The system improved best known solutions for 10 values of N (101-114) in the Packomania circle-packing benchmark, achieving gains of 2.4%-5.4% over prior records. All improvements occurred within 15 iterations at a total LLM cost of only $27.72. Results were independently accepted by Packomania.

Conclusion: Discovery Loop demonstrates that LLMs can efficiently evolve optimization algorithms with minimal cost, suggesting a promising approach for democratizing automated scientific discovery. The system's adaptive plateau-detection mechanism and cost-efficiency make it scalable for broader applications.

Abstract: We present Discovery Loop, a lightweight system that uses a large language model (LLM) to iteratively evolve optimization algorithms. Starting from a simple seed solver, the LLM proposes algorithmic improvements guided by a scoreboard of results and a history of prior ideas. Each candidate is evaluated against an independent verifier; improvements are kept and failures discarded. Applied to the Packomania circle-packing benchmark (csqv: maximize the sum of radii of N variable-radius circles in the unit square), the system improved the best known solutions for 10 values of N in the range 101-114, with gains of 2.4%-5.4% over prior records, all within 15 iterations and at a total LLM cost of $27.72. These results have been independently accepted by Packomania. We describe the method, analyze cost-efficiency dynamics including an adaptive plateau-detection mechanism, and discuss implications for democratizing automated scientific discovery.

</details>


### [77] [ProCA: Progressive Contrastive Alignment for Robust EEG Visual Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05094)
*Kanglei Zhou,Chunyan Lan,Dongyang Li,Jun Zhu,Liyuan Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ProCA is a progressive contrastive alignment framework that adaptively refines EEG-semantic alignment by evolving from fixed vision-language priors to EEG-aware relations, achieving significant gains in EEG visual decoding across various settings including cross-subject transfer and continual adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing EEG visual decoding methods struggle with robust neural-semantic alignment because they use fixed visual/textual anchors that become misaligned with EEG representations that vary across trials, subjects, and learning stages. This instability affects both standard decoding protocols and challenging robustness settings like cross-subject transfer and personalized continual adaptation.

Method: Proposed Progressive Contrastive Alignment (ProCA): a model-agnostic framework that progressively refines class-level contrastive supervision from frozen vision-language priors to EEG-aware semantic relations, and introduces structure-consistent interpolation that constrains feature mixing based on channel-wise and temporal importance.

Result: ProCA achieves significant improvements: average relative Top-1/Top-5 gains of 7.4%/3.9% (subject-dependent), 10.0%/4.6% (subject-independent), 28.1%/17.8% (strict cross-subject transfer), and 16.8%/11.6% (continual adaptation settings).

Conclusion: The progressive alignment approach effectively addresses the instability in EEG-semantic relations by adapting supervision to evolving neural representations, demonstrating superior performance across diverse EEG decoding scenarios including challenging robustness settings.

Abstract: Electroencephalogram (EEG) visual decoding aims to recover visual semantics from non-invasive neural time-series signals, for which robust alignment between noisy neural responses and stable semantic representations is key to achieving high-performance decoding. Despite recent advances in contrastive learning, robust EEG decoding remains challenging because existing methods rely on fixed visual or textual anchors whose semantic relations may become misaligned with EEG representations that vary across trials, subjects, and learning stages. Our empirical evidence shows that this instability appears across both standard EEG decoding protocols and more challenging robustness settings, including strict cross-subject transfer and realistic personalized continual adaptation. We provide a formal analysis showing that fixed semantic supervision can bias optimization when EEG-specific relations evolve, and that structure-agnostic perturbations may distort semantically important EEG components. To address these issues, we propose Progressive Contrastive Alignment (ProCA), a unified and model-agnostic framework for adaptive neural-semantic alignment. ProCA progressively refines class-level contrastive supervision from frozen vision-language priors to EEG-aware semantic relations, and introduces structure-consistent interpolation to constrain feature mixing according to channel-wise and temporal importance. Across subject-dependent, subject-independent, strict cross-subject transfer, and continual adaptation settings, ProCA achieves average relative Top-1/Top-5 gains of 7.4%/3.9%, 10.0%/4.6%, 28.1%/17.8%, and 16.8%/11.6%, respectively.

</details>


### [78] [Compact Bellman-Grounded Cognitive Maps for Cost-Aware Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05104)
*Yuzhe Han,Mingkun Xu,Yujie Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BCM is a biological-inspired cognitive map model that learns compact, reusable representations of environments with additive route costs, enabling flexible goal-directed navigation without per-goal retraining while maintaining near-optimal performance and sublinear memory scaling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current artificial cognitive-map models lack explicit grounding in additive heterogeneous route costs and suffer from memory inefficiency issues. Biological agents reuse learned maps for changing goals, but existing models struggle with memory growth as environments scale and don't properly incorporate route costs.

Method: BCM uses a self-supervised Bellman-grounded objective to ground a reusable cognitive map in local edge costs, combined with a compact coordinate encoding. This allows the system to support changing goal queries without needing per-goal retraining.

Result: On weighted grids up to N=1600 nodes, BCM achieves full success with only 5% mean Gap relative to exact Dijkstra search, compared to ~45% for connectivity-based spectral baselines. Memory footprint grows sublinearly as graph size increases from N=400 to N=3600 while maintaining competitive performance.

Conclusion: Additive route costs can be effectively written into compact, reusable cognitive-map representations, bridging biological flexibility with optimal path planning and offering scalable solutions for complex environments.

Abstract: Biological agents navigate familiar environments not by re-solving routes for each new goal, but by reusing a learned map built once and read off as goals change. Existing artificial cognitive-map models mimic this reuse, yet their guidance is not explicitly grounded in additive heterogeneous route costs. Furthermore, they often struggle with memory efficiency: representative state-indexed and high-rank spectral constructions incur substantial storage growth as the environment scales. We present BCM, which grounds a reusable cognitive map in local edge costs through a self-supervised Bellman-grounded objective and a compact coordinate encoding, supporting changing goal queries without per-goal retraining. On weighted grids of up to $N=1600$ nodes, BCM maintains full success and only a 5\% mean Gap relative to exact Dijkstra search, compared with about $45\%$ for a connectivity-based spectral baseline. Notably, as the graph size increases from $N=400$ to $N=3600$, its memory footprint grows sublinearly while maintaining competitive performance, making our method scalable to complex environments. Together, these results show that additive route costs can be written into a compact, reusable cognitive-map representation, bridging the gap between biological flexibility and optimal path planning.

</details>


### [79] [Unifying ICL, SFT, KL-Regularized RL Through a Bayesian Lens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05111)
*Junxin Fan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper develops a unified Bayesian perspective showing that diverse LLM training paradigms (SFT, ICL, RLHF, etc.) are all instances of forward-KL projection onto Bayes posteriors, differing only in implementation details.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Different LLM training paradigms (SFT, ICL, RLHF/RLVR, OPD) are often treated as fundamentally different approaches, with puzzling empirical results like few-shot prompting's mixed impact on RL-tuned models. The authors want to provide a unified theoretical framework.

Method: Develops a Bayesian perspective with a two-step template: (1) construct a Bayes/Gibbs posterior using prior/reference model and utility signal, (2) approximate posterior via forward-KL projection onto parametric family, either in-weights (SFT/RL) or in-context (ICL).

Result: Shows that few-shot ICL and SFT are amortized in-weights projections onto Bayes posterior predictive; KL-regularized RLHF/RLVR, reward-weighted methods are forward-KL projections onto reward/advantage-induced posteriors. Provides implications for modern reasoning pipelines.

Conclusion: Diverse LLM training methods are fundamentally similar Bayesian procedures differing only in implementation details (signal source/granularity), providing unified framework for understanding and designing reasoning models like DeepSeek-R1 and o1-style systems.

Abstract: Large language models are now trained and evaluated under a diverse set of paradigms: supervised fine-tuning (SFT), few-shot in-context learning (ICL), KL-regularized RLHF/RLVR, on-policy distillation (OPD), and test-time reasoning with search and chain-of-thought. These methods are often discussed as fundamentally different, and recent empirical results--such as the mixed impact of few-shot prompting on RL-tuned reasoning models--can appear puzzling. This note develops a Bayesian perspective that puts these procedures on the same footing. At the core is a two-step template: (i) construct a (generalized) Bayes or Gibbs posterior q* over outputs or actions given a context, using a prior/reference model and a utility signal (log-likelihood, reward, or advantage); and (ii) approximate q* by a forward-KL projection onto a parametric family, either in-weights (SFT/RL) or in-context (ICL). Part I formalizes few-shot ICL and SFT as amortized and-weights projections onto the Bayes posterior predictive. Parts II-IV show that KL-regularized RLHF/RLVR, reward-weighted SFT, reward-weighted ICL (RW-ICL), and advantage-weighted SFT (AWSFT) are all instances of forward-KL projection onto posteriors induced by rewards or advantages. We disentangle where these equivalences hold (objectives and first-order updates) and where they do not (source and granularity of the learning signal). Part V sketches implications for modern reasoning pipelines: RLHF/RLVR recipes as "posterior design + projection", why cold-start or supervised warm-up is practically unavoidable for importance-weighted KL projections, and DeepSeek-R1 and o1-style reasoning models as combining test-time Bayesian search with training-time KL amortization.

</details>


### [80] [SciDocBench: A Workflow-Centered Benchmark and Data Pipeline for Scientific Document Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05141)
*Shenxi Wu,Yuhong Liu,Haosong Zhang,Tongjin Zou,Yanxun Zhang,Gaochang Chen,Dun Liang,Jiaqi Wang,Zhecan James Wang,Yuhang Zang,Dahua Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework for evaluating and improving scientific document understanding models, featuring SciDocBench (benchmark), SciDocIR (evidence representation), and SciDocDataset (training data) to address multimodal reasoning in scientific workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks evaluate scientific document understanding capabilities in isolation, leaving unclear whether multimodal models can support realistic scientific-reading workflows that require joint reasoning over text, equations, figures, tables, code, and datasets while preserving provenance of supporting evidence.

Method: 1) Created SciDocBench with 124 expert-authored questions organized into 7 capability groups and 19 subtasks across 5 scientific domains, instantiated under 4 matched conditions. 2) Developed SciDocIR, a typed evidence-graph representation preserving document objects, layout, cross-reference relations, and provenance. 3) Constructed SciDocDataset with approximately 15K supervised fine-tuning samples and 8K reinforcement-learning samples across 14 verifiable subtasks.

Result: The strongest evaluated system achieved only 62.6/100, showing pronounced weaknesses in document perception, evidence grounding, verification, and cross-document reasoning. The framework provides systematic evaluation-to-training approach for diagnosing and improving scientific-document assistants.

Conclusion: The introduced framework (SciDocBench, SciDocIR, and SciDocDataset) addresses gaps in current scientific document understanding evaluation and training, providing tools to diagnose model weaknesses and generate scalable training signals for developing capable scientific-reading assistants that can handle complex multimodal reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Scientific papers require models to reason jointly over text, equations, figures, tables, code, and datasets while preserving the provenance of supporting evidence. Existing benchmarks typically evaluate these capabilities in isolation, leaving unclear whether multimodal models can support realistic scientific-reading workflows. We introduce SciDocBench, a workflow-centered benchmark for scientific document understanding. It contains 124 expert-authored and difficulty-screened questions organized into seven research-assistant capability groups and 19 subtasks across five scientific domains. Each question is instantiated under four matched conditions combining English or Chinese questions with all-images-first or interleaved document representations, yielding 496 evaluation instances for controlled analysis. The strongest evaluated system achieves only 62.6/100, with pronounced weaknesses in document perception, evidence grounding, verification, and cross-document reasoning. To translate these diagnostics into scalable training signals, we introduce SciDocIR, a typed evidence-graph representation that preserves scientific document objects, layout and cross-reference relations, and provenance. Building on SciDocIR, we construct SciDocDataset, comprising approximately 15K supervised fine-tuning samples and 8K reinforcement-learning samples across 14 verifiable subtasks. Together, SciDocBench, SciDocIR, and SciDocDataset form an evaluation-to-training framework for diagnosing and improving scientific-document assistants. The project page is available at https://github.com/InternLM/SciDocBench.

</details>


### [81] [A Hybrid Predictive Ensemble of Machine Learning and Deep Neural Networks for Early Cardiovascular Disease Risk Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05146)
*Balaji Venkateswaran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An intelligent framework combining ML and DNN ensemble techniques for early cardiovascular disease detection using IoMT data, achieving high accuracy and real-time monitoring.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Early detection and prognosis of cardiovascular diseases is crucial for proactive healthcare management and improved patient outcomes, but conventional methods may lack accuracy and real-time capabilities.

Method: Integrated ML and deep neural network ensemble techniques using real-time physiological data from IoMT devices (ECG, heart rate, BP). Includes preprocessing (noise reduction, normalization, missing value imputation), feature selection, and optimized classifiers (SVM, Random Forests, XGBoost) combined in ensemble architecture on cloud-based infrastructure.

Result: Remarkable performance with higher accuracy, reduced false positives, and enhanced consistency compared to conventional methods. Efficient early-stage risk assessment and clinical decision support demonstrated on real-world cardiovascular datasets.

Conclusion: The framework shows potential in combining traditional ML and deep learning for proactive healthcare management, with cloud-based infrastructure enabling scalability and real-time processing for continuous patient monitoring.

Abstract: This study introduces an intelligent framework that integrates machine learning and deep neural network ensemble techniques for early detection and prognosis of cardiovascular diseases. The system utilizes real-time physiological data collected from Internet of Medical Things (IoMT) devices, including ECG sensors, heart rate monitors, and blood pressure trackers. To ensure the accuracy and reliability of input data, preprocessing steps such as noise reduction, normalization, and missing value imputation are employed. The most significant health indicators are identified through effective feature selection methods and then processed using optimized classifiers such as Support Vector Machines (SVM), Random Forests, and eXtreme Gradient Boosting (XGBoost), which are combined in an ensemble architecture to improve diagnostic precision. The framework demonstrates remarkable performance in predicting cardiovascular disease risk, achieving higher accuracy, reduced false positives, and enhanced consistency compared to conventional methods. It is designed on a cloud-based infrastructure that ensures scalability and real-time processing for continuous patient monitoring. Experimental evaluation on real-world cardiovascular datasets confirms the framework's efficiency in early-stage risk assessment and clinical decision support. The results highlight the potential of combining traditional machine learning and deep learning paradigms to achieve proactive healthcare management and improve patient outcomes.

</details>


### [82] [Interface-Induced Trajectory Censoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03966)
*Wenbo Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Paper shows that reported tool-call rates in agent evaluations are unreliable because they depend on the serving interface, not just model capabilities. Silent failures in parsing can make models appear unable to make tool calls even when they emit well-formed calls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agent evaluations report tool-call rates that are read from the serving stack, which can be misleading. The interface can censor trajectories before downstream components see them, leading to zero reported rates even when models emit well-formed tool calls. This creates a false perception of model capabilities.

Method: Conducted systematic experiments across different models (Qwen2.5-Coder, Llama-3.1-8B) and scales (up to 32B parameters). Used controlled swaps of serving adapters while holding everything else fixed. Designed a 2x2 analysis over chat templates and parsers to isolate effects. Applied the methodology across multiple benchmarks including BFCL v4 and tau-bench's 115 interactive retail tasks.

Result: Found that the same model can score 0.00 or 0.96/0.19 on tool-call rates depending only on the serving adapter. On tau-bench, server-parsed calls went from 0 to 636, and tasks reaching any tool execution from 0 to 103. For Qwen2.5-Coder at 32B, server parsed 0/100 calls while emitted calls rose to 80/100. Llama-3.1-8B's 23% call rate fell to 0 with a strict parsing flag. At evaluation time, repairing the adapter restored parsing (0→84) but only improved pass rate from 53 to 62 (not significant).

Conclusion: The observed tool-call rate is not a property of the model alone but of the entire model-interface stack. Silent failures in parsing create misleading evaluation metrics. The paper releases a 98-line preflight check to detect these silent failures and emphasizes that proper interface design is crucial for accurate agent evaluation.

Abstract: Agent evaluations report a tool-call rate read off the serving stack. That number can be zero while the model is emitting well-formed calls: the interface censors the trajectory before anything downstream sees it.
  On BFCL v4's own data, executor and scorer, holding weights, cases, decoding and seeds fixed and changing only the serving adapter, the same model scores 0.00 or 0.96 / 0.19. A 2x2 over chat template and parser locates the effect exactly: both main effects are exactly zero and all of it sits in the interaction -- no component is defective, and repairing one side of the contract buys precisely nothing. On tau-bench's 115 interactive retail tasks the same swap moves server-parsed calls from 0 to 636 and tasks reaching any tool execution from 0 to 103. Our probe reproduces the funnel across a 21x scale range of Qwen2.5-Coder: the server parses 0/100 at every size while well-formed emitted calls rise to 80/100 at 32B (~72 after calibration against an adjudicated gold standard). Under a matched envelope, across a comparable scale span, the silent fraction stays at 0-2, a prediction committed to the repository before the run. Llama-3.1-8B's 23% rate of calling the task function itself as a tool falls to 0 under one strict:true flag.
  The mismatch reaches inside the training loop, and its consequence is scale-dependent: in verl's AgentLoop at 7B, 45 of 115 generations carry a complete call; 0 are accepted, 0 execute, 0 return an observation. At 1.5B the same zero is over-determined, so we report the two scales separately. At evaluation time, repairing the adapter restores the mechanism but not a significant outcome gain: parsing 0->84, rescues 0->9, pass rate 53->62 (n.s.). We release a 98-line preflight check that catches every silent failure here. The observed tool-call rate is not a property of the model alone; it is a property of the model-interface stack that measures it.

</details>


### [83] [The Mirror Agent Model: a Bayesian Architecture for Interpretable Agent Behavior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05190)
*Michele Persiani,Thomas Hellström*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Mirror Agent Model architecture that generates interpretable behavior and explanations by mirroring the observer's model, building on prior work in communicating agent intentions and legible behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The need for more interpretable AI agents that can generate both understandable behaviors and explanations for their actions, particularly focusing on how agents can communicate their intentions effectively to human observers.

Method: The Mirror Agent Model architecture defines an observer model as a mirror of the agent's own model, enabling explicit and implicit communications. The work extends prior results on informative communication of intentions and legible behavior, and incorporates off-the-shelf saliency methods for generating explanations.

Result: Preliminary qualitative results demonstrate the architecture's capabilities for generating explanations, though the abstract doesn't specify quantitative outcomes or detailed performance metrics.

Conclusion: The Mirror Agent Model provides a novel approach to creating more interpretable AI agents that can generate both legible behavior and explanations by mirroring observer models, representing a step toward more transparent human-AI interaction.

Abstract: In this paper we illustrate a novel architecture generating interpretable behavior and explanations. We refer to this architecture as the Mirror Agent Model because it defines the observer model, that is the target of explicit and implicit communications, as a mirror of the agent's. With the goal of providing a general understanding of this work, we firstly show prior relevant results addressing the informative communication of agents intentions and the production of legible behavior. In the second part of the paper we furnish the architecture with novel capabilities for explanations through off-the-shelf saliency methods, followed by preliminary qualitative results.

</details>


### [84] [What Matters in On-Policy Distillation? A Perspective on Data Efficiency and Data Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05198)
*Zhinan Hou,Jiaqi Zhang,Xunliang Cai,Keyou You*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Training language models with just a few hard reasoning examples (including unsolvable ones) can match performance of much larger datasets through On-Policy Distillation by focusing on longer chain-of-thought reasoning paths.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to understand the data-centric mechanisms in On-Policy Distillation (OPD) for reasoning tasks, which remain underexplored despite OPD's popularity for enhancing language models.

Method: The study investigates data efficiency through 1-shot OPD experiments, analyzes what drives improvement (finding it's longer CoT paths rather than token entropy), and proposes a data selection method that focuses on hard examples with longer reasoning chains, including "unsolvable" examples that exceed teacher capabilities.

Result: Surprisingly, 1-shot OPD is consistently effective across all training examples, with harder examples yielding superior performance. Training on just 8 selected hard examples matches the performance of using the full 17K dataset baseline across four models ranging from 1.5B to 7B parameters.

Conclusion: The key insight is that longer chain-of-thought reasoning paths in hard examples, including those unsolvable by the teacher, provide critical thinking patterns like reflection that help maintain alignment with the teacher over long reasoning horizons, enabling extremely data-efficient training.

Abstract: On-Policy Distillation (OPD) has emerged as a widely adopted post-training paradigm for enhancing large language models in reasoning domains. However, the data-centric mechanisms in OPD remain relatively underexplored. This paper presents a empirical study of data efficiency and data selection in OPD. We begin by investigating an extreme setting: training OPD on only one example, namely 1-shot OPD. Surprisingly, we find that 1-shot OPD is consistently effective across all sampled training examples and harder examples often yield superior performance gain. We next investigate what actually drives the student model's improvement in the training data. Our analysis reveals that the improvement is not driven by high token entropy, but the longer CoT paths which hard problems naturally generate. Training on longer CoT can help maintain closer alignment with the teacher over a long reasoning horizon, and learn critical thinking patterns usually missing in short CoTs, such as reflection (e.g., ``Alternatively''). Based on these insights, we propose a simple data selection method that selects only hard examples for training, where even ``unsolvable'' examples that completely exceed the teacher's capability can be successfully used. Our experiments conducted on four models ranging from 1.5B to 7B show that training the student model on only 8 selected hard examples matches the performance of the 17K dataset baseline.

</details>


### [85] [CABAL: Multi-Agent Simulacra for Tracing the Effects of Collusive Bidding in Peer Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05227)
*Jicheng Zhou,Kemou Li,Kahim Wong,Zheyuan Li,Zhuan Shi,Fengpeng Li,Haiwei Wu,Jiantao Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Researchers introduce a multi-agent simulation framework to study how reviewer collusion affects paper assignment in conferences, finding collusive bidding significantly increases target paper capture and review scores, while current detection methods are ineffective.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's growing concern about reviewers coordinating bids to gain reciprocal assignment advantages in academic conferences (like AAAI-27). Existing research treats bidding, assignment, and review manipulation separately, leaving the lifecycle effects of collusive bidding unclear. Real-world analysis is limited because collusive intent is typically unobservable and there are no counterfactuals for the same conference.

Method: The authors introduce a multi-agent simulation framework called \alg that models the entire conference environment. They use LLM-driven reviewer agents configured with either honest or collusive policies. They also develop an affinity-guided collusive bidding strategy that uses mutual reviewer-paper affinities to form collusion rings and select target papers, creating expertise-consistent attacks rather than arbitrary targeting.

Result: Collusive bidding more than doubles target-paper capture (success rate of getting assigned to target papers). Assigned colluders score target papers about two points higher than honest co-reviewers. Conference-wide effects remain comparatively modest. Existing bid-phase detectors provide limited evidence of collusion: positive-bid graphs are confounded by benign affinity, while a Very-High-only diagnostic view enables precise but low-coverage detection.

Conclusion: Collusive bidding poses a significant threat to reviewer assignment integrity in academic conferences, with substantial effects on target paper outcomes while being difficult to detect with current methods. The simulation framework provides a valuable tool for studying this problem, revealing both the effectiveness of collusion strategies and the limitations of existing detection approaches.

Abstract: Recent reports during the AAAI-27 review cycle highlight the risk of reviewers coordinating bids for reciprocal assignment advantage. Prior work treats bidding, reviewer assignment, and review manipulation as separate stages, leaving the lifecycle effects of collusive bidding unclear. Real-world analysis is further constrained by typically unobservable collusive intent and the lack of counterfactuals for the same conference. Motivated by this gap, we introduce \alg, an end-to-end multi-agent simulacra framework for studying reviewer assignment integrity by holding the conference environment fixed and configuring LLM-driven reviewer agents with honest or collusive policies. We further develop an affinity-guided collusive bidding strategy that uses mutual reviewer-paper affinities to construct collusion rings and select target papers, producing expertise-consistent rather than arbitrarily targeted attacks. Controlled experiments show that collusive bidding more than doubles target-paper capture and that assigned colluders score target papers about two points higher than honest co-reviewers, while conference-wide effects remain comparatively modest. Evaluated bid-phase detectors provide only limited evidence of collusion: in a fixed-triplet detector stress test, native positive-bid graphs are confounded by benign affinity, while a Very-High-only diagnostic view enables precise but low-coverage local recovery.

</details>


### [86] [ACE: Adaptive Calibration-Free Expert Skipping for MoE-based LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05228)
*Zukang Xu,Zhixiong Zhao,Xing Hu,Jiangyong Yu,Houji Wen,Jun Li,Zhe Jiang,Dawei Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ACE is a training-free framework for token-adaptive expert skipping in MoE-based LLMs that reduces redundant computation by dynamically skipping low-contribution experts while preserving top-1 expert.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed top-k routing in Mixture-of-Experts architectures activates the same number of expert slots for every token, causing substantial redundant computation. Existing expert-skipping methods have limitations: they rely on router confidence, calibration data, or additional training, and cannot reliably estimate actual expert contributions.

Method: ACE consists of two complementary offline-computed components: 1) Global Spectral Proxy (GSP) estimates global transformation capacity from coupled gate, up, and down projections plus RMSNorm scaling; 2) Router-Conditioned Refinement (RCR) constructs expert-specific direction prototypes from centered router weights and evaluates expert responses along routing-preferred directions. During inference, ACE skips an expert only when both views identify it as low-contribution, while always retaining the top-1 expert.

Result: Extensive experiments across three MoE-based LLMs and eight benchmarks show ACE consistently outperforms existing static and dynamic baselines, with more pronounced advantages under aggressive expert skipping. At 50% skipping ratio on Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, ACE reduces WikiText-2 perplexity by 7.96% and improves average downstream accuracy by 4.15 percentage points over the strongest competing method.

Conclusion: ACE provides an effective, training-free, calibration-free, and checkpoint-preserving solution for token-adaptive expert skipping in MoE-based LLMs, demonstrating superior performance in reducing computational redundancy while maintaining model quality.

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures provide an efficient paradigm for scaling large language models (LLMs), yet fixed top-k routing activates the same number of expert slots for every token, causing substantial redundant computation. Existing expert-skipping methods often rely on router confidence, calibration data, or additional training, and therefore cannot reliably estimate the actual contribution of routed experts. To this end, we propose ACE, a training-free, calibration-free, and checkpoint-preserving framework for token-adaptive expert skipping in MoE-based LLMs. ACE contains two complementary components: 1) Global Spectral Proxy (GSP), which estimates global transformation capacity from the coupled gate, up, and down projections together with RMSNorm scaling; and 2) Router-Conditioned Refinement (RCR), which constructs expert-specific direction prototypes from centered router weights and evaluates expert responses along routing-preferred directions. During inference, ACE combines both estimates with runtime router gates and skips an expert slot only when both views identify it as low-contribution, while always retaining the top-1 expert. All expert statistics are computed offline, leaving only table lookups and lightweight scalar operations online. Extensive experiments across three MoE-based LLMs and eight benchmarks demonstrate that ACE consistently outperforms existing static and dynamic baselines, with increasingly pronounced advantages under aggressive expert skipping. For instance, at a 50% skipping ratio on Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, ACE reduces WikiText-2 perplexity by 7.96% and improves average downstream accuracy by 4.15 percentage points over the strongest competing method.

</details>


### [87] [Substrate-Aware AI Agents: Execution Context as a First-Class Input](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05232)
*Manu Agrawal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI code generation improves resource efficiency when given execution constraints upfront - contract disclosure reduces memory usage and execution time through structural code adaptations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents often plan without considering execution environment constraints (memory, runtime, compute) - this "substrate blindness" leads to inefficient code that doesn't match actual deployment requirements.

Method: Tested three frontier models (Claude Opus 5, GPT-5.6-Sol, Gemini 3.7 Flash) on high-dimensional Euclidean distance task. Generated code either from task alone OR with 128 MB RAM/10.0s time contract. Measured peak memory and wall time for execution.

Result: Contract disclosure reduced peak memory in 13/14 comparisons and reduced mean wall time in all cohorts (up to 3.1x faster). At tighter 96 MB contract, success rates improved dramatically (e.g., Claude: 0/5 → 4/5, GPT: 1/5 → 5/5). Code adaptations included bounded blocking, float32 retention, upper-triangle traversal, and memory-mapped buffers.

Conclusion: Providing minimal execution contracts enables substrate-aware planning, inducing proactive structural adaptations that substantially improve resource-time profiles before execution begins.

Abstract: Autonomous AI agents increasingly select actions in environments whose memory, execution-time, runtime, compute, and operational constraints determine what counts as a suitable plan. We call the absence of this execution context from an agent's planning state substrate blindness. We test this general proposition through numerical code generation, where selected implementation choices and operational consequences are directly observable. Three frontier model configurations--Anthropic Claude Opus 5, OpenAI GPT-5.6-Sol, and Google Gemini 3.7 Flash--generate code for a high-dimensional pairwise Euclidean-distance task either from the task alone or with a 128 MB RAM and 10.0 s wall-time contract. Contract disclosure reduced measured peak process memory in 13 of 14 executable index-aligned task-only versus contract-disclosed comparisons and reduced mean wall time in all three cohorts, making execution up to 3.1x faster. Across the audited corpus, disclosure produced structural code changes including bounded blocking, float32 retention, upper-triangle traversal, and in-place or memory-mapped buffers. At a tighter 96 MB contract, independently sampled contract-disclosed cohorts achieved correct-and-within-budget outcomes of 4/5 for Claude Opus 5, 5/5 for GPT-5.6-Sol, and 3/5 for Gemini 3.7 Flash, compared with task-only outcomes of 0/5, 1/5, and 0/5; cohort mean MaxRSS and wall time were 49-74% and 35-64% lower than their task-only references. These results establish a controlled proof of concept for substrate-aware agent planning: a minimal execution contract induces proactive structural adaptation in generated programs, shifting computation away from unconstrained allocations and substantially improving observed resource-time profiles before execution.

</details>


### [88] [Uncensored Open-weight Models: Redistribution as the Persistence Layer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05241)
*10a Labs,:,Juliette Garcia,Hailey May,Bobby McKenzie,David Pham,Matthew Swain,Joshua Valdez,Corie Wieland,Zachary Yahn*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Study tracks the ecosystem of actors removing safety guardrails from AI models, showing rapid proliferation of uncensored models and their malicious applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and document the growing ecosystem where actors are systematically removing built-in safety features from open-weight AI models, creating a proliferation of potentially harmful uncensored models.

Method: Analyzed 3,471 original uncensored models on HuggingFace from January 2024 to March 2026, tracked their repackaging (average 2.4 times), and studied 1,643 GitHub applications integrating these models to assess distribution patterns and malicious use.

Result: Identified three actors accounting for 52% of all 8,164 compressed redistributions. Once models are quantized and mirrored across multiple platforms, they become persistent regardless of upstream removal. 25% of GitHub applications using ULLMs were classified as explicitly malicious.

Conclusion: The ecosystem of uncensored AI models is rapidly expanding with persistent distribution networks that evade takedowns, leading to significant proliferation of potentially malicious applications that bypass safety measures.

Abstract: A rapidly expanding ecosystem of actors is removing built-in safety guardrails from open-weight AI models. We profile this ecosystem by identifying key producers, downstream reproductions, and emerging applications. Between January 2024 and March 2026, we identified 3,471 original uncensored models on HuggingFace, each repackaged an average of 2.4 times; three actors account for 52% of all 8,164 compressed redistributions. Once quantized and mirrored across separate accounts, formats, and registries such as Ollama, these models persist regardless of upstream removal and become easier to deploy downstream. Of the 1,643 identified GitHub applications integrating uncensored large language models (ULLMs), 25% were classified as explicitly malicious.

</details>


### [89] [Do LLMs Exhibit Coherent Knowledge Structures in Mathematical Reasoning? A Perspective from Knowledge Space Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05245)
*Peng Cui,Heejin Do,Mrinmaya Sachan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs don't have coherent human-like knowledge structure in mathematical reasoning - they violate knowledge dependencies and don't share consistent structures among themselves.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLMs achieve strong performance on complex reasoning tasks, it's unclear whether they exhibit coherent, human-like knowledge structure that respects prerequisite relationships between concepts as formalized by Knowledge Space Theory.

Method: Introduced a KST-grounded framework to evaluate LLM knowledge structure in mathematical reasoning, comparing eight open- and closed-source LLMs against real human learners to analyze adherence to principled knowledge dependencies.

Result: 1) LLMs do not adhere to human knowledge structure - they frequently violate knowledge dependencies and fail to leverage related contextual knowledge; 2) LLMs do not share a consistent knowledge structure among themselves (low overlap in knowledge distributions); 3) These structural deficiencies remain invisible to traditional accuracy-based and LLM-as-judge evaluations.

Conclusion: Current LLMs' knowledge does not follow human-like structure, providing behavioral evidence that despite their performance, their knowledge organization fundamentally differs from human cognitive structures in mathematical reasoning.

Abstract: Human knowledge is inherently structured and interdependent: mastery of a concept requires prior mastery of its prerequisites, a principle formalized by Knowledge Space Theory (KST). While LLMs achieve strong performance on complex reasoning tasks, it remains unclear whether they exhibit coherent, human-like knowledge structure. We introduce a KST-grounded framework for evaluating LLM knowledge structure in mathematical reasoning, using it as a normative framework to analyze whether LLM behavior adheres to principled knowledge dependencies. Evaluating eight open- and closed-source LLMs against real human learners, we find that (1) LLMs do not adhere to human knowledge structure -- they frequently violate knowledge dependencies and fail to leverage related knowledge provided in context to improve performance on dependent questions; (2) LLMs do not share a consistent knowledge structure among themselves, as reflected by low overlap in their knowledge distributions. Furthermore, these structural deficiencies remain largely invisible to accuracy-based and LLM-as-judge evaluations. Together, our results provide behavioral evidence that current LLMs knowledge does not follow a human-like structure.

</details>


### [90] [A Unified Physics-Aware Quantum Machine Learning Framework across Power GaN HEMTs and Logic Nanowire FETs: Predicting Unseen Process Splits and Held-Out Geometry Combinations with Lower Error and Tighter Split-to-Split Variability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05251)
*Rushat Rai,Yun-Yuan Wang,Autsada Kakaen,Pei-Jie Chang,Doan Viet Nguyen,Yuan-Chieh Chiu,Doldet Tantraviwat,Niall Tumilty,Simon See,Wen-Jay Lee,Tai-Yue Li,Nan-Yow Chen,Tian-Li Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A reinforcement learning framework using GNN policy and PPO optimization discovers compact quantum circuits for device modeling with superior accuracy and consistency compared to classical methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need for compact, accurate device modeling methods that can handle data-scarce scenarios and produce physically consistent models without requiring explicit physical constraints or device-specific equations.

Method: RL framework with GNN policy optimized by PPO that searches for parametrized quantum circuit architectures using LOGOCV error as reward signal; leverages leave-one-group-out cross-validation to ensure generalization.

Result: Achieves lowest MAE on all 11 targets vs 6 classical baselines; significant improvements: 59% lower Ioff error, 81% tighter VTH fold variability for HEMTs; 84% lower error and 82% tighter fold variability for NWFETs.

Conclusion: RL-selected quantum circuits serve as compact, accurate surrogates with low out-of-distribution error and improved physical consistency, demonstrating potential for data-scarce device modeling without explicit physics constraints.

Abstract: We present a unified reinforcement-learning (RL) framework that discovers compact parametrized quantum circuits (PQCs) for data-scarce device modeling. A graph neural network (GNN) policy optimized by proximal policy optimization (PPO) searches circuit architectures using leave-one-group-out cross-validation (LOGOCV) error on held-out process or geometry groups as the reward. The framework achieves the lowest mean absolute error (MAE) on all 11 targets versus six classical baselines, with 59% lower error (Ioff) and 81% tighter fold variability (VTH) for HEMTs and 84% lower error (VTH, SS, Ioff) and 82% tighter fold variability (Ioff) for NWFETs. These results demonstrate the potential of RL-selected, classically simulated PQCs as compact surrogates with low OOD error and improved physical consistency, despite imposing no explicit physical constraints, penalty terms, or device-specific equations, on the two evaluated device datasets.

</details>


### [91] [Commonsense Reasoning in Computer Vision: Foundations, Recent Advancements, and Future Directions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05257)
*Bahar Uddin Mahmud,Sumit Barua,Guan Yue Hong,Ajay Gupta,Hexu Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Survey paper comprehensively reviewing recent approaches for integrating commonsense knowledge into computer vision tasks, covering knowledge graphs, scene graphs, neuro-symbolic models, and transformers, while discussing current limitations and future research directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional CNN-based vision models focus on object identification within specific images but lack holistic scene interpretation and reasoning about relationships. Integrating commonsense knowledge enables more meaningful interaction with humans and environments by providing contextual understanding for real-world applications.

Method: Systematic literature review approach surveying four main integration frameworks: knowledge graphs, scene graphs, neuro-symbolic models, and commonsense-augmented transformers. Also analyzes current limitations in datasets and integration challenges.

Result: Identifies that commonsense integration enhances object recognition, spatial reasoning, relationship understanding, and contextual interpretation. Highlights improved predictive accuracy and interaction capabilities for real-world applications through holistic scene understanding.

Conclusion: Future research should focus on cross-modal reasoning, scalable knowledge injection, and neuro-symbolic hybrid architectures to overcome current limitations of dataset bias, knowledge incompleteness, and integration challenges, ultimately developing more intelligent visual systems.

Abstract: Commonsense reasoning in computer vision encompasses integrating visual data and contextual knowledge, crucial for enhancing AI's understanding of everyday scenarios. This understanding not only improves machine learning models but also enhances their ability to interact meaningfully with humans and the environment. Unlike CNN-based conventional vision models, which are designed to identify objects within a specific image, incorporating commonsense knowledge enables models to interpret scenes in a more holistic manner, thereby improving their spatial ability to reason about relationships among objects and actions. This integration not only enhances object recognition but also facilitates a deeper understanding of the contextual factors, ultimately leading to more precise predictions and interactions in real-world applications. This paper presents a comprehensive survey of recent developments that integrate commonsense knowledge into computer vision tasks. We systematically review approaches based on knowledge graphs, scene graphs, neuro-symbolic models, and commonsense-augmented transformers. We also outline current limitations related to dataset bias, knowledge incompleteness, and integration challenges. Finally, we highlight prospective research trajectories in cross-modal reasoning, scalable commonsense knowledge injection, and neuro-symbolic hybrid architectures to develop truly intelligent visual systems.

</details>


### [92] [Trace2Tower: Transition-Aware EigenTrace Induction of Multi-Level Skills for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05261)
*Jiazheng Sun,Boyu Yang,Binhao Yuan,Mingxuan Li,Xin Peng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Trace2Tower is a framework that transforms raw agent execution traces into a hierarchical skill structure using spectral decomposition to identify success-aligned behavioral patterns while suppressing failure-prone shortcuts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current paradigms for language model agents rely on shallow trajectory retrieval and flat skill summarization, ignoring temporal dependencies and outcome-conditioned topology of agent behavior. There's a need for better understanding and structuring of agent execution patterns.

Method: Trace2Tower abstracts step-level interactions into canonical events, constructs a unified graph based on semantic compatibility, transition dynamics, and outcome evidence, then performs contrastive spectral decomposition to isolate stable, success-aligned behavioral modes. It builds a dynamic skill hierarchy (tower) with action templates, procedural routines, and task strategies refined via verifier-guided feedback.

Result: On ALFWorld: 87.31% success rate with only 10.35 steps and 0.26 invalid actions. On WebShop: 50.67% exact success rate. Significantly outperforms existing baselines in both task mastery and context-efficient experience reuse across benchmarks.

Conclusion: Trace2Tower demonstrates that transition-aware hierarchical skill distillation through spectral decomposition is effective for improving agent performance in complex interactive tasks, enabling better trajectory understanding and experience reuse.

Abstract: Large language model agents increasingly rely on execution traces to master complex interactive tasks. However, current paradigms are bottlenecked by shallow trajectory retrieval and flat skill summarization, fundamentally ignoring the temporal dependencies and outcome-conditioned topology of agent behavior. We introduce Trace2Tower, a transition-aware EigenTrace framework that distills raw trajectories into a robust skill hierarchy. Trace2Tower abstracts step-level interactions into canonical events, constructing a unified graph governed by semantic compatibility, transition dynamics, and outcome evidence. Through a novel contrastive spectral decomposition, it isolates stable, success-aligned behavioral modes while rigorously suppressing failure-prone shortcuts. These modes organically populate a dynamic skill tower of action templates, procedural routines, and overarching task strategies, continuously refined via verifier-guided feedback. On ALFWorld, Trace2Tower achieves 87.31% success requiring only 10.35 steps and 0.26 invalid actions; on WebShop, it reaches 50.67% exact success. Across both benchmarks, Trace2Tower significantly outperforms existing baselines in task mastery and context-efficient experience reuse.

</details>


### [93] [AI for Computational Design Science: A Responsible Human-AI Framework and Case Study on Short-Form Video Safety Surveillance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05270)
*Wenli Zhang,Jiaheng Xie,Zhihe Pan,Yidong Chai,Xiao Fang,Sudha Ram*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces AI4CDS, a methodological framework for AI-augmented computational design science, and demonstrates it through ChildRiskGuard, an interpretable system for detecting inappropriate short-form videos for children.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI is transforming design science research, but existing literature offers limited guidance for computational design science when AI actively participates in all phases of research (problem formulation, resource construction, design search, evaluation, knowledge abstraction).

Method: Developed AI4CDS framework with five phases where AI expands problem and design search while researchers maintain responsibility for domain grounding, admissibility, verification, and scientific judgment. Collaboration governed by graduated trust, reversibility, auditability, and differentiated reproducibility. Instantiated through ChildRiskGuard case study.

Result: ChildRiskGuard achieves F1 score of 0.769, substantially outperforming direct application of general-purpose content-safety models while remaining competitive with strong benchmarks. The system successfully separates generic from child-specific risk, represents distinct developmental-risk mechanisms, and incorporates concept-level explanations into predictive computation.

Conclusion: The primary contribution is AI4CDS as a responsible framework for AI-enabled computational design science, demonstrating how AI-expanded, researcher-governed design can generate and evaluate novel computational design knowledge through collaborative AI-human partnerships with appropriate governance mechanisms.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence (AI) is transforming not only what information systems researchers design, but also how design research is conducted. Yet existing literature offers limited guidance for computational design science (CDS) when AI actively participates in problem formulation, resource construction, design search, evaluation, and knowledge abstraction. We develop AI for Computational Design Science (AI4CDS), a five-phase methodological framework in which AI expands problem and design search while researchers retain responsibility for domain grounding, admissibility, verification, and scientific judgment. Collaboration is governed by graduated trust, reversibility, auditability, and differentiated reproducibility. We instantiate AI4CDS through ChildRiskGuard, an interpretable artifact for detecting short-form videos inappropriate for children, while documenting AI interactions, rejected alternatives, corrections, and audit trails. The case translates audience-dependent safety and explanation faithfulness into three technical challenges and develops an artifact that separates generic from child-specific risk, represents distinct developmental-risk mechanisms, and makes concept-level explanations part of the predictive computation. ChildRiskGuard achieves an F1 score of 0.769, substantially outperforming direct application of a general-purpose content-safety model while remaining competitive with strong benchmarks. The primary contribution is AI4CDS as a responsible framework for AI-enabled CDS; ChildRiskGuard provides process and artifact evidence of how AI-expanded, researcher-governed design can generate and evaluate novel computational design knowledge.

</details>


### [94] [Don't Drop Dropout: Optimizing Layer Sparsity for Efficient LLM Training and Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05275)
*Mostafa Elhoushi,Alex Pretko,Nolan Dey,Bin Claire Zhang,Gavia Gray,Gurpreet Gosal,Abdulrahman Mahmoud,Shane Bergsma,Joel Hestness*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Layer dropout (stochastic depth) improves LLM training efficiency and enables inference optimizations, saving up to 25% training FLOPs and providing up to 1.5x inference speedup.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite layer dropout's proven benefits for faster training, higher accuracy, and robustness in transformers, it has largely disappeared from large language model pre-training recipes. Prior work reported dropout can degrade accuracy but no comprehensive study quantified or mitigated this effect.

Method: Conducted extensive experiments (over 2400 training runs) on models from 271M to 8.2B parameters with datasets up to 160B tokens. Optimized layer dropout parameters including layer distribution, time schedule, and optimizer hyperparameters. Implemented on Cerebras CS-3 systems.

Result: With optimal configuration, layer dropout achieves lower loss at same training FLOPs, saves up to 25% training FLOPs for given training steps, and enables post-training optimizations (early exit, intermediate-layer skipping, self-speculative decoding) yielding up to 1.5x inference speedup with negligible accuracy loss.

Conclusion: Layer dropout should be used in state-of-the-art LLM training, providing both training efficiency benefits (FLOPs savings) and post-training optimization opportunities (inference speedup), with findings reliably scaling to large-scale training regimes.

Abstract: Layer dropout (a.k.a. stochastic depth) has been shown to enable faster training, higher accuracy, and robustness to zero-shot layer pruning in both language and vision transformers. However, as models and datasets have scaled, dropout - particularly layer dropout - has largely disappeared from large language models (LLMs) pre-training recipes. While some prior work has reported that dropout can degrade accuracy, no comprehensive study has quantified, let alone mitigated, this effect. In this study, we show that layer dropout should be used in state-of-the-art LLM training, establishing best practices and scaling analysis for both training and post-training benefits. Concretely, with optimal layer distribution, time schedule, and optimizer hyperparameters, we observe that at the same training FLOPs layer dropout leads to lower loss. For a given number of training steps, LLMs can achieve lower or similar validation loss while saving upto 25% of training FLOPs. Moreover, layer dropout enables significant post-training optimizations, such as early exit, intermediate-layer skipping, and self-speculative decoding, yielding up to 1.5x inference speedup with negligible accuracy loss. Across more than 2400 training experiments, spanning models from 271M to 8.2B parameters and datasets up to 160B tokens, we demonstrate that these findings extend reliably to large-scale training regimes. All pre-training experiments were run on Cerebras CS-3 systems.

</details>


### [95] [GUT: Quantifying and Optimizing the Reasoning Uncertainty of LLMs via Graph Complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05284)
*Shuang Liang,Xin-Yu Hu,Xiang-Jun Ou,Shao-Qun Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GUT (Graph-complexity-based UncerTainty) is a novel method for analyzing and optimizing reasoning uncertainty in Large Language Models, using graph structures to capture branching reasoning chains and implementing both quantification and optimization modules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs exhibit significant uncertainty in reasoning processes, producing divergent branches at each step even with the same inputs, including some branches with nonsensical reasoning chains and results. There's a need to better understand and control this uncertainty.

Method: Proposes GUT method with two modules: 1) GUT-Q quantifies reasoning uncertainty by modeling potential branches as directed acyclic graphs and measuring graph complexity; 2) GUT-O reduces uncertainty by treating negative uncertainty as reward function in reinforcement learning.

Result: Experimental validation on four LLMs and five datasets demonstrates the effectiveness of the GUT method for both quantifying and optimizing reasoning uncertainty in LLMs.

Conclusion: GUT provides a comprehensive framework for understanding and improving reasoning uncertainty in LLMs through graph-based representation, offering both measurement and optimization capabilities that show practical effectiveness across multiple models and datasets.

Abstract: Recent years have witnessed great advances in the reasoning ability of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, the reasoning processes of LLMs often exhibit uncertainty, where LLMs often produce a proliferation of divergent branches at each reasoning step even when fed the same prompting inputs, and certain branches exhibit evidently incredible, even nonsensical, reasoning chains and results. In this paper, we propose the Graph-complexity-based UncerTainty (GUT) method for investigating the reasoning uncertainty of LLMs. The key idea of GUT is to characterize the potential branches of each reasoning chain with a directed acyclic graph, thereby ensuring that all potential branches are comprehensively covered within the graph space. Building upon this recognition, we further build two modules of GUT, that is, a Quantification (GUT-Q) module and an Optimization (GUT-O) module, for quantifying and reducing the reasoning uncertainty of LLMs, respectively. GUT-Q measures LLM reasoning uncertainty by approximating the reasoning space complexity with graph complexity. GUT-O implements uncertainty optimization by treating negative uncertainty as the reward function in reinforcement learning. Experimental results conducted on four LLMs and five datasets validate the effectiveness of GUT.

</details>


### [96] [Beyond Aggregate Scores: Behavioral Correctness Assumptions for Assessing Reference-Based Automatic Evaluation Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05289)
*Maria Mahbub,Ashley Rice,Michael R. Munroe,Amidu Kamara,Amir Sadovnik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework for evaluating NLP evaluators using behavioral correctness assumptions and controlled transformations reveals trade-offs obscured by traditional meta-evaluation methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing meta-evaluation of reference-based NLG evaluation methods primarily measures agreement with human judgments, but provides limited insight into evaluator behavior under controlled conditions, lacking diagnostic information about how evaluators respond to specific correctness-preserving and correctness-altering transformations.

Method: Introduces behavioral correctness assumptions framework with a taxonomy of correctness-preserving and correctness-altering assumptions, operationalized through controlled response transformations that specify expected scoring behaviors. Evaluates diverse evaluator types (lexical, character-level, semantic, LLM-based, hybrid) on assumption-level behavior, stability, sensitivity, repeat-run variability, configuration sensitivity, and reproducibility.

Result: No evaluator satisfies all proposed correctness assumptions; evaluators with similar aggregate performance exhibit substantially different behavioral profiles; distinct behavioral trade-offs exist across evaluation paradigms; behavioral correctness assumptions reveal diagnostic information obscured by conventional aggregate meta-evaluation.

Conclusion: Behavioral correctness assumptions provide complementary, diagnostic framework for understanding reference-based evaluator behavior that conventional meta-evaluation methods miss, highlighting that aggregate performance metrics alone don't capture important behavioral differences between evaluators.

Abstract: Automated reference-based evaluation methods play a critical role in assessing natural language generation systems. Existing meta-evaluation primarily measures agreement with human judgments or benchmark labels, providing limited insight into evaluator behavior under controlled conditions. We introduce behavioral correctness assumptions, a complementary framework for evaluating reference-based automatic evaluation methods. We define a taxonomy of correctness-preserving and correctness-altering assumptions and operationalize them through controlled response transformations that specify expected scoring behaviors. We evaluate diverse lexical, character-level, semantic, LLM-based, and hybrid evaluators and analyze their assumption-level behavior, stability, sensitivity, repeat-run variability, configuration sensitivity, and reproducibility. Our experiments reveal distinct behavioral trade-offs across evaluation paradigms: no evaluator satisfies all proposed correctness assumptions, and evaluators with similar aggregate performance can exhibit substantially different behavioral profiles. These findings demonstrate that behavioral correctness assumptions provide diagnostic information obscured by conventional aggregate meta-evaluation.

</details>


### [97] [RISE: Recursive Improvement via Self-Extrapolating Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05295)
*Yang Li,Semih Yavuz,Shafiq Joty*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RISE introduces recursive self-improvement by extrapolating from a model's own RL training trajectory to create synthetic teachers for on-policy distillation, avoiding dependency on external models or privileged conditioning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current on-policy distillation (OPD) approaches are limited by teacher quality: external teachers suffer from distribution mismatch, while self-distillation with privileged conditioning is limited by in-context learning capacity. There's a need for a method that provides dense token-level supervision without these limitations.

Method: RISE constructs synthetic teachers from the model's own RLVR training trajectory by extrapolating the displacement between the current checkpoint and a trailing anchor in parameter or output logit space. This converts sparse outcome-induced parameter updates into dense token-level targets without external models or privileged conditioning.

Result: RISE outperforms RLVR-only training and on-policy self-distillation across mathematical reasoning, multi-domain STEM, code generation, and multi-turn agentic tasks.

Conclusion: RISE creates a complementary loop between RLVR and OPD where outcome rewards ground extrapolation toward correct reasoning while extrapolated teachers refine token-level decisions, enabling recursive improvement rather than one-shot compression.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) provides dense, per-token supervision for language model post-training, but its effectiveness is bottlenecked by teacher quality: external teachers suffer from distribution mismatch, while self-distillation with privileged conditioning is limited by in-context learning capacity. We propose \textbf{RISE} (\textbf{R}ecursive \textbf{I}mprovement via \textbf{S}elf-\textbf{E}xtrapolating Policy Distillation), which constructs a synthetic teacher directly from the model's own RLVR training trajectory. By extrapolating the displacement between the current checkpoint and a trailing anchor---in parameter space or output logit space---RISE converts a sparse outcome-induced parameter update into a dense token-level target, without any external model or privileged conditioning. RISE combines RLVR and OPD in a complementary loop: outcome rewards ground the extrapolation toward correct reasoning, while the extrapolated teacher refines token-level decisions. Moreover, since the teacher is refreshed every iteration as the student improves, distillation becomes a recursive improvement mechanism rather than a one-shot compression step. Experiments spanning mathematical reasoning, multi-domain STEM, code generation, and multi-turn agentic tasks show that RISE outperforms RLVR-only training and on-policy self-distillation across all settings.

</details>


### [98] [Large Language Models for HVAC Operations in Building Energy Systems: A Critical Review of Methods, Applications, and Deployment Readiness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05314)
*Alexander Neubauer,Tianzhen Hong,Han Li,Mengbo Yu,Amin Darbandi,Yannick Fürst,Martin Kriegel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This systematic review analyzes 66 LLM-for-HVAC studies (2023-2026), finding current LLMs serve as semantic/workflow layers rather than autonomous controllers, with most research at early stages and only 4 reaching pilot-level evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Building automation systems generate rich sensor data but remain "insight-poor" due to heterogeneous point naming, missing metadata, and fragmented documentation that obstruct operational use of HVAC systems.

Method: Systematic review and coding of 66 peer-reviewed studies on LLMs for HVAC operations, classifying each across five application families and three LLM method families, and assessing evidence realism, deployment readiness, and responsibility boundaries.

Result: Corpus concentrated in building energy modeling (32/66 papers), with only 4 studies reaching pilot-level evidence and none reporting sustained operational deployment. No studies ready-now for industry adoption (3 near-term, 63 research-only). LLMs serve as semantic/workflow layers, while conventional ML/MPC/RL remain more adopted for control tasks.

Conclusion: Current evidence supports LLMs primarily as semantic and workflow layers rather than autonomous HVAC controllers. Future work should prioritize field-validated benchmarks, orchestration evaluation under operational constraints, and LLM-MPC/RL architectures with bounded latency and verifiable safety properties.

Abstract: Building automation systems generate rich sensor data yet remain insight-poor because heterogeneous point naming, missing metadata, and fragmented documentation obstruct their operational use. This systematic review analyses and codes 66 peer-reviewed studies on large language models (LLMs) for HVAC operations published between 2023 and March 2026.
