Each study is classified across five application families and three LLM method families and assessed for evidence realism, deployment readiness, and the responsibility boundary between the LLM and physical HVAC decisions. The corpus is concentrated in building energy modelling (BEM, 32 of 66 papers), while load forecasting remains too sparse for subfield-level conclusions. Only four studies reach pilot-level evidence, and none reports sustained operational deployment. No study was classified as ready-now for industry adoption; three were near-term and 63 research-only. Nevertheless, several bounded, human-in-the-loop uses merit near-term trials, including point-name normalisation, document-grounded operator support, BEM workflow assistance, and advisory interfaces around physics-based controllers. Conventional machine learning (ML), model predictive control (MPC), reinforcement learning (RL) and ontology-based tools remain more adopted for high-frequency control, short-horizon numerical forecasting, and well-posed ontology mapping, while autonomous agentic operation and unvalidated occupant proxies remain research-stage. Current evidence therefore supports LLMs primarily as semantic and workflow layers rather than autonomous HVAC controllers. Future work should prioritise field-validated benchmarks, orchestration evaluation under operational constraints, and LLM-MPC/RL architectures with bounded latency and verifiable safety properties.

</details>


### [99] [LLM-Driven Algorithm Design for Quantum Circuit Synthesis based on Binary Decision Diagrams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05327)
*Yoonju Sim,Federico Berto,Chuanbo Hua,Jinkyoo Park,Changhyun Kwon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: QuantumEvo uses LLMs to evolve BDD variable ordering heuristics that optimize quantum circuit cost rather than just BDD size, achieving competitive performance against baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current BDD-based reversible circuit synthesis methods rely on variable ordering heuristics that minimize BDD size, which is an imperfect proxy for quantum circuit cost (QCC). There's a need for QCC-aware variable ordering heuristics to generate more efficient quantum circuits.

Method: Proposed QuantumEvo evolutionary framework uses LLMs as heuristic generators for QCC-aware BDD variable ordering. Instead of directly predicting orderings, it searches over ordering heuristics initialized from multiple heuristic families. Candidate heuristics manipulate variable orderings using standard BDD operations and are selected based on downstream QCC performance. The discovered heuristic HGA-QE modifies the sifting step inside a genetic algorithm to better align with QCC.

Result: HGA-QE achieves 70.9% tie-or-win rate against per-function best baseline, and is strictly best on 13.5% of functions. Shows clearer relative advantage on benchmark suites from sources different from training data used for heuristic discovery.

Conclusion: Evolutionary framework with LLMs can effectively discover QCC-aware BDD variable ordering heuristics, leading to improved quantum circuit synthesis performance compared to traditional BDD-size-focused approaches.

Abstract: Quantum circuits are central to implementing quantum algorithms on quantum devices, where quantum gates must be reversible. Many quantum algorithms rely on Boolean functions, which must therefore be implemented reversibly within quantum circuits. Reversible circuit synthesis provides a way to translate such Boolean functions into reversible circuits. Binary decision diagrams (BDDs) offer a scalable approach to this task, but the resulting BDDs and circuits depend heavily on variable ordering. Existing ordering heuristics commonly minimize BDD size because it is closely tied to the circuit size. However, BDD size is an imperfect proxy for the quantum cost of the synthesized circuit (QCC). We propose \texttt{QuantumEvo}, an evolutionary framework that uses an LLM as a heuristic generator for QCC-aware BDD variable ordering. Instead of predicting orderings directly, \texttt{QuantumEvo} searches over ordering heuristics initialized from multiple heuristic families. Candidate heuristics directly manipulate variable orderings using standard BDD operations and are selected by downstream QCC. The discovered heuristic, HGA-QE, modifies the sifting step inside a genetic algorithm so that the procedure is better aligned with QCC. Across the benchmark set, HGA-QE achieves a 70.9\% tie-or-win rate against the per-function best baseline and is strictly best on 13.5\% of the functions. The results demonstrate broadly competitive QCC performance, with HGA-QE showing a clearer relative advantage in strict wins on the two benchmark suites drawn from sources different from the data used for heuristic discovery.

</details>


### [100] [Technical Manual for a Toolkit for Measuring Contextual Individuation in Transformer Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05333)
*José Luciano Verçosa Marques,Frederico Jorge Heitmann,Daniel Omar Perez,Marcelo Vinicius de Paula,Tárcio André dos Santos Barros*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Researchers developed an open toolkit to test whether transformer language models can distinguish different senses of the same word across domains, using controlled "bridge forms" and comprehensive pipeline design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test the widely held belief that transformer language models individuate word occurrences by context, researchers needed a clean construct that holds word form constant while varying context and sense in a controlled way.

Method: Created a toolkit using "bridge forms" - single words with different senses across domains. The pipeline includes: declarative specification of bridge forms and source domains, Wikipedia corpus acquisition, occurrence localization, layer-wise representation extraction, domain-pairwise silhouette measurement of separation, and paired visualization protocols.

Result: The paper documents a complete methodological toolkit with specific design choices to avoid various failure modes (sense contamination, multi-group bias, subword-tokenization misalignment, visualization artifacts), but does not report empirical results from applying it.

Conclusion: The toolkit provides a rigorous instrument for testing how transformer models represent word senses across contexts, with the design published as a reference for studies that will use it to produce and interpret empirical results.

Abstract: A transformer language model assigns a single, context-independent vector to a word type at its embedding layer, yet is widely believed to individuate that word's occurrences by context in its later layers. Testing this belief cleanly requires a construct that holds the word form fixed while its context and intended sense vary in a controlled, labeled way. This manual documents an open toolkit built around such a construct, which we call a bridge form: a single written word that recurs, unchanged, across two or more subject domains with a different sense in each. We describe, and justify, every stage of the pipeline: the declarative specification of bridge forms and their source domains, corpus acquisition from Wikipedia, occurrence localization, layer-wise representation extraction, a domain-pairwise silhouette measurement of separation in the model's representation space, and a paired visualization protocol. Each design choice is presented together with the methodological failure mode it is meant to avoid (sense contamination from overly broad category labels, the multi-group bias of the silhouette coefficient, subword-tokenization misalignment, and axis-comparability artifacts in dimensionality-reduced plots, among others). This manuscript is a methodological and implementation reference: it does not report or interpret empirical outcomes of running the toolkit on any particular model or bridge-form set. The toolkit, its full source, and the corpora used to exercise it are archived separately (Section 9) under a persistent identifier, and are intended to be cited as an instrument by studies that use it to produce and interpret empirical results.

</details>


### [101] [Does Your Agent's Memory Survive a Model Upgrade? A Controlled Study of Memory Portability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05339)
*Ankit Goyal,Jaideep Ray*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Model upgrades can cause memory failures: fixed-schema knowledge graphs transfer reliably, while compressed natural-language notes exhibit high model coupling and asymmetrical accuracy shifts during migrations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Model upgrades are routine but memory migrations are problematic - new models may interpret old memory differently, mixed embeddings may break retrieval, and memory repair may fail without original evidence.

Method: Compared four memory approaches: verbatim long-context reading (LC-RAW), chunked retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), compressed natural-language notes (NOTES), and fixed-schema knowledge graph (KG-fixed). Used 48 synthetic histories with randomized codes, exact scoring, and two open-weight models (<10B parameters).

Result: KG-fixed transfers reliably (+0.0004 ±0.0020 accuracy change). NOTES show high model coupling with asymmetric accuracy shifts (+9.91 or -13.28 pp). RAG with 50/50 mixed index captures only 4.96-point improvement vs 11.90-point full re-embedding. 80% of NOTES deficit from information loss during construction; 81% of RAG deficit from retrieval failures. NOTES repair fails to reach 90% recovery in all cases, while raw source history enables recovery in 34/48 cases.

Conclusion: Memory migration requires direction-specific testing, strict embedding space isolation, and retention of source histories for effective repair. Fixed-schema structures offer reliable transfer during model upgrades.

Abstract: Model upgrades are routine; memory migrations are not. An agent can keep the same memory store and still forget: a new model may interpret old notes differently, mixed embedding versions may break retrieval, and repair may fail without the original evidence. We compare memory as the same history is preserved verbatim for long-context reading (LC-RAW), divided into chunks for retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), compressed by a model into natural-language notes (NOTES), or normalized into a fixed-schema knowledge graph (KG-fixed). The study uses 48 synthetic histories with randomized answer codes, exact scoring, and two open-weight models with sub 10 billion parameters.
  Our measurements show that fixed-schema structures transfer reliably, with KG-fixed accuracy changing by only $+0.0004 \pm 0.0020$ following a writer swap. Conversely, compressed NOTES exhibit high model coupling, with accuracy shifting asymmetrically by $+9.91$ or $-13.28$ percentage points depending on the specific migration direction. In RAG systems, partial embedding migrations using a 50/50 mixed index capture only a 4.96-point accuracy improvement, forfeiting the majority of the 11.90-point gain achieved through full re-embedding. Diagnostic decomposition attributes 80% ($0.467 \pm 0.014$) of the NOTES accuracy deficit to information lost during initial construction, whereas retrieval failures drive 81% ($0.364 \pm 0.012$) of the RAG deficit. Finally, store-only repair of NOTES fails to reach a 90% performance recovery target in all 48 test cases, whereas retaining the raw source history enables successful recovery in 34 of 48 cases for one tested direction. These findings highlight the necessity of direction-specific migration testing, strict embedding space isolation, and the retention of source histories for memory repair.

</details>


### [102] [Who Should Grade My Work? Student Perspectives on Transparent AI-Assisted Writing Assessment in Higher Education](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05346)
*Rayed AlGhamdi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Students distinguish between AI feedback's usefulness for surface-level revision and human instructors' authority over grading decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Limited research on how students interpret AI-generated evaluation when explicitly informed it's from AI rather than human instructor, despite increasing use of GenAI tools in higher education assessment.

Method: Qualitative pedagogical inquiry with 13 male computing students at Saudi public university, using handwritten writing task evaluated by ChatGPT, followed by student reflections analyzed through inductive thematic analysis.

Result: Four themes identified: perceived usefulness of feedback; awareness of AI's contextual/pedagogical limitations; conditional trust distinguishing feedback utility from evaluative authority; and reflection on human instructor's institutional/pedagogical role.

Conclusion: Students accept GenAI feedback for surface-level revision but reserve grading authority for human instructors, demonstrating important distinction between feedback utility and evaluative authority in AI-mediated assessment.

Abstract: The integration of GenAI tools into higher education assessment raises important questions about how students understand, interpret, and respond to AI-mediated evaluation. As instructors increasingly explore AI tools for providing feedback, prior research has examined whether GenAI-generated feedback improves writing performance and how students perceive its usefulness; comparatively little is known, however, about how students interpret such evaluation when they are explicitly informed that an AI system, rather than a human instructor, produced the feedback and the score. This study reports findings from a qualitative pedagogical inquiry conducted in an undergraduate technical communication course for computing students at a Saudi public university. Thirteen male undergraduate computing students completed an in-class handwritten writing task; the scanned submissions were evaluated by ChatGPT using a rubric-based prompt aligned with the task objectives. Students were then explicitly informed that ChatGPT had generated the score and feedback and were invited to reflect on the evaluation in writing. Inductive thematic analysis of these reflections identified four themes: perceived usefulness of feedback; awareness of AI's contextual and pedagogical limitations; conditional trust, distinguishing feedback utility from evaluative authority; and reflection on the institutional and pedagogical role of the human instructor. Participants accepted GenAI feedback as useful for surface-level revision but consistently positioned the human instructor as the appropriate authority over grading decisions. The study identifies this as a distinction between feedback utility and evaluative authority, two judgments that students treat as analytically separate rather than as opposite ends of a single approval scale...

</details>


### [103] [CUA-Universe: A Scalable and Dynamic Environment for Hybrid GUI+CLI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05374)
*Haoting Shi,Wenhao Wang,Weicheng Fang,Yaozhong Liang,Tian Jin,Pengxiang Zhao,Guangyi Liu,Siheng Chen,Yanfeng Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CUA-Universe is a scalable environment-to-data pipeline that turns real desktop software into hybrid GUI+CLI environments, enabling computer-use agents to coordinate both modalities effectively and improving agent performance on benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current computer-use agents mostly operate through GUI interfaces, producing inefficient trajectories. Real-world computer work requires hybrid operations combining visual-state inspection with command-line operations, but scalable hybrid environments are scarce due to manual engineering requirements for each application. Existing agents struggle to use GUI and CLI complementarily.

Method: CUA-Universe has three components: App-Forge adapts applications into reproducible VMs with command-line surfaces (discovering, wrapping, or generating CLI interfaces, scaling to 16 apps); Task-Weave synthesizes diverse hybrid tasks with controllable difficulty from reusable operations; Path-Steer steers rollouts along efficient hybrid paths and harvests verified trajectories.

Result: Training on CUA-Universe data shifts agent behavior from inefficient GUI interactions toward effective GUI+CLI orchestration. Their 9B model improves success rates and efficiency: CUA-Verse (Score +39.3 pts; -37% steps, -60% tokens), OSWorld (SR +16.8 pts; -57% steps, -44% tokens), and OSWorld-MCP (Score +7.84 pts; -27% steps, -30% tokens).

Conclusion: CUA-Universe provides a scalable path toward more capable and efficient computer-use agents by enabling hybrid GUI+CLI orchestration in real desktop applications, addressing the limitations of current single-modality approaches.

Abstract: Computer-use agents have advanced on benchmarks like OSWorld and AndroidWorld, but still act mostly through the GUI, often producing inefficient trajectories. Real-world computer work is hybrid, combining visual-state inspection with precise, high-throughput command-line operations, so capable agents must coordinate both modalities over shared application state. Yet scalable hybrid environments remain scarce because supporting both GUI and CLI over real applications typically requires substantial manual engineering for each application. Existing agents also struggle to use the two interfaces complementarily: CLI-native agents lack visual perception for tasks involving interface state or layout, while GUI-native agents are inefficient for operations better executed through commands. We introduce CUA-Universe, a scalable environment-to-data pipeline that turns real desktop software into hybrid GUI+CLI environments. App-Forge adapts applications into reproducible VMs and command-line surfaces it discovers, wraps, or generates, scaling to 16 applications; Task-Weave synthesizes diverse hybrid tasks of controllable difficulty from reusable operations over seed files; and Path-Steer steers rollouts along efficient hybrid paths and harvests verified trajectories for post-training. Training on this data shifts behavior from inefficient GUI interaction and brittle CLI scripting toward effective GUI+CLI orchestration. Our 9B model improves both success and efficiency on CUA-Verse (Score +39.3 pts; -37% steps, -60% tokens), OSWorld (SR +16.8 pts; -57% steps, -44% tokens), and OSWorld-MCP (Score +7.84 pts; -27% steps, -30% tokens). CUA-Universe provides a scalable path toward more capable and efficient computer-use agents.

</details>


### [104] [Molecular Déjà Vu: Digit-Level Retrieval of Published Values in Frontier Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05381)
*Matthias Busch,Marius Tacke,Sviatlana V. Lamaka,Mikhail L. Zheludkevich,Christian J. Cyron,Roland C. Aydin,Christian Feiler*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs performing molecular property prediction often verbatim retrieve published benchmark values rather than genuinely predicting properties, with retrieval patterns varying by dataset, reasoning level, and model capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM evaluations on molecular property benchmarks cannot distinguish between genuine prediction and verbatim retrieval of published benchmark values, raising concerns about the validity of claimed model capabilities.

Method: Audited 22 frontier models on 12 regression benchmarks to detect verbatim retrieval, conducted experiments at two reasoning levels, and tested methods to interrupt retrieval using transformed SMILES strings and original labels.

Result: Found widespread but benchmark-specific verbatim retrieval (5 datasets had >50% retrieval by LLMs), reasoning levels affected retrieval detection (89% more flagged at higher reasoning), and strongest models could still recognize transformed inputs, suggesting retrieval patterns don't solely determine predictive capability.

Conclusion: LLM performance on molecular regression benchmarks is significantly influenced by verbatim retrieval behavior, which varies by dataset and reasoning level, indicating that true predictive capability differs from memorization patterns, necessitating more sophisticated evaluation methods.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly evaluated on molecular property benchmarks, but accuracy cannot distinguish a model that predicts a property from one that retrieves a published number. We audit 22 frontier models on 12 regression benchmarks for verbatim retrieval and find that it is widespread but relatively benchmark-specific: on five datasets more than $50\%$ of the LLMs show verbatim retrieval, while on the remaining datasets it appears only in isolated cells. We run our experiments at two reasoning levels and find that reasoning changes retrieval. The same experiments, on the same molecules and with the same prompt, are flagged $89\%$ more often at the higher reasoning level than at the lowest one. Finally, we test a way to interrupt retrieval in our most contaminated cases, and find that the strongest models in some cases still recognise a combination of transformed SMILES strings and original labels. Furthermore, suppressing retrieval moves the prediction errors of the different models closer together in relative terms, while their differing use of verbatim retrieval spreads them apart. This indicates that the general predictive capability of an LLM is not determined solely by the amount of memorised values. This work provides an overview of the amount and depth of verbatim retrieval in molecular regression benchmarks using LLMs.

</details>


### [105] [Necessary or Sufficient? Evaluating LLM Explanations With Behavioural Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05385)
*Urja Pawar,Rajitha Ramanayake,Nabeel Kemal,Ashwin Kandath,Owen O'Neill,Guillaume Bourgeon,Houssem Chatbri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM explanations in agent workflows are evaluated for reliability using necessity and sufficiency interpretations, showing moderate correlation but often failing to identify the most influential factors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM decision components in agent workflows provide explanations alongside recommendations/judgments, which operators rely on for monitoring, error diagnosis, and escalation decisions. There's a crucial assumption that these explanations accurately reflect the actual decision behavior, which needs validation.

Method: The paper tests two interpretations of named factors in LLM explanations: necessity (changing a factor changes output) and sufficiency (retaining factor while removing other information preserves output). They use controlled black-box interventions to estimate necessity and sufficiency scores for cited factors in two synthetic use cases: advisor recommendation and prompt monitoring for harmfulness/risk.

Result: Across eight models (Claude, GPT, Gemini families), mean Spearman correlations between cited ranking and necessity/sufficiency scores were 0.349 and 0.354 for advisor recommendation, and 0.431 and 0.580 for prompt monitoring. Uncited factors often scored higher than cited ones (57.6-58.1% for advisor, 8.9-25.8% for prompt monitoring). Cited top-three factors contain useful information but don't reliably identify the three most influential factors.

Conclusion: The framework provides a black-box reliability check for LLM explanations used in agent oversight, showing that while explanations contain useful information, they don't reliably identify the most influential factors. This helps validate explanations but also reveals limitations in current LLM explanation reliability.

Abstract: LLM decision components that can operate within agent workflows often produce action-relevant recommendations or judgements together with explanations. Operators may use the named factors to monitor a system, diagnose errors, or decide when to escalate an output. Such use assumes that the explanations agree with the component's observable decision behaviour. We test two interpretations of the named factors: necessity, meaning that changing a factor would change the output, and sufficiency, meaning that retaining it while removing other changeable information would preserve the output. We evaluate these interpretations in two synthetic use cases: recommending advisors to clients and judging prompts for harmfulness or risk. Models return an output and the top three factors that most influenced it. Controlled black-box interventions estimate a necessity score for each factor by measuring how often changing it changes the output, and a sufficiency score by measuring how often retaining it preserves the output. Across eight models from the Claude, GPT, and Gemini families, the mean Spearman correlations between the cited ranking and the necessity and sufficiency scores are 0.349 and 0.354 for advisor recommendation, and 0.431 and 0.580 for prompt monitoring. Furthermore, an uncited factor scores above the lowest-scoring cited factor in 57.6% of advisor responses under necessity and 58.1% under sufficiency; the corresponding prompt-monitoring rates are 25.8% and 8.9%. The cited top three contain useful information but do not reliably identify the three factors with the strongest measured influence under necessity or sufficiency. The framework provides a black-box reliability check for explanations used in agent oversight while remaining scoped to individual LLM decisions.

</details>


### [106] [Multi-Step Tool-Calling over Korean Open Public APIs: A Benchmark and a Data-Synthesis Recipe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05395)
*Dain Kim,Eungi Cho,Kyumin Kim,Shinyeong Noh,Kyuseong Lim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EDGE is a method to synthesize multi-step tool-calling data by building execution-verified graphs of tool interactions, enabling better training of open-source LLMs for government API tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Public institutions need open-source, on-premise LLM agents for data sovereignty, but current open-source models underperform in multi-step tool-calling scenarios, and there's no benchmark to measure this performance gap.

Method: EDGE (Execution-grounded Dynamic Graph) synthesizes tool-calling data by: 1) building a graph of how tool outputs can feed other tool inputs, 2) verifying links by actually executing against live APIs, 3) traversing verified links to create executable multi-step trajectories, and 4) fine-tuning models via GRPO on this synthesized dataset.

Result: The 9B model fine-tuned with EDGE nearly matches the performance of untuned 27B models from the same family, showing substantial improvements on both KOPA-Bench (145 real-world tasks) and BFCL benchmark.

Conclusion: EDGE effectively closes the performance gap for open-source LLMs in multi-step tool-calling scenarios, enabling data-sovereign deployment of capable on-premise agents for government applications.

Abstract: Data-sovereignty regulations increasingly require public institutions to deploy open-source, on-premise LLM agents that chain multiple tool-calls across live government APIs. However, open-source models consistently underperform in this multi-step setting, and no existing benchmark measures the gap. We introduce the Korean Open Public API Benchmark (KOPA-Bench), comprising 145 real-world tasks. To close this gap, we present EDGE, an Execution-grounded Dynamic Graph for tool-calling data synthEsis driven by live execution. EDGE builds a graph of how each tool's output can feed another's input, keeps only the links that succeed when actually called against the live APIs, and traverses these verified links to synthesize executable multi-step trajectories. Fine-tuned via GRPO on the resulting dataset, our 9B model nearly matches the untuned 27B model from the same family, improving substantially not only on KOPA-Bench but also on the BFCL benchmark.

</details>


### [107] [A Deep Generative Model for Synthesizing Labeled Wireless Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05396)
*Yuxiao Li,Keke Hu,Santiago Mazuelas,Yuan Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IIns-GAN uses deep learning to generate realistic labeled wireless signals for sensing tasks, addressing high data collection costs and model limitations of traditional synthesis methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world wireless signal datasets are expensive to collect and label. Traditional synthetic methods based on environmental models require extensive hyperparameter tuning and lack realism needed for effective model training.

Method: Introduces Inter-Instance Generative Adversarial Networks (IIns-GAN), a deep learning-based approach to generate realistic labeled wireless signals adaptable to different environment scenarios.

Result: Extensive experiments on Ultra-Wideband datasets show generated signals mirror physical characteristics of real measurements and significantly improve model training for wireless sensing tasks like distance estimation and environment identification.

Conclusion: IIns-GAN provides an effective solution for generating realistic labeled wireless signals that enhance model training while overcoming the high costs and limitations of traditional data collection and synthesis methods.

Abstract: Wireless signals with position-related labels are pivotal for both performance evaluation and model training in the realm of wireless sensing. However, acquiring real-world datasets is often challenged by significant measurement and labeling costs. Traditional methods for synthesizing labeled wireless signals typically rely on environmental models, leading to extensive hyper-parameter tuning and inadequate realism for comprehensive model training purposes. To address these limitations, we introduce a novel deep learning (DL)-based method, namely Inter-Instance Generative Adversarial Networks (IIns-GAN), to generate realistic labeled wireless signals. The generated signals are particularly adaptive to different environment scenarios and well-suited for various model training tasks, including distance estimation and environment identification. We have conducted extensive experiments on public Ultra-Wideband (UWB) datasets to evaluate the realism and utility of the generated signals. The results demonstrate that the signals generated by IIns-GAN mirror the physical characteristics of real-world measurements, and significantly contribute to the improvement of model training in diverse wireless sensing tasks.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [108] [Spectral-Target Physical Latent Structuring for JEPA-Style World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04264)
*Penghao Zhu,Salvatore Penachio,Kaustav Mukherjee,Aneesh Jonelagadda*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A method using Fourier auxiliary head supervision prevents physical representation laziness in latent world models, improving planning success in dynamic environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Latent world models can suffer from "physical representation laziness" where learned latent states don't collapse but fail to represent key physical properties, causing downstream planning failure in dynamic environments.

Method: Proposes training-time auxiliary supervision with a lightweight "Fourier auxiliary head" that enforces physically-informed structuring of latent space without inference-time cost.

Result: Auxiliary head substantially improves planning success rates in dynamic environments with representation laziness, leads to modest improvements elsewhere, and shows higher latent space correlations with physical properties.

Conclusion: Fourier auxiliary head method improves overall success rate, data efficiency, and prevents representation laziness in latent world models through physically-structured latent states.

Abstract: Latent world models have become increasingly popular as a method to predict and plan in latent space rather than pixel space. Recent architectures, such as LeWorldModel (LeWM), jointly train the encoder and predictor using regularization techniques like SIGReg to prevent representation collapse. Even with such regularization preventing representation collapse, we identify a new world model failure mode of \textit{physical representation laziness}, particularly noted in highly dynamic environments. For these lazy cases, the learned latent states do not collapse but nonetheless fail to represent key physical properties, causing ubiquitous downstream planning failure. To resolve this issue, we propose training-time auxiliary supervision with a lightweight "Fourier auxiliary head", which enforces physically-informed structuring of the latent space with no additional inference-time cost and can be generalized to any environment. Experimentally, we show that the auxiliary head substantially improves planning success rates in dynamic environments where the baseline LeWM exhibits physical representation laziness. It also leads to modest improvements in other environments, even when the baseline does not exhibit physical representation laziness. We further observe superior planning performance being accompanied by higher latent space correlations with key physical properties, indicating both the ability of our method to physically structure latent states and the potential planning-side benefit to the learned representation being physically structured. We also see in low-data regimes, auxiliary supervision is particularly impactful in increasing success rate. These findings support the use of our Fourier auxiliary head method to improve both overall success rate and data efficiency, while avoiding representation laziness in latent world models.

</details>


### [109] [ProToMEx: Rapid, Interpretable Explanations via Structured Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04265)
*Athina Georgara,Adarsh Valoor,Sarvapali D. Ramchurn*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ProToMEx introduces probabilistic topic modeling for explainable AI, learning high-level semantic topics as reasons for classifications instead of just feature importance, while being 30-40x faster than SHAP/LIME.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current feature attribution explainers (like SHAP, LIME) struggle to capture complex combinatorial patterns that drive model decisions, focusing only on individual feature importance without revealing underlying semantic structures.

Method: ProToMEx leverages Probabilistic Topic Models (PTMs) to learn latent "topics" representing distinct, high-level reasons for classifications. It's a model-agnostic framework that provides both global explanations of overall model behavior and local explanations disentangling multiple co-existing reasons for specific predictions.

Result: ProToMEx produces explanations with comparable fidelity to SHAP and LIME while drastically reducing computational cost (~30-40x faster). It generates both global and local explanations, making it suitable for real-time applications.

Conclusion: ProToMEx offers a new paradigm for explainability that moves beyond simple feature attribution to reveal semantic structures, addressing limitations of existing methods while being computationally efficient enough for real-time use.

Abstract: Existing post-hoc explainers for machine learning classifiers primarily focus on feature attribution, assigning importance scores to individual features. While valuable, this approach struggles to articulate the complex, combinatorial patterns that often drive a model's decision-making process. To overcome this limitation, we introduce ProToMEx, a new paradigm for explainability that leverages Probabilistic Topic Models (PTMs). Our model-agnostic framework learns latent ''topics'' that represent distinct, high-level reasons for a classification, moving beyond simple feature importance to reveal underlying semantic structures. ProToMEx naturally provides both global explanations of a model's overall behaviour and local explanations that can disentangle multiple co-existing reasons for a specific prediction. We demonstrate empirically that ProToMEx not only produces explanations of comparable fidelity to popular methods like SHAP and LIME but also drastically reduces the amortised computational cost of generating local explanations, making it highly suitable for real-time applications. Specifically, we show that ProToMEx is ~30-40x faster than SHAP and LIME over standardised tabular datasets and synthetic datasets.

</details>


### [110] [A Data Fusion Framework for Grounding Aerospace Surrogate Model via Experimental Wind-Tunnel Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04267)
*Nitin Nagesh Kulkarni,Dheeraj Vemula,Yin Yu,Peter Lyu,Juan J. Alonso*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A framework for experimentally correcting CFD-trained deep learning surrogates using wind-tunnel PSP measurements improves predictive accuracy while preserving computational efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CFD-trained surrogate models show systematic discrepancies with experimental data, limiting their real-world predictive fidelity despite excellent CFD reproduction.

Method: A correction network is trained on spatially registered PSP measurements to learn the discrepancy between surrogate-predicted and experimentally measured surface-pressure distributions, without modifying the pretrained surrogate parameters.

Result: The correction substantially improves agreement with PSP data, reducing prediction error magnitude and area exceeding 0.05 Cp, achieving within 2.3-2.7% of measured Cp range on held-out data, outperforming direct interpolation.

Conclusion: Experimental measurements can effectively ground large-scale simulation-trained surrogates by learning systematic CFD-to-experiment discrepancies while preserving generalization capability and computational efficiency.

Abstract: Aerodynamic surrogate models trained on high-fidelity CFD data reproduce numerical predictions of both scalar outputs and entire fields accurately, yet their predictive fidelity is limited by systematic discrepancies between CFD and experimental observations. We present an experimentally grounded correction framework that adapts a CFD-trained deep learning surrogate using wind-tunnel PSP measurements. A Geotransolver surrogate trained on 2,300 high-fidelity CFD simulations of the NASA CRM wing-body configuration, spanning geometric variation, Mach 0.70-0.85, and angles of attack 0 to 4 degrees, reproduces the CFD integrated aerodynamic forces and pitching moment to R2 > 0.99 but does not match the experimental data. To incorporate experimental information without retraining the surrogate, a correction network is trained on spatially registered PSP measurements at two freestream Mach numbers (0.70 and 0.85) across the same angle-of-attack range, learning the discrepancy between the surrogate-predicted and experimentally measured surface-pressure distributions. At Mach 0.85 the correction substantially improves agreement with PSP, particularly at the wing suction peak, shock location, and subsequent pressure recovery, reducing both the magnitude of the prediction error and the fraction of wetted surface on which it exceeds 0.05 in Cp, and it does so from a limited experimental dataset without modifying the pretrained surrogate parameters. On held-out angles of attack the grounded surrogate agrees with measurement to within 2.3-2.7% of the measured Cp range, and outperforms direct interpolation between the measured conditions at every state tested. Experimental measurements can therefore ground a large-scale simulation-trained surrogate by learning systematic CFD-to-experiment discrepancies while preserving its generalization capability and computational efficiency.

</details>


### [111] [Quantum-Assisted Memory-Efficient Training for Parameter-Intensive Wi-Fi-Based Human Activity Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04271)
*To Truong An,Jie Zhang,Guolin Yin,Junqing Zhang,Yanjiao Li,Trung Q. Duong,Simon L. Cotton*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A quantum-assisted memory-efficient training framework (Q-MET) for Wi-Fi human activity recognition that reduces parameter count by 90-95% while maintaining accuracy, with additional model pruning for efficient inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wi-Fi-based human activity recognition (HAR) systems rely on computationally intensive deep learning models that are challenging to deploy in real-world scenarios due to high memory consumption during both training and inference phases.

Method: Proposes Q-MET: a hybrid quantum-classical neural network framework that indirectly generates parameters for HAR models, reducing trainable parameters. Integrates structured pruning during training to support deployment on resource-constrained devices.

Result: Achieves 90-95% reduction in trainable parameters compared to conventional backpropagation, maintains or exceeds classical classification accuracy. Enables 75-85% model sparsity with less than 2% accuracy loss through structured pruning.

Conclusion: Q-MET represents the first quantum-assisted approach to address memory inefficiencies in both training and inference stages of Wi-Fi HAR systems, making such systems more practical for real-world deployment.

Abstract: Wi-Fi-based human activity recognition (HAR) has become an important part of integrated sensing and communications, paving the way for a range of context-aware services. However, most existing Wi-Fi-based HAR systems rely on deep learning (DL) models that are computationally and memory intensive in both training and inference, which poses significant challenges for real-world deployment. Conventional training requires simultaneous updates of millions of parameters, leading to prohibitive memory consumption. In this paper, we propose a novel quantum-assisted memory-efficient training framework (Q-MET) designed to improve efficiency in both training and inference. Q-MET utilizes a hybrid quantum classical neural network to indirectly generate parameters for HAR models, significantly reducing the trainable parameter count compared to direct optimization. To further support the deployment on resource-constrained devices, we integrate structured pruning during the training phase. Experimental results demonstrate that Q-MET achieves a 90% to 95% reduction in trainable parameters compared with conventional backpropagation-based DL training while maintaining or even exceeding classical classification accuracy. Additionally, Q-MET supports lightweight inference through structured pruning, achieving 75% to 85% model sparsity with less than 2% loss in classification accuracy. To the best of our knowledge, this work represents the first quantum-assisted approach to simultaneously tackle memory inefficiencies in both the training and inference stages of HAR systems.

</details>


### [112] [Evaluating Large Language Models for Forced Outage Risk Prediction: Benefits and Comparison to Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04272)
*Christos Petridis,Zoran Obradovic,Mladen Kezunovic*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLMs can predict weather-related grid outages in zero-shot setting but are outperformed by supervised models on traditional metrics, though newer LLMs show competitive performance and offer complementary benefits in reasoning and scalability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional supervised approaches for outage prediction require extensive labeled training data, which can be expensive and time-consuming to collect. There's interest in exploring whether LLMs can perform similar tasks in a zero-shot setting without labeled data, potentially offering more scalable and cost-effective solutions.

Method: Formulates outage prediction as binary severity classification across three forecast horizons (3h, 6h, 12h). Uses six years of outage records and high-resolution weather data from a Texas utility. Benchmarks four zero-shot LLMs against two supervised classifiers using two input configurations: current weather observations and weather forecast data.

Result: Supervised models outperform LLMs on macro-F1 and precision metrics, but newer LLM generations achieve competitive scores. LLMs demonstrate complementary strengths in actionable reasoning and geographic scalability, suggesting they could be valuable despite lower traditional performance metrics.

Conclusion: While supervised models currently achieve better traditional performance metrics, LLMs offer unique advantages in reasoning and scalability. The optimal approach may involve combining both methods to leverage their complementary strengths for power grid outage prediction.

Abstract: This study examines the ability of large language models (LLMs) to predict the risk of weather-related forced outages in the distribution grid in a zero-shot framework, without labeled training data. The problem is formulated as a binary severity classification task across three forecast horizons (3h, 6h, 12h), using six years of outage records and high-resolution weather data for a utility service area in central Texas. Four zero-shot LLMs are benchmarked against two supervised classifiers across two input configurations: one using current weather observations and the other using weather forecast data. Results show that supervised models outperform LLMs on macro-F1 and precision, while newer LLM generations achieve competitive scores. Beyond accuracy, LLMs offer complementary strengths in actionable reasoning and geographic scalability, suggesting that combining them with supervised models may be the best practice.

</details>


### [113] [BER-PEF: Unified Human Mobility Predictability Evaluation via Bayes Error Rate Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04292)
*En Xu,Jingtao Ding,Zhiwen Yu,Yong Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BER-PEF: A Bayesian framework for evaluating mobility predictability estimators without observable ground truth by converting Bayes error rate estimation into predictability assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human mobility predictability's ground truth is not directly observable from real data, making it challenging to evaluate and compare different predictability estimators accurately.

Method: A Bayes-error-rate-based framework that maps diverse data types (symbolic sequences, numeric trajectories, contextual features, learned representations) into a common feature-label space, then evaluates estimator outputs along controlled perturbation curves against a shared predictability reference interval.

Result: Experiments on multiple datasets show BER-based estimators achieve lower reference discrepancy than existing methods, track changes in empirical prediction performance under perturbation, and allow evaluation of contextual inputs and multiple structured representations under the same protocol.

Conclusion: BER-PEF provides a unified and verifiable framework for evaluating predictability estimators on heterogeneous mobility data when ground-truth predictability is unavailable, with evidence aggregation across perturbation levels offering more reliable estimator selection.

Abstract: Human mobility predictability concerns the best prediction performance attainable from a given target and input information, but its ground truth is not directly observable on real mobility data. We present BER-PEF, a Bayes-error-rate-based framework that converts BER estimation into mobility predictability estimation and provides a unified protocol for comparing estimators without observable ground truth. The framework maps symbolic sequences, numeric trajectories, contextual features, and learned representations into a common feature--label space, then evaluates estimator outputs along controlled perturbation curves against a shared predictability reference interval by measuring deviations below the interval, above the interval, and across the full interval. Experiments on Foursquare NYC and TKY, GeoLife, and T-Drive show that several BER-based estimators achieve lower reference discrepancy than existing predictability methods on symbolic sequences and numeric trajectories, while their estimates track changes in empirical prediction performance under perturbation. Additional analyses show that contextual inputs and multiple structured representations can be evaluated under the same protocol, and that aggregating evidence across multiple perturbation levels provides a more reliable basis for estimator selection than relying on a single unperturbed observation. BER-PEF therefore offers a unified and verifiable path for evaluating predictability estimators on heterogeneous mobility data when ground-truth predictability is unavailable.

</details>


### [114] [Data-Driven Learning of Unknown Nonlinear Differential Equations Using Functional Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04329)
*Seyyed Shaho Alaviani,Yongzhi Qu,Gregory W. Vogl*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A new interpretable machine learning method for discovering unknown nonlinear ODEs from single trajectory data without prior physics knowledge, using functional analysis formulation and integral-based cost functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for data-driven discovery of nonlinear ODEs often lack interpretability, require prior physics knowledge, and use discrete error metrics that may not capture function space properties well.

Method: 1) Formulation based on Functional Analysis and Operator Theory
2) Cost function constructed in function space as integral distance between functions (vs. discrete sum of errors)
3) Incremental learning algorithm for online handling of new training samples
4) Capable of discovering vector fields from forced/unforced, autonomous/non-autonomous systems
5) Simultaneously discovers unknown external forces and underlying dynamics

Result: The method can discover unknown vector fields from single trajectory data without physics knowledge, handle various system types (autonomous/non-autonomous, forced/unforced), and identify unknown external forces simultaneously.

Conclusion: The proposed method provides an interpretable, functional analysis-based approach for nonlinear ODE discovery that outperforms existing ML methods by using integral-based function space metrics and online incremental learning, with numerical examples demonstrating its advantages.

Abstract: In this paper, the problem of data-driven discovery of nonlinear ordinary differential equations (ODEs) is recast, and a new interpretable machine learning (ML) method is proposed. The proposed method aims to learn the unknown vector field of nonlinear dynamics without prior knowledge of the system's physics from only one single state trajectory's data. The proposed method has two fundamental differences with existing methods: 1) the formulation presented in this method is derived based on Functional Analysis and Operator Theory, and 2) the cost function is constructed in the function space as a distance between two functions as an integral, instead of the discrete-sum of errors used in existing ML approaches. An incremental learning algorithm is proposed to learn the unknown vector field to handle new training samples in an online manner. The proposed method can discover the unknown vector field from both forced and unforced autonomous and non-autonomous (or time-varying) dynamical systems. The proposed method is able to simultaneously discover unknown external forces as a function of time and unknown underlying dynamics. Finally, numerical examples are given to demonstrate the advantages of the proposed method.

</details>


### [115] [Modular Deep Recurrent Neural Network: Application to Quadrotors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04339)
*Nima Mohajerin,Steven L. Waslander*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A modular RNN framework enables easy deployment and automatic gradient computation, with feedforward inter-layer connections improving learning of high-order dynamics and mitigating vanishing/exploding gradients, demonstrated on quadrotor altitude control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To simplify the deployment of various RNN architectures and enable automatic gradient computation for gradient-based learning methods, while addressing limitations of traditional RNNs in learning complex dynamics.

Method: Introduces a modular deep RNN framework that facilitates architecture deployment and automatic derivative computation. Proposes new architectures including feedforward inter-layer connections in multi-layer RNNs to enhance learning capability.

Result: Feedforward inter-layer connections significantly improve RNN's ability to learn high-order dynamics and nonlinearities, and alleviate vanishing/exploding gradient problems in space. Demonstrated superior performance in quadrotor altitude dynamics modeling compared to existing methods.

Conclusion: The modular RNN with feedforward inter-layer connections offers an effective approach for modeling complex dynamical systems, with demonstrated advantages over existing methods in learning capability and gradient stability.

Abstract: A modular deep Recurrent Neural Network (RNN) is introduced to facilitate the process of deploying various architectures of RNNs, and to automatically compute derivatives for gradient-based learning methods. The modularity leads to a set of new architectures, one of which includes feedforward inter-layer connections. By adding feedforward inter-layer connections in a multi-layer RNN, it is observed that the capability of the RNN to learn and model high-order dynamics and nonlinearities is significantly improved. The problem of vanishing/exploding gradient in space for a multilayer RNN is also alleviated using feedforward connections. These results are demonstrated using a quadrotor case study, for which a model of the altitude dynamics is learned with our particular network structure, while existing methods are unable to generalize as quickly or at all.

</details>


### [116] [SharedSAE: One Feature Dictionary Across Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04344)
*Daniil Ognev,Célian Vasson,Lijie Hu,Kentaro Inui,Benjamin Heinzerling*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SharedSAE enables a single sparse autoencoder to work across multiple language models by sharing a dictionary while using model-specific encoders/decoders, achieving near-dedicated performance with cross-model latent transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current sparse autoencoder (SAE) approaches require training separate SAEs for each language model, which is inefficient and prevents latent knowledge sharing across models.

Method: SharedSAE combines a shared dictionary across models with model-specific encoder-decoder pairs, normalizes only selection scores to preserve magnitudes, uses model dropout for single-model inference, and enables efficient adaptation of new models to the frozen shared dictionary.

Result: SharedSAE retains 96.6% of dedicated SAEs' mean explained variance, achieves 1.8× higher cross-model latent correlations than post-hoc aligned separate SAEs, enables latent description transfer across models, and allows new models to achieve near-dedicated-SAE quality with the frozen shared dictionary.

Conclusion: A single shared sparse autoencoder can effectively replace multiple dedicated SAEs, enabling efficient cross-model interpretability with preserved performance and latent knowledge transfer.

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are widely used to interpret language model activations, but SAE training and latent labelling are typically repeated for every model. Here, we show that a single shared SAE can replace a collection of dedicated per-model SAEs. Our method, SharedSAE, combines a shared dictionary with model-specific encoder-decoder pairs. Unlike the closest prior method, which discards activation magnitudes and requires all models at inference, SharedSAE instead normalizes only selection scores, preserving magnitudes, and uses model dropout for single-model inference. We train SharedSAE on four 1B-scale base language models spanning distinct families and tokenizers. Despite sharing its latents across models, SharedSAE retains 96.6% of dedicated SAEs' mean explained variance; its latent activations exhibit cross-model correlations 1.8 times as high as separate SAEs aligned post-hoc, and its latent descriptions transfer across models. After the dictionary is frozen, new models can be efficiently adapted to it, achieving near-dedicated-SAE reconstruction quality while reusing the shared latent descriptions.

</details>


### [117] [A Quantum Variational Approach to Prototypical Recurrent Unit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04354)
*Mahyar Sadeghi Garjan,Tommaso Cesari,Michel Barbeau*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A lightweight Quantum Prototypical Recurrent Unit (QPRU) is introduced that requires fewer parameters than classical LSTM/GRU and quantum QLSTM/QGRU while achieving competitive forecasting performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current quantum recurrent architectures like QLSTM and QGRU, as well as classical LSTM and GRU, have high parameter counts that limit scalability and practicality for quantum computing applications.

Method: The authors develop a lightweight Quantum Prototypical Recurrent Unit (QPRU) that significantly reduces the number of parameters compared to existing classical and quantum recurrent architectures.

Result: QPRU achieves competitive forecasting performance matching state-of-the-art baselines while offering structural advantages including enhanced scalability and reduced trainable parameters.

Conclusion: The QPRU provides an efficient quantum recurrent architecture that maintains competitive performance while addressing scalability and parameter efficiency challenges in quantum machine learning.

Abstract: We introduce a lightweight Quantum Prototypical Recurrent Unit (QPRU) that requires significantly fewer parameters than both classical recurrent architectures, such as Long Short- Term Memory (LSTM) and Gated Recurrent Unit (GRU), and quantum variants, including Quantum LSTM (QLSTM) and Quantum GRU (QGRU). Despite its compact design, the QPRU achieves competitive forecasting performance, matching state-of-the-art baselines while offering important structural and practical advantages, including enhanced scalability and a reduced number of trainable parameters.

</details>


### [118] [On the Abundance of Critical Points of the t-SNE Energy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04379)
*Nakul Haridas,Ryan Murray*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper investigates why t-SNE produces complex, sometimes misleading visualizations by analyzing its non-convex energy landscape, revealing infinite families of critical points that explain observed artifacts like topology breaking and spurious clustering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: t-SNE is widely used but has a non-convex energy function that makes it hard to understand theoretically. Many empirical examples show it produces counterintuitive results where local minimizers don't respect the true data structure, creating misleading visualizations of topology and clustering. The paper aims to provide rigorous explanations for these phenomena.

Method: The authors analyze a general family of energies covering both original t-SNE and large-data limits. For data distributions with continuous symmetry in feature space, they construct infinite families of distinct critical points by identifying pairs of discrete symmetries (one in original space, one in target space) that are preserved under gradient dynamics. They provide both numerical examples and analytical illustrations.

Result: The main result shows that t-SNE's energy landscape contains infinite families of critical points with properties like topology breaking and spurious clustering. These critical configurations explain many empirically observed artifacts. The analysis reveals how discrete symmetries in both spaces interact through gradient dynamics to create these problematic configurations.

Conclusion: The paper provides a theoretical foundation for understanding t-SNE's complex behavior, explaining why it sometimes produces misleading visualizations. The identification of symmetry-preserving critical points offers insight into the algorithm's limitations and suggests directions for improvement in dimensionality reduction methods.

Abstract: This paper considers the energy landscape of the t-SNE algorithm. While this algorithm has enjoyed broad adoption, the non-convexity of the associated energy has made it difficult to rigorously understand what the algorithm captures in many settings. In particular, a number of well-known numerical examples, several of which are reproduced in this article, suggest a complicated energy landscape with many local minimizers that do not respect the topology or clustering structure of the underlying data. This work seeks to provide first steps towards a rigorous explanation of these phenomena. Specifically, for a general family of energies, which include both the original t-SNE algorithm and recently identified large data limits, and for densities in feature space which obey a continuous symmetry, we construct infinite families of distinct critical points. These critical points are based upon identifying pairs of discrete symmetries, one in the original feature space and the other in the target embedding space, which are preserved under gradient dynamics. These critical configurations exhibit many characteristics, such as topology breaking and spurious clustering, which are often observed empirically. Finally, numerical and analytical examples are given throughout as a means of illustrating the approach.

</details>


### [119] [Disentangling Attention in Deep Operator Learning: A Controlled Study of Data-Driven and Physics-Informed Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04407)
*Amar Alem Koric,Qibang Liu,Seid Koric*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Attention mechanisms significantly improve DeepONet accuracy for PDE solving, with cross-attention being most reliable and branch self-attention helping for complex spatial inputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While attention mechanisms have been introduced into neural operators for PDE solving, most studies change multiple architectural components simultaneously, making it difficult to identify what actually improves accuracy. There's a need for a controlled, systematic study to isolate the effects of different attention mechanisms.

Method: Systematic study of five DeepONet variants with distinct attention mechanisms (cross-attention, self-attention, tokenization, attention depth) trained under both data-driven and physics-informed regimes. Evaluated on three benchmark problems: 1D nonlinear diffusion-reaction equation, 1D viscous Burgers equation with variable initial conditions, and 2D Poisson heat-conduction with heterogeneous source fields.

Result: Per-sensor tokenization with cross-attention reduces mean relative L2 error of classical DeepONet by factors of 2.4-28.0 across all benchmarks. Best attention configurations achieve 3.5-32.3x error reduction. Cross-attention is most reliable, while branch self-attention helps primarily for large, spatially complex functional inputs. Increasing cross-attention depth improves accuracy with diminishing returns and higher physics-informed training costs.

Conclusion: Query-dependent cross-attention is the most reliable attention mechanism for improving DeepONet accuracy, whereas branch self-attention is most useful for large, spatially complex functional inputs. Tokenization combined with cross-attention provides substantial improvements, offering guidance for designing effective neural operators for PDE solving.

Abstract: Deep neural operators learn mappings between input functions and complete PDE solution fields, enabling forward evaluations of new problem instances orders of magnitude faster than conventional numerical solvers. Attention mechanisms have recently been introduced into neural operators, but most studies change several architectural components at once, making it difficult to identify what actually improves accuracy. This work presents a controlled and systematic study of five deep operator network (DeepONet) variants with distinct attention mechanisms, trained under both data-driven and physics-informed regimes, to isolate the effects of cross-attention, self-attention, tokenization, and attention depth. We evaluate them on a source-driven transient one-dimensional nonlinear diffusion-reaction equation, a transient one-dimensional viscous Burgers equation with variable initial conditions, and a two-dimensional Poisson heat-conduction problem with heterogeneous source fields. Per-sensor tokenization with cross-attention reduces the mean relative L_2 error of the classical DeepONet in all benchmark-training combinations by factors of 2.4-28.0, while the best attention configurations reach 3.5-32.3. Branch self-attention paired only with dot-product fusion is inconsistent, degrading the one-dimensional problems while helping the more complex two-dimensional source field; added on top of cross-attention it improves all six cases, though by less than cross-attention fusion alone. Global pre-mixing provides no consistent benefit. Increasing cross-attention depth further improves accuracy, but with diminishing returns and a substantially higher cost under physics-informed training. Overall, query-dependent cross-attention is the most reliable mechanism, whereas branch self-attention is most useful for large, spatially complex functional inputs.

</details>


### [120] [REFINE: LLM Refinement over Budgeted Text-Attributed Graphs for Personalized Medical Concept Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04415)
*Mohsen Nayebi Kerdabadi,Arya Hadizadeh Moghaddam,Dongjie Wang,Zijun Yao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: REFINE is a KG-aware LLM framework that creates patient-personalized medical concept representations by dynamically constructing patient-specific temporal graphs with reinforcement learning-based KG expansion, then refining concepts using GNNs and LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing medical concept encoders process concepts uniformly across patients, but a code's meaning and predictive value actually depends on patient-specific clinical context and trajectory. Learning patient-personalized concept representations from text-attributed knowledge graphs (TKGs) faces challenges in determining appropriate KG context for each code and aligning semantic information with patient-specific relational structure.

Method: REFINE starts from a global TKG and constructs patient-specific temporal graphs. It uses sequential reinforcement learning to select personalized KG expansion budgets for each observed code. The resulting patient graph is processed by a heterogeneous GNN to capture relation-aware structural dependencies, while a frozen LLM uses graph-aware soft prompts to semantically refine concept representations.

Result: Experiments on MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV show REFINE consistently improves diverse EHR backbones, outperforms strong baselines, and demonstrates robust gains across component ablation, KG selection, and data insufficiency scenarios.

Conclusion: REFINE effectively addresses the challenges of patient-personalized medical concept representation learning by combining dynamic KG construction with reinforcement learning, structural GNN processing, and semantic LLM refinement, leading to improved EHR prediction performance.

Abstract: Learning rich medical concept representations is essential for EHR prediction. Text-attributed knowledge graphs (TKGs) provide a natural foundation by organizing heterogeneous medical relations together with textual semantics. However, most existing encoders process concepts uniformly across patients, despite the fact that a code's meaning and predictive value depend on patient-specific clinical context and trajectory. Learning patient-personalized concept representations from TKGs introduces two key challenges: (1) deciding how much KG context to incorporate for each observed code, and (2) aligning semantic information with the patient-specific relational structure. We propose REFINE, a KG-aware budgeted LLM graph refinement framework for patient-personalized medical concept encoding. Starting from a global TKG, REFINE constructs patient-specific temporal graphs. A sequential reinforcement learning policy selects a personalized KG expansion budget for each observed code. The resulting patient graph is processed by a heterogeneous GNN to capture relation-aware structural dependencies, while a frozen LLM uses graph-aware soft prompts to semantically refine concept representations. Experiments on MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV show that REFINE consistently improves diverse EHR backbones, outperforms strong baselines, and demonstrates robust gains across component ablation, KG selection, and data insufficiency.

</details>


### [121] [Beyond a Universal Forecasting Selector: Demand-Conditioned Model Selection across Demand Patterns and Horizons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04425)
*Adolfo González*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: No single forecasting model selector dominates across all conditions; selector performance depends on demand type (smooth, erratic, intermittent, lumpy), data availability, and horizon length, supporting context-dependent rather than universal selection approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Forecasting-model selection is challenging in heterogeneous demand environments because the most suitable decision rule varies depending on demand structure, data availability, and forecasting horizon. The study investigates whether the selector itself should be treated as a context-dependent component rather than using a one-size-fits-all approach.

Method: Five selection mechanisms (RMSSE, ERA, OWA, CCG-AHSC, and CCG-AHSCD) were compared across 24 optimized forecasting models, nine datasets, three training-testing partitions, and horizons from 1 to 12 cycles. Performance was evaluated ex post using Global Relative Accuracy (GRA), statistical tests, and a best-attainable-model reference.

Result: No selector dominated across all conditions. CCG-AHSC and CCG-AHSCD were more competitive for smooth demand and several erratic configurations, while OWA and ERA performed better in intermittent and lumpy demand settings. Selector suitability also changed with historical data availability and forecast horizon.

Conclusion: The findings support a context-dependent rather than universal approach to forecasting-model selection, as selector performance varies significantly with demand characteristics, data availability, and forecasting horizon, making the selector itself a context-dependent component of the forecasting process.

Abstract: Forecasting-model selection remains difficult in heterogeneous demand because the most suitable decision rule may vary with demand structure, data availability, and forecasting horizon. This study examines whether the selector itself should be treated as a context-dependent component of the forecasting process. Five selection mechanisms - RMSSE, ERA, OWA, CCG-AHSC, and CCG-AHSCD - are compared across 24 optimized forecasting models, nine datasets, three training-testing partitions, and horizons from 1 to 12 cycles. Selector performance is evaluated ex post using Global Relative Accuracy (GRA), statistical tests, and a best-attainable-model reference. No selector dominates across all conditions. CCG-AHSC and CCG-AHSCD are more competitive for Smooth demand and several Erratic configurations, whereas OWA and ERA perform better in Intermittent and Lumpy settings. Selector suitability also changes with historical data availability and horizon, supporting a context-dependent rather than universal approach to forecasting-model selection.

</details>


### [122] [A Repeated-Measurement Study for Cultural Analytics of English Song Lyrics Using Five Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04428)
*E. Cho Smith,Samuel Ho,Dawn Laux*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLMs can annotate cultural texts at scale, but their reliability varies across different social constructs, requiring stability tests before treating them as valid measurements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used to annotate cultural texts at large scales, but before treating their outputs as measurements of latent social constructs, it's necessary to establish whether those measurements are reliable.

Method: Evaluated five LLMs as zero-shot annotators of four social constructs (self-esteem, self-control, seeking belonging, seeking recognition) in English song lyrics. Used repeated annotations of a large lyric corpus to examine consistency across runs, convergence across models, and transferability of consensus labels to supervised classification.

Result: LLM-based measurement reliability varies across constructs. Self-esteem shows strongest repeated-measurement reliability, seeking recognition is least stable, while self-control and seeking belonging show intermediate but model-dependent reliability. Consensus LLM labels contain learnable signal for downstream classification.

Conclusion: LLM annotations for cultural analytics require reporting repeated-measurement stability and cross-model convergence before treating them as scalable measurements. Reliability is not uniform across constructs, and transferability doesn't itself establish construct validity.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to annotate cultural texts at scales that are impractical for human coders. However, before their outputs are treated as measurements of latent social constructs, it is necessary to establish whether those measurements are reliable. This study evaluates five LLMs as zero-shot annotators of four social constructs expressed in English song lyrics: self-esteem, self-control, seeking belonging, and seeking recognition. Using repeated annotations of a large lyric corpus, we examine three properties of LLM-based measurement: consistency across repeated runs, convergence across models, and transferability of consensus labels to supervised classification. The findings show that LLM-based measurement is not uniformly reliable across constructs. Self-esteem exhibits the strongest repeated-measurement reliability across models, while seeking recognition is generally less stable; self-control and seeking belonging show intermediate but model-dependent reliability. Downstream classification further indicates that consensus LLM labels contain learnable signal, although transferability does not itself establish construct validity. Repeated-measurement stability and cross-model convergence should therefore be reported before LLM annotations are treated as scalable measurements in cultural analytics.

</details>


### [123] [Conformity Breaks Conformal Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04445)
*Yibo Hu,Hanyu Su*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Conformal prediction in multi-agent LLM systems fails under peer pressure due to "score-mechanism shift" - models change their scoring behavior when seeing unanimous wrong answers from peers, breaking calibration guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conformal prediction provides statistical guarantees for LLM outputs, but these guarantees assume the model's scoring behavior remains consistent. The paper investigates whether these guarantees hold when LLMs operate in multi-agent environments where they can be influenced by peers' answers.

Method: The researchers demonstrate "score-mechanism shift" phenomenon where LLMs change their answer scoring when exposed to unanimous wrong answers from peers, despite the same question. They evaluate this across open-weight models and multiple-choice QA tasks, measuring coverage degradation under attack scenarios.

Result: Coverage falls from calibrated 90% to 74% under unanimous-wrong peers at alpha=0.10. Attackers targeting low-confidence items can nearly halve coverage from 87% to 47% on that subgroup. The failure extends to decision layers where systems that should escalate uncertain answers instead become confident enough to act on wrong answers.

Conclusion: Conformal prediction silently breaks in multi-agent LLM systems due to score-mechanism shifts under peer pressure. Standard conformal fixes don't address this because the problem isn't changing question distributions but rather the model's scoring behavior changes when influenced by peers. This reveals a fundamental vulnerability in applying conformal certificates to peer-influenced LLM systems.

Abstract: A conformal certificate can be valid when an LLM answers alone and invalid when the same LLM sees peers that unanimously assert a wrong answer. The question is unchanged; the model's score for the correct answer changes. We call this a score-mechanism shift: clean calibration certifies how the model scores answers alone, but not how it scores them under peer pressure. We show that this shift silently breaks conformal prediction in multi-agent LLM systems. Across open-weight models and multiple-choice QA tasks, coverage falls from a calibrated 90% to 74% under unanimous-wrong peers at the standard alpha = 0.10 operating point. The average hides a sharper failure: by targeting the low-confidence items the certificate still covers, an attacker nearly halves coverage on that subgroup, from 87% to 47%, while the monitored average remains much higher. The failure also reaches the decision layer: a system that should escalate when uncertain can instead become confident enough to act on the attacker's wrong answer. Standard conformal fixes do not solve the problem, because the question distribution has not changed; the model's scoring behavior has.

</details>


### [124] [When Load-Balancing Goes Too Far: Expert Pruning in Over-Dispersed Mixture-of-Experts Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04453)
*Berkcan Kapusuzoglu,Connor Pryor,Sangwoo Cho,Supriyo Chakraborty,Shi-Xiong Zhang,Sambit Sahu,Milind Naphade*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Expert pruning in MoE models fails under over-dispersed routing regimes; proposed MESA method minimizes worst-case domain degradation when pruning load-balanced models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard expert pruning assumes router probabilities reliably indicate expert importance, but this breaks down under over-dispersed routing (aggressive load-balancing regimes) where importance signals collapse and perplexity doesn't predict downstream task accuracy.

Method: Proposed Minimax Expert Score Allocation (MESA), a domain-aware method that iteratively boosts importance scores for experts serving whichever domain is currently worst-affected, minimizing worst-case domain degradation rather than average accuracy.

Result: At 25% expert pruning, MESA achieves smallest worst-case degradation across domains, outperforming activation-aware baselines on 7 of 11 benchmarks with reduced memory footprint, and generalizes to multiple large models (gpt-oss-120B, Gemma-4-26B-A4B, OLMoE-1B-7B).

Conclusion: Over-dispersed routing represents a qualitatively distinct pruning regime where standard assumptions fail; recognizing this regime is essential for principled expert pruning of load-balanced MoE models.
