Abstract: Expert pruning reduces the memory and serving cost of Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models by removing low-importance experts identified by the router, assuming router probabilities provide a reliable importance signal. We observe that this assumption breaks down under over-dispersed routing, a regime associated with aggressive load-balancing during training, in which tokens are distributed nearly uniformly across experts and importance signals collapse. In this regime, perplexity does not predict downstream task accuracy: on gpt-oss-20B, the lowest-perplexity pruning configuration yields the worst mathematical reasoning, while the highest-perplexity configuration preserves it. This does not occur under standard routing (e.g., Mixtral-8x7B-Instruct), where perplexity and accuracy degrade together. Pruning under over-dispersed routing also exposes a capability trade-off in which no single scoring metric dominates: activation-aware scoring preserves mathematical reasoning but severely degrades knowledge-intensive science (an 18-point gap on GPQA), whereas frequency-based scoring exhibits the reverse. We propose Minimax Expert Score Allocation (MESA), a domain-aware method that iteratively boosts importance scores for experts serving whichever domain is currently worst-affected, minimizing worst-case domain degradation rather than average accuracy. At 25% expert pruning MESA achieves the smallest worst-case degradation across domains, outperforming activation-aware baselines on 7 of 11 benchmarks at a correspondingly reduced memory footprint, and it generalizes to gpt-oss-120B, Gemma-4-26B-A4B, and OLMoE-1B-7B. Our results indicate that over-dispersed routing is a qualitatively distinct pruning regime in which standard assumptions fail, and that recognizing it is a prerequisite for principled expert pruning of load-balanced MoE models.

</details>


### [125] [On-board ML for Trace Gas detection in Imaging Spectroscopy data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04458)
*Vít Růžička,Adam Chlus,Andrew Thorpe,David R. Thompson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: First demonstration of on-board methane point source detection from aerial imaging spectroscopy using edge machine learning during the Tokyo 2026 field campaign.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current imaging spectroscopy processing pipelines require slow, ground-based processing which delays detection of transient events like trace gas emissions and prevents immediate follow-up actions.

Method: Used on-board processing of AVIRIS-5 imaging spectroscopy data with an efficient, small machine learning model that runs on edge hardware, downlinking only predicted events due to communication bandwidth constraints.

Result: Successfully demonstrated the first on-board detection of methane point source emissions using edge machine learning with imaging spectroscopy data.

Conclusion: Edge ML enables real-time detection of transient events like methane emissions from aerial platforms, overcoming communication bottlenecks and enabling immediate response actions.

Abstract: Data collected during aerial and spaceborne imaging spectroscopy campaigns enables the detection of transient events such as trace gas emissions. However, current processing pipelines depend on slow, on-the-ground processing, which delays the time to information of each detected event and prohibits immediate follow-up actions. During the Tokyo Field Campaign of March 2026, we explored on-board processing of Imaging Spectroscopy data from the equipped AVIRIS-5 sensor. Due to communication bottlenecks, full datacubes cannot be downlinked immediately during the flight. Instead we downlink the potential events predicted by our efficient and small machine learning model. We show the first on-board detection of methane point source emission with Imaging Spectroscopy data using Edge ML.

</details>


### [126] [Nested Inductive Bias Framework for SPD Manifold Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04466)
*Tushar Das*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a Nested Inductive Bias framework that uses diffeomorphic compositions to pull non-Euclidean geometries onto SPD manifolds, creating curvature-aligned Riemannian classifiers that respect both matrix constraints and data relational geometry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current representation learning on SPD manifolds often uses pullback Euclidean metrics (like Log-Euclidean Metric) for computational efficiency, but these induce flat geometry that fails to capture intrinsic relational priors of datasets. While metrics like Poincaré metric are good for relational priors, generalizing them to SPD manifolds has been challenging.

Method: Proposes a Nested Inductive Bias framework using two-stage diffeomorphic composition to formally pull back non-Euclidean target geometries onto SPD manifolds. For vectorized architectures, introduces Rational Conformal Metric (RCM) to bound representation space for geometric robustness against outliers.

Result: Empirical evaluations on kinematic and signal processing benchmarks with synthetic experiments show that deep manifold networks degrade in class separability unless metric curvature aligns with intrinsic data distribution. The framework enables construction of curvature-aligned Riemannian classifiers.

Conclusion: The proposed framework successfully bridges the gap between manifold constraint enforcement and relational prior embedding on SPD manifolds, providing geometric classifiers that align with both matrix constraints and data distribution geometry, while offering improved robustness against outliers.

Abstract: In Geometric Deep Learning, inductive biases serve two primary functions: enforcing manifold constraints and embedding relational priors. Currently, representation learning on SPD manifolds frequently relies on pullback Euclidean metrics, such as the Log-Euclidean Metric, to satisfy the former. While computationally efficient in avoiding domain boundary violations, these metrics induce a flat geometry that may fail to capture the intrinsic relational priors of datasets. While metrics such as the Poincaré metric are widely utilized to induce domain-aligned relational priors, generalizing them from standard vector representations to the SPD manifold has remained a challenge. To bridge this gap, we introduce a Nested Inductive Bias framework that utilizes a two-stage diffeomorphic composition to formally pull back non-Euclidean target geometries onto the SPD manifold. This framework enables the construction of curvature-aligned Riemannian classifiers that simultaneously respect matrix constraints and the latent relational geometry of the data. Empirical evaluations on kinematic and signal processing benchmarks, together with synthetic experiments, demonstrate that deep manifold networks experience degradation in class separability unless the metric curvature aligns with the intrinsic data distribution. Furthermore, for standard vectorized architectures, we propose the Rational Conformal Metric (RCM), designed to establish state-of-the-art geometric robustness against outliers by bounding the representation space.

</details>


### [127] [Hakken: Predicting future discoveries to fill the gaps in today's knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04494)
*Tarek R. Besold,Uchenna Akujuobi,Pablo Sanchez,Alessandra Toniato,Kana Maruyama,Jihun Choi,Samy Badreddine,Frederick Gifford,Daniel Evans-Yamamoto,Sucheendra K. Palaniappan,Miquel Ferrer,Kae Nagano,Iris Rossell,Tom Joy,Hatem ElShazly,Chrysa Iliopoulou,Christoph Wehner,Thiviyan Thanapalasingam,Susana Nunes,Pedro G. Cotovio,Peter Wurman,Peter Stone,Hiroaki Kitano,Michael Spranger*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hakken is a domain-agnostic system that predicts novel scientific relationships not deducible from existing knowledge, using transformer models on temporal knowledge graphs fused with LLM knowledge, with practical validation in biomedicine where it discovered previously undocumented interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To grow scientific knowledge by establishing novel relationships beyond the deductive hull of previous knowledge, addressing the challenge of discovering undocumented connections between scientific concepts that are not obvious from existing literature.

Method: Uses transformer-based prediction models on temporal sequences of knowledge graphs extracted from research publications, fused with LLM semantic knowledge, to predict undocumented relationships. Includes a model-agnostic explanation framework to help scientists evaluate predictions.

Result: In biomedical domain: sets new benchmark for time-aware multi-label relation prediction; generated 1.5 million high-confidence aging-related hypotheses; biologists validated batches; two significant predictions confirmed: undocumented interactions between TP53 and BAMBI, and between RAF1 and TNF, with drug discovery implications.

Conclusion: Hakken successfully predicts novel scientific relationships beyond existing knowledge, demonstrates practical utility in biomedicine with validated discoveries, and shows potential for accelerating scientific discovery across domains through automated knowledge prediction with explainable AI.

Abstract: We present Hakken, a domain-agnostic prediction and explanation system performing knowledge prediction, i.e., growing scientific knowledge by establishing novel relationships, ones that are not limited to the deductive hull of previous knowledge. Hakken uses a transformer-based prediction model built on temporal sequences of knowledge graphs extracted from vast bodies of research publications, fused with an LLM's semantic knowledge, to predict the presence and define the type of as-yet undocumented relationships between scientific concepts. It then calls a model-agnostic explanation framework to provide accompanying information for each prediction that allows scientists to evaluate the suggested new relationship. While general purpose, we demonstrate Hakken's practical capabilities by applying it to the biomedical domain. There, Hakken's prediction model establishes a new benchmark for time-aware multi-label relation prediction, and we show that the model's output stays coherent and informative over extended time spans in historic data. In addition, we scored 1.5 million above-confidence-threshold hypotheses related to aging, qualitatively validated batches of these predictions with biologists and progressed three of them for empirical validation in wet-lab. Two predictions with potentially significant impact in the context of drug discovery and repurposing were confirmed, introducing previously undocumented interactions between TP53 and BAMBI, and between RAF1 and TNF, to biomedical science.

</details>


### [128] [An Energy-Based Conservative-Dissipative Latent Neural Evolution Operator for Magnetization Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04530)
*Sebastian Schaffer,Lukas Exl*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel energy-based reduced-order model for micromagnetic magnetization dynamics using convolutional autoencoders and structured latent neural ODEs that preserves the precessional-dissipative structure of the Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert equation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a more efficient reduced-order model for micromagnetic magnetization dynamics that can predict trajectories substantially cheaper than full micromagnetic solvers, while preserving the underlying physical structure of the Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert equation.

Method: Combines a convolutional autoencoder with structured latent neural ODEs where the latent vector field is generated from the gradient of a learned scalar potential through antisymmetric and symmetric positive-semidefinite operators. Training uses only latent and decoded-rollout losses without time-derivative supervision, physical-energy labels, or dissipation penalties.

Result: The antisymmetric-dissipative models provide markedly more accurate trajectory predictions on uninterrupted rollouts compared to dissipative-only models. The deep-quadratic energy formulation achieves best overall accuracy for both field directions and exhibits slower error growth when rollouts are extended beyond training horizons.

Conclusion: The energy-based reduced-order model successfully captures the essential physics of micromagnetic magnetization dynamics while enabling significantly cheaper trajectory predictions than full micromagnetic solvers, with the antisymmetric-dissipative structure being crucial for accurate long-term predictions.

Abstract: We develop an energy-based reduced-order model for micromagnetic magnetization dynamics that couples a convolutional autoencoder to a structured latent neural ordinary differential equation. Motivated by the precessional-dissipative structure of the Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert equation, the latent vector field is generated from the gradient of a learned scalar potential through an antisymmetric operator and a symmetric positive-semidefinite dissipative operator. This potential is learned in nonunique latent coordinates and is not identified with the Gibbs free energy, but decreases monotonically along autonomous continuous-time solutions, while the antisymmetric component permits motion along its level sets. The encoder, decoder, latent energy, and operators are trained jointly on short trajectory windows using latent and decoded-rollout losses alone, without time-derivative supervision, physical-energy labels, or dissipation penalties. At inference, an initial state is encoded once, evolved in latent space, and decoded only at the requested output times, enabling substantially cheaper trajectory prediction than the micromagnetic solver used to generate the training data. We compare quadratic, deep, and additive deep-quadratic latent energies on two datasets parameterized by field amplitude and generated for the two applied-field directions of the NIST $μ$MAG Standard Problem 4. Dissipative-only and antisymmetric-dissipative models achieve comparable accuracy on short training-style windows but differ substantially on uninterrupted rollouts, for which the antisymmetric-dissipative models provide markedly more accurate trajectory predictions. The deep-quadratic energy gives the best overall accuracy for both field directions and exhibits slower error growth when rollouts are extended to twice the training horizon.

</details>


### [129] [Distilled Continuous Diffusion Language Models Can Write Code in Few Steps---or One](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04531)
*Fred Zhangzhi Peng,Kaiwen Zheng,Anru R. Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PlaidQ is a 0.7B continuous diffusion language model for code generation that enables few-step or even one-step generation through aggressive distillation, surpassing traditional autoregressive and discrete diffusion approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current language generation models are sequential and slow: autoregressive models generate one token at a time, while diffusion models require many iterative refinement steps. The authors aim to enable efficient code generation with few or single-step inference by leveraging continuous diffusion's distillation capabilities.

Method: 1) Create PlaidQ - a 0.7B continuous diffusion language model for code by repurposing a pretrained autoregressive model as a bidirectional denoiser over continuous token embeddings. 2) Distill the model using two approaches: distribution matching for few-step generation and paired-trajectory supervision for one-step generation.

Result: PlaidQ is competitive with discrete diffusion models at matched scale. A 16-step distilled student achieves 31.78 and 40.49 pass@10 on HumanEval and MBPP+, outperforming the original 512-step teacher. One-step distillation reaches 7.07 pass@1 on HumanEval, generating functionally correct programs. Continuous diffusion enables significant acceleration through distillation.

Conclusion: Continuous diffusion provides a viable path for efficient code generation, enabling few-step and one-step inference through distillation. It offers an interface for language models to inherit acceleration techniques from continuous diffusion modeling, establishing a new paradigm for fast generation beyond sequential approaches.

Abstract: Language generation is almost universally treated as a sequential process: autoregressive models emit one token at a time, while diffusion language models replace token-level seriality with a long trajectory of iterative refinement. In this work, we introduce PlaidQ, a 0.7B continuous diffusion language model for code generation, and show that its trajectory can be aggressively distilled into only a few denoising steps---or even one, enabling efficient code generation. PlaidQ repurposes a pretrained autoregressive model as a bidirectional denoiser over continuous token embeddings. We distill PlaidQ with distribution matching for few-step generation and paired-trajectory supervision for one-step generation. At matched model scale, PlaidQ is competitive with discrete diffusion language models on code generation. Distillation then shifts the quality--compute frontier: a 16-step student reaches 31.78 and 40.49 pass@10 on HumanEval and MBPP+, surpassing the same PlaidQ teacher sampled for 512 steps. At the extreme, paired-trajectory distillation achieves 7.07 pass@1 on HumanEval with a single denoising step, producing functionally correct programs. Together, these results establish continuous diffusion as a viable path to few-step and one-step code generation. Broadly, continuous diffusion is not merely another representation for language: it provides an interface through which language models can inherit the acceleration and distillation machinery of continuous diffusion modeling. Training and inference code and model checkpoints are available at https://github.com/pengzhangzhi/plaidq.

</details>


### [130] [Mitra-v2 Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04540)
*Yefan Tao,Xiyuan Zhang,Xinyi Liu,Boran Han,Danielle Maddix,Haoyang Fang,Zhen Han,Jiading Gai,Xuanqing Liu,Michael Bohlke-Schneider,Yuyang,Wang,Gerald Friedland,Kevan Mah,Chris Lee,Chris Kong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Mitra-v2 is a tabular foundation model that achieves state-of-the-art performance on real-world classification and regression tasks using only synthetic data for pretraining, delivering comparable performance to larger models with just 77M parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop an efficient and powerful tabular foundation model that can handle diverse real-world classification and regression problems (credit risk, clinical prediction, equipment failure, house pricing) while being computationally efficient and trained only on synthetic data.

Method: Trained on synthetic data with a larger and more diverse pretraining distribution than Mitra-v1. Built on a small 2D Transformer backbone that supports longer contexts and larger feature spaces. Improved optimization techniques to learn from this expanded task distribution.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance on TabArena (300+ datasets) matching industry-scale TabFM and EXAONE Tabular models despite having only 77M parameters (5% of TabFM's size). Outperforms TabPFN-3 significantly in both classification and regression. Remains top-ranked on TALENT benchmark, outperforming TabPFN-3 and TabICLv2, and ranks first on multi-class classification despite pretraining limited to ≤10 classes.

Conclusion: Mitra-v2 establishes itself as one of the strongest and most broadly applicable open tabular foundation models, delivering frontier performance at a fraction of the computational cost of larger models, with full open-source release under Apache-2.0 license.

Abstract: We introduce Mitra-v2, a tabular foundation model that delivers state-of-the-art performance on real-world classification and regression problems, from credit-risk scoring and clinical prediction to equipment-failure detection and house-price estimation. Mitra-v2 is trained only on synthetic data, with a pretraining distribution that is much larger and more diverse than Mitra-v1's. Built on a small 2D Transformer backbone, Mitra-v2 supports longer contexts and larger feature spaces. Improved optimization lets it learn from this larger task distribution. We evaluate Mitra-v2 on the TabArena and TALENT benchmarks, comprising more than 300 real-world datasets under two evaluation protocols. On the full TabArena benchmark, Mitra-v2 delivers state-of-the-art performance at the level of the industry-scale TabFM and EXAONE Tabular models, while surpassing TabPFN-3 by a wide margin in both classification and regression. Mitra-v2 matches the 1.6B-parameter TabFM with only 5% of its size (77M parameters), delivering frontier performance at a fraction of the cost. On TALENT, Mitra-v2 remains among the leading models, clearly outperforming TabPFN-3 and TabICLv2. It also ranks first on classification tasks with more than ten classes, even though it was pretrained only on tasks with at most ten classes. These results make Mitra-v2 one of the strongest and most broadly applicable open tabular foundation models released to date. We release the model weights, the inference and fine-tuning code, and our evaluation results under the Apache-2.0 license.

</details>


### [131] [Fast Surrogate Modeling of Excitable and Oscillatory FitzHugh-Nagumo Dynamics with Parametric Neural Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04549)
*Andrew Franck,Justin Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Researchers train parameter-conditioned Fourier Neural Operators (FNOs) as fast, differentiable surrogates for the FitzHugh-Nagumo neuronal model, achieving high accuracy and speedup across both oscillatory and excitable firing regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The FitzHugh-Nagumo model is important for studying neuronal dynamics and neuromodulation, but traditional finite-difference solvers are computationally expensive for parameter sweeps, making rapid exploration of the 5D parameter space challenging.

Method: Train parameter-conditioned Fourier Neural Operators (FNOs) as fast surrogates for FHN voltage and recovery fields on 1D spatial domains, conditioning each Fourier layer on 5 physiological parameters via FiLM (feature-wise linear modulation). Apply bifurcation analysis to separate oscillatory and excitable regimes, training separate operators for each.

Result: In oscillatory regime: sub-0.1% relative L² error, ~1000x speedup vs finite-difference baseline, uniform generalization across parameter space, and low single-digit percentage errors outside training bounds. In excitable regime: accurately reproduces firing threshold, conduction-velocity law (c ∝ √Du), and full traveling pulses while capturing bifurcation structure.

Conclusion: FNOs serve as effective, fast, and accurate surrogates for FHN neuronal dynamics across different firing regimes, enabling rapid parameter exploration for neuromodulation and biophysical mapping while capturing essential bifurcation structures beyond simple interpolation.

Abstract: The FitzHugh-Nagumo (FHN) system serves as a simplified model of neuronal voltage dynamics, capturing the activator-inhibitor structure behind both isolated action potentials and the rhythmic spiking seen across the brain. Exploring its 5D physiological parameter space is important for neuromodulation and mapping voltage recordings back to biophysics, yet classical finite-difference solvers make rapid parameter sweeps expensive. We train parameter-conditioned Fourier Neural Operators (FNOs) as fast, differentiable surrogates for the FHN voltage and recovery fields on a one-dimensional spatial domain, conditioning each Fourier layer on the parameter vector $λ= (D_u, D_v, a, b, τ)$ via feature-wise linear modulation (FiLM). We apply a single bifurcation analysis that delimits the two distinct regimes the model spans, oscillatory (tonic firing) and excitable (action-potential propagation), and we train one operator in each. In the oscillatory regime the surrogate attains sub-$0.1\%$ relative $L^2$ error on both fields, runs nearly three orders of magnitude faster than the finite-difference baseline, generalizes uniformly across the parameter space, and extrapolates to low single-digit percentage errors outside of the training bounds. In the excitable regime the same operator accurately reproduces the firing threshold and the $c \propto \sqrt{D_u}$ conduction-velocity law and replicates full traveling pulses, fully capturing the excitable bifurcation structure rather than just smoothly interpolating fields.

</details>


### [132] [Training-Free Halving of Activated Experts in Fine-Grained Mixture-of-Experts Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04575)
*Xing Chen,Hengshuai Yao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Decoupling activation and normalization in MoE routing by separating top-k selection (k1) from probability mass normalization (k2) enables efficient expert pruning with minimal accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard MoE models couple expert selection and normalization, causing problems when reducing expert count at inference: changing k affects both which experts are used AND the strength of the expert branch due to probability renormalization.

Method: Propose separating activation (top k1 experts) from normalization (using probability mass of top k2 experts), introducing one integer hyperparameter k2 with no parameters, training, or compute overhead. This decouples expert selection from calibration.

Result: On Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, reducing from 8 to 4 experts causes only 0.35 MMLU drop (vs 4.65 drop with standard renormalization) while halving compute. Replicated on 11× larger Qwen3.5-397B-A17B with similar minimal loss. Removing renormalization entirely is catastrophic, showing suitable reference mass is crucial.

Conclusion: Expert identity matters more than weighting; MoE compression settings shouldn't be selected using unlabeled text alone (perplexity vs downstream accuracy favor different k2); balanced/domain-specialized routing leaves limited room for expert pruning.

Abstract: Modern fine-grained Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models route each token to a small number of experts and renormalize their router probabilities. We show that this renormalization implicitly calibrates expert output gain to the training top-$k$: reducing $k$ at inference changes not only which experts are used but also the strength of the expert branch. We separate these effects by activating the top $k_1$ experts while normalizing by the probability mass of the top $k_2$ experts, introducing one integer with no parameters, training, or measurable compute overhead. On Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, reducing from 8 to 4 experts causes a 4.65-point MMLU drop under standard renormalization but only 0.35 points with $k_2=16$, while halving routed-expert compute. The result replicates on the $11\times$ larger Qwen3.5-397B-A17B, where reducing from 10 to 5 experts loses only 0.55 points with an appropriate reference set. Removing renormalization entirely is catastrophic, showing that preserving a suitable reference mass is crucial. We further find that perplexity and downstream accuracy favor different $k_2$, cautioning against selecting MoE compression settings using unlabeled text alone. Analyses also show that expert identity matters substantially more than expert weighting, while balanced and domain-specialized routing leaves limited room for expert pruning.

</details>


### [133] [Optimizer Memory Schedules for Outscaling the Overtraining Axis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04577)
*Katie Everett,Shikai Qiu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Optimizer performance and optimal hyperparameters change substantially with training horizon; ADANA outscales AdamW at long horizons, while matrix-preconditioned methods provide constant advantages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how optimizers scale across the overtraining axis and how relative optimizer performance and optimal hyperparameters change with training horizon.

Method: Study four optimizers (Muon, SOAP, ADANA, AdamW) across models from 51M to 253M parameters and overtraining factors from 1x to 256x, sweeping base learning rates at every setting.

Result: Preferred learning rate schedule can reverse across overtraining axis; best weight decay scales as sqrt(OT); longer horizons favor longer fixed memory; ADANA outscales AdamW with exponent advantage; Muon and SOAP provide roughly constant token-efficiency advantages; ADANA surpasses Muon and becomes competitive with SOAP at highest OT factors.

Conclusion: Training horizon is an essential axis for optimizer evaluation and design, with different optimizers showing varying scaling behaviors across the overtraining spectrum.

Abstract: We investigate how optimizers scale across the overtraining axis and show that relative optimizer performance and optimal hyperparameters change substantially with training horizon. In particular, we study how matrix-preconditioned methods (Muon and SOAP) and a momentum-scheduled method (ADANA) scale relative to AdamW. We compare these four optimizers across models from 51M to 253M parameters and overtraining (OT) factors from 1x to 256x, sweeping the base learning rate at every setting. The preferred learning rate schedule can reverse across the overtraining axis, the best weight decay coefficient scales approximately as sqrt(OT), and longer horizons generally favor longer fixed memory. ADANA's scaling advantage over AdamW persists after tuning AdamW's fixed memory separately at each horizon. Log-time weight decay and momentum cooldown provide substantial gains for ADANA that compound as training increases. With this treatment, ADANA outscales AdamW with an exponent advantage close to that predicted by DANA theory on power-law random features. Muon and SOAP instead provide roughly constant token-efficiency advantages over AdamW across most of the measured range, although SOAP may gain further at the highest overtraining factors. ADANA begins behind both matrix-preconditioned optimizers but closes the gaps as training increases, surpassing Muon and becoming competitive with SOAP at our highest OT factors. These results establish training horizon as an essential axis for optimizer evaluation and design.

</details>


### [134] [Representation Redundancy and Structural Complexity in Finite-Field Inversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04583)
*Zheng Zhang,Na Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper shows that different ordered bases for field inversion in F_{2^n} lead to different Boolean inversion maps unless they are in the same Galois orbit, and that these different representations affect both algebraic complexity and neural network learning difficulty.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how the choice of mathematical representation affects both algebraic properties and empirical learning difficulty for inversion operations over finite fields.

Method: Theoretical analysis of ordered bases and Galois orbits for inversion maps in F_{2^n}, examination of three Boolean formulations with different algebraic degrees, exhaustive computations, and controlled experiments with multilayer perceptrons.

Result: Proved that two ordered bases give the same inversion map only if in the same Galois orbit (n-to-one correspondence), established algebraic degrees for three Boolean formulations, found computational agreement, and showed neural networks exhibit same ordering in learning difficulty.

Conclusion: Exact redundancy in representations can coexist with changes in Boolean structure and learning behavior, with Galois orbit redundancy providing only limited generalization benefit under tested conditions.

Abstract: The representation chosen for a mathematical operation can affect both its algebraic form and its empirical learning difficulty. We study this phenomenon for inversion over \(\mathbb F_{2^n}\), with field elements expressed in varying ordered \(\mathbb F_2\)-bases. We prove that two ordered bases induce the same coordinate inversion map if and only if they belong to the same Galois orbit. Since every orbit has size \(n\), the correspondence between ordered bases and distinct inversion maps is exactly \(n\)-to-one. We then analyze three Boolean formulations of inversion. The reference formulation has algebraic degree \(n-1\) and joint ANF leap \(1\), the mixed representation formulation has degree \(2(n-1)\) and joint ANF leap \(2\), and the complete raw formulation has degree at most \(3(n-1)\) and joint ANF leap at least \(n\). Exhaustive computations agree with the theoretical results and bounds in the cases considered. Controlled experiments with multilayer perceptrons show the same ordering in learning difficulty, while Galois orbit redundancy provides only a limited generalization benefit under the tested conditions. These results show that exact redundancy among representations can coexist with changes in Boolean structure and learning behavior when the representation is exposed as part of the input.

</details>


### [135] [GNN-Guided Graph Coarsening and Adaptive QUBO Penalties for the Capacitated Vehicle Routing Problem with Time Windows on a Quantum Annealer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04593)
*Youssef Kamel Rezk,Paweł Gora*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Graph coarsening reduces QUBO problem size for quantum annealing of CVRPTW by merging customers, with adaptive penalty calibration and GNN-based merging improving feasibility significantly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph coarsening heuristics for QUBO formulations in vehicle routing problems require family-specific tuning and remain unreliable on random instances, creating limitations for quantum annealing solutions.

Method: Three key innovations: 1) Adaptive penalty calibration including controlling coefficient range, removing non-binding constraints, and normalizing/scaling binding ones; 2) Replacing hand-tuned merge scores with graph neural networks for customer merging; 3) Hardware experiments on D-Wave Advantage2 processor to validate approach.

Result: Substantial improvements: Penalty calibration reduced mean constraint violations from 33.0 to 0.06; GNN achieved 100% feasibility at N=10 across Solomon families (vs 80% for heuristic); Overall feasibility improved from 69% to 83%; Hardware experiments showed feasible samples increased from 0.02% to 39%.

Conclusion: Adaptive penalty conditioning and GNN-based coarsening significantly improve quantum annealing performance for CVRPTW, enabling reliable solutions with 5-6x reduced QUBO size while maintaining high feasibility rates across problem families without family-specific tuning.

Abstract: Graph coarsening reduces the large Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO) formulations arising when vehicle-routing problems are solved by quantum annealing. Nearby customers with compatible time windows are merged into super-nodes, the reduced problem is solved, and the solution is expanded to the original graph. For the Capacitated Vehicle Routing Problem with Time Windows (CVRPTW), existing coarsening heuristics require family-specific tuning and remain unreliable on random instances. We address these limitations on the Solomon benchmark using simulated annealing and a D-Wave Advantage2 processor.
  We first introduce adaptive penalty calibration. Uniform penalty scaling has little effect, whereas controlling the internal coefficient range substantially improves raw samples. Removing non-binding constraints, normalising binding ones, and scaling the remaining penalties reduces mean raw constraint violations from 33.0 to 0.06 at the same solver budget (p=3.7e-11, n=56). A variable-count-preserving control attributes this gain to conditioning rather than problem size.
  Second, we replace the hand-tuned merge score with a graph neural network (GNN) using one configuration across all families. At N=10, it achieves 100% feasibility across all Solomon families, including R-type (100% vs. 80% for the tuned heuristic). Across N=10,...,100, feasibility is 83% vs. 69%, with the GNN better or tied on 85/90 instance-size pairs. At N=80,100, the difference is significant (p=0.002; 25/25 pairs), while the QUBO remains approximately 5-6 times smaller.
  Finally, hardware experiments reproduce the conditioning effect at fixed logical variable count: feasible samples increase from 0.02% to 39% across 13 instances. Classical repair with local search remains a reference bound for end-to-end solution cost.

</details>


### [136] [Too Rare to Learn: Prescribed Cyclone Tracks Degrade a Bay of Bengal Ocean Emulator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04635)
*Sumaiya Islam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Neural ocean emulators that include prescribed cyclone inputs perform worse than ocean-only models because cyclone inputs are rare training events, causing the model to learn confidently wrong responses when cyclones actually occur.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether neural ocean emulators for regional forecasting in cyclone-exposed coastal seas should include prescribed cyclone inputs, as this seems like a natural design choice for cyclone prediction.

Method: Tested two identical U-Net architectures in the Bay of Bengal - one with four prescribed cyclone-track channels and one ocean-only. Withheld 15 whole cyclones from GLORYS12 reanalysis. Compared performance across three random seeds, analyzing why cyclone inputs hurt performance despite seeming like logical inputs.

Result: Ocean-only model consistently beat persistence forecasts, while storm-conditioned model consistently lost to persistence. The skill ranges were completely disjoint (p = 3.1e-5). Extra error occurred inside prescribed storm footprints. Remarkably, replacing real cyclone maps with no-storm maps at inference improved held-out storm forecasts by 7.5-16.4%.

Conclusion: Including prescribed cyclone inputs harms neural ocean emulator performance because cyclone channels are non-zero on only 7.9% of training days, making them out-of-distribution when activated. The conditioned network learns a confidently wrong response to rare cyclone signals, demonstrating that what seems like a logical design choice can actually degrade forecasting skill.

Abstract: Neural ocean emulators are being proposed for regional forecasting in cyclone-exposed coastal seas, and a natural design choice is to hand the network the cyclone as a prescribed input. We test that choice in the Bay of Bengal and find it harmful. We withhold 15 whole cyclones spanning 65 to 150 kt from GLORYS12 reanalysis and compare two U-Nets that are identical except for four prescribed cyclone-track channels. Across three seeds the ocean-only model beats persistence in every run and the storm-conditioned model loses to it in every run, with the two skill ranges disjoint (p = 3.1e-5, paired across storms). The cause is exposure frequency rather than signal content: the channels are non-zero on only 7.9% of training days, so they are out of distribution the moment they activate. The extra error falls inside the prescribed storm footprint, and replacing the real cyclone map with a no-storm map at inference improves held-out storm forecasts by 7.5 to 16.4% in every seed. The conditioned network has learned a response to a rare signal that is confidently wrong.

</details>


### [137] [SMILE: Bridging Continuous Optimization and Discrete Symbolic Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04639)
*Mansooreh Montazerin,Antonio Ortega,Ajitesh Srivastava*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SMILE is a hybrid symbolic regression framework combining continuous gradient optimization with discrete symbolic recovery to efficiently discover interpretable mathematical expressions from data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing symbolic regression methods suffer from slow convergence in combinatorial search spaces and lack mechanisms to exploit the compositional structure present in data.

Method: Three-stage approach: 1) structural analysis to identify compositional hierarchy, 2) continuous optimization with interpretable activations, 3) symbolic recovery through structured pruning, coefficient optimization, and rounding.

Result: SMILE achieves highest symbolic solution rate at largest noise levels, lies on Pareto front of accuracy vs complexity, recovers simpler expressions faster than competing methods on SRBench.

Conclusion: SMILE successfully bridges continuous optimization with symbolic recovery, offering robust, efficient, and interpretable symbolic regression that handles noise well while maintaining simplicity.

Abstract: Symbolic regression (SR) discovers closed-form mathematical expressions from data, offering interpretability beyond black-box models. Existing methods suffer from slow convergence in combinatorial search spaces and lack mechanisms to exploit compositional structure in the data. We introduce SMILE (Sine, Multiplication, Identity, Logarithm, Exponential), a hybrid framework that unifies continuous gradient-based optimization with discrete symbolic recovery through three stages: structural analysis of the data to identify the compositional hierarchy of the target expression, continuous optimization to learn parameters of a network that encodes the target expression using interpretable activations, and symbolic recovery through structured pruning, coefficient optimization, and rounding. This final stage distills the learned network into a compact expression with exact symbolic constants. We evaluate SMILE on SRBench across ground-truth and black-box datasets, with ablation studies validating each component. SMILE achieves the highest symbolic solution rate at the largest noise levels, demonstrating strong robustness where competing methods degrade substantially. It consistently lies on the Pareto front of accuracy versus complexity, recovering significantly simpler expressions in a fraction of the time required by the competing methods.

</details>


### [138] [Interpretability for Turing Machines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04661)
*Billy Snikkers,Rumi Salazar,Daniel Murfet,Will Troiani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Susceptibility analysis reveals algorithmic structure in Turing machines by examining their local loss landscapes, identifying symmetries and path separation through permutation symmetries and low-rank blocks in susceptibility matrices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to bridge interpretability techniques from neural networks with algorithmic analysis, specifically exploring whether interpretability methods can detect algorithmic structure in computational models like Turing machines and finite automata.

Method: The authors apply susceptibility analysis (an interpretability technique) to the learning problem for noisy Turing machines introduced by Murfet and Troiani. They theoretically prove connections between algorithmic symmetries/path separation and properties of susceptibility matrices, then empirically validate on deterministic finite automata using PCA and clustering methods.

Result: Theoretical results show that algorithmic symmetries and path separation induce specific patterns (permutation symmetries and low-rank blocks) in susceptibility matrices. Empirical results demonstrate that algorithmic features of deterministic finite automata can be recovered through principal component analysis and clustering in susceptibility space.

Conclusion: Susceptibility analysis is an effective interpretability tool for revealing algorithmic structure in computational models, providing a bridge between machine learning interpretability techniques and theoretical computer science that enables detection of algorithmic properties through mathematical patterns in learning landscapes.

Abstract: We show that susceptibilities, an interpretability technique developed for neural networks, can identify the presence of algorithmic structure in Turing machines by probing the local loss landscape of a learning problem for noisy Turing machines introduced by Murfet and Troiani (arXiv:2504.08075). We prove that symmetries and path separation in the algorithm implemented by a Turing machine induce permutation symmetries and low-rank blocks in its susceptibility matrix. We study this empirically on a set of deterministic finite automata (DFAs) and demonstrate that algorithmic features can be recovered by principal component analysis and clustering methods in susceptibility space.

</details>


### [139] [WEECFP-SuRGE: Wide Embedded Extended Connectivity Fingerprint with Substructure Rotary Graph-distance Encoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04672)
*Robert Epps*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: WEECFP is a new continuous molecular fingerprint with transformer architecture (WEECFP-SuRGE) that achieves state-of-the-art performance on ADMET prediction without external pretraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a need for better molecular representation methods that can achieve competitive ADMET prediction performance without relying on extensive pretraining or external data.

Method: WEECFP creates 1024-dimensional continuous fingerprints that scatter Morgan substructures across positions. WEECFP-SuRGE uses a transformer with SuRGE encoding that applies RoPE-like rotations parameterized by molecular shortest-path graph distance.

Result: The method achieves top positions on TDC ADMET leaderboard (#2 overall, #1 among non-pretrained methods), wins multiple benchmark tasks, and demonstrates near-lossless reconstruction (99.9% of in-distribution SMILES recovered).

Conclusion: WEECFP with SuRGE encoding provides an effective parameter-free molecular representation that competes with pretrained methods while offering high-fidelity reconstruction and efficient positional encoding.

Abstract: We introduce WEECFP, a parameter-free 1024-dimensional continuous molecular fingerprint that scatters each Morgan substructure across roughly thirty-two signed positions of a single vector, and WEECFP-SuRGE, a transformer architecture whose self-attention applies SuRGE (Substructure Rotary Graph-distance Encoding) -- a RoPE-like rotation parameterized by molecular shortest-path graph distance -- to WEECFP substructure tokens. A 7-model blend of this architecture (the WEECFP-SuRGE Blend) achieves the lowest average regression rank on the TDC ADMET leaderboard; is #2 overall on the TDC ADMET leaderboard (behind only pretrained MapLight+GNN), and is #1 overall among methods that use no external pretraining; takes leaderboard #1 finishes on Pgp, Lipophilicity, CYP2D6 Substrate, Clearance Microsome, and LD50 (with the WEECFP-NoSuRGE Blend separately reaching #1 on HIA) across the full 22-benchmark suite -- without any external pretraining. On MoleculeNet, WEECFP-SuRGE beats every classical-fingerprint baseline on 3 of 4 regression tasks (ESOL, Lipophilicity, QM9). We further show that WEECFP tokenization is near-lossless: a greedy overlap reconstruction recovers the exact canonical SMILES of 99.9% of in-distribution molecules across 9 MoleculeNet datasets and 98.93% of molecules in a cross-dataset holdout (HIV->Lipophilicity), and that a three-reference farthest-first encoding of graph distance correlates at Pearson r = 0.901 with the true pairwise distance, enabling O(S) positional memory at matching accuracy.

</details>


### [140] [Simulation-free Unbalanced Dynamic Optimal Transport with General Growth Penalty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04710)
*Junda Ying,Yuxuan Wang,Bowen Yang,Peijie Zhou,Lei Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SUDO introduces a simulation-free framework for unbalanced dynamic optimal transport that supports general convex growth penalties, enabling efficient trajectory inference from unpaired single-cell snapshots beyond the limitations of existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inferring cellular dynamics from unpaired single-cell snapshots requires modeling both state transitions and population growth/death. Current unbalanced dynamic optimal transport (UDOT) methods are limited: they either rely on computationally expensive NeuralODE simulations or only work with quadratic penalties (Wasserstein-Fisher-Rao geodesics), restricting their efficiency and applicability.

Method: SUDO (Simulation-free Unbalanced Dynamic Optimal transport) is a simulation-free framework that works with general non-quadratic convex growth penalties. It learns conditional paths and transport costs, solves the induced semi-coupling problem, and leverages unbalanced flow matching to achieve simulation-free solutions.

Result: On WFR benchmarks, SUDO matches the accuracy of efficient analytical solution-driven algorithms while outperforming simulation-based methods in computational speed. Beyond WFR, SUDO supports asymmetric penalties that encode proliferation-dominant priors and produces more plausible trajectories and growth estimates on synthetic and single-cell datasets.

Conclusion: SUDO enables efficient UDOT with general growth penalties, overcoming limitations of existing methods. The approach provides a flexible framework for inferring cellular dynamics from unpaired single-cell data while supporting biological priors on proliferation and apoptosis through asymmetric penalty structures.

Abstract: Inferring cellular dynamics from unpaired single-cell snapshots requires modeling both state transitions and population growth or death. Unbalanced dynamic optimal transport (UDOT) addresses this by penalizing growth along transport paths, making the choice of growth penalty a key way to encode biological priors on proliferation and apoptosis. However, existing UDOT solvers either rely on computationally expensive NeuralODE simulations or depend on analytical solutions of conditional paths, restricting their efficiency solely to quadratic penalties, i.e. Wasserstein-Fisher-Rao (WFR) geodesics. To enable an efficient UDOT solver for general growth penalties, we first show that concave growth penalties lead to degenerate solutions where growth and transport are separated. We then introduce \textbf{S}imulation-free \textbf{U}nbalanced \textbf{D}ynamic \textbf{O}ptimal transport (SUDO), a simulation-free framework for UDOT with general non-quadratic convex growth penalties. SUDO learns the conditional paths and transport costs, solves the induced semi-coupling problem, and subsequently leverages unbalanced flow matching to achieve a simulation-free solution. On WFR benchmarks, SUDO matches the accuracy of efficient, analytical solution-driven algorithms while outperforming simulation-based methods in computational speed. Beyond WFR, SUDO supports asymmetric penalties that encode proliferation-dominant priors and produce more plausible trajectories and growth estimates on synthetic and single-cell datasets.

</details>


### [141] [Locating and Steering Refusal Beyond Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04721)
*Preethi Carmel Bosco,Gopalakrishnan Srinivasan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Refusal representations transfer across LLM architectures - a single direction in vector space aligns refusal behavior between transformers and state-space models, enabling cross-architecture safety tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand if safety representations (specifically refusal mechanisms) transfer across different neural network architectures, or if they must be rediscovered for each new architecture type.

Method: The paper examines how refusal mechanisms work in transformers vs. state-space models (SSMs), uses rigid rotation to align representation spaces between architectures, tests harm probes trained on transformers on SSMs, and develops detector-triggered gating interventions.

Result: The refusal representation transfers across architectures - a single rigid rotation aligns transformer and SSM refusal spaces. Harm probes trained on transformers work on SSMs, and removing the aligned direction makes models answer harmful queries they would normally refuse. The key insight is that where the direction is read (at the write site) matters more than where it's applied.

Conclusion: Safety tooling based on refusal representations can be ported to new architectures by re-estimating the direction at that architecture's write site rather than rebuilding it from scratch, making safety tools more transferable across different neural network architectures.

Abstract: Where inside a language model does refusal live, and does that place change when the architecture does? In a transformer, refusal is governed by a single direction in the residual stream, a finding that safety and interpretability tooling now depend on. State-space models (SSMs) route information through a recurrent update instead of attention, sharing no token-mixing mechanism with a transformer. Does the same safety representation survive this shift, or must it be rediscovered per architecture? It survives. A single rigid rotation, which can only reorient a space and not reshape it, aligns one model's representation space with another's, so the two genuinely share the representation. A harm probe trained on a transformer then flags an SSM's harmful inputs, and removing the aligned direction makes a model answer attacks it would otherwise refuse, while a random direction of the same size does far less. What is architecture-specific is not where the direction is steered but where it must be read. Each layer computes a fresh output that is then added into the residual stream, and harm is cleanly readable at this output, the write site, before the addition. A control that holds the intervention's strength fixed shows that what matters is where the direction is estimated, not where it is applied. Applied through a detector-triggered gate, this direction lowers jailbreak success in all four architecture families we test (SSM, transformer, recurrent, hybrid), and on the SSM it holds against an attacker that tunes its prompt against the defense. The gate only matches a trivial rule that returns a fixed refusal whenever the same detector fires, so what transfers across architectures is the direction itself, not defense strength. Safety tooling built on refusal therefore ports to a new architecture by re-estimating the direction at that architecture's write site, not by rebuilding it.

</details>


### [142] [Training Large Language Models for Small-Molecule Design with Synthetic Task Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04735)
*Frank Hu,Shriram Chennakesavalu,Zichen Wang,Patricia Suriana,Bodhi Vani,Kirill Shmilovich,Kangway Chuang,Colin Grambow*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLMs can be trained on cheap synthetic molecular design tasks using curriculum learning, enabling them to generalize to expensive real-world drug lead optimization without needing direct training on costly experimental evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Drug discovery involves searching vast chemical spaces with multiple competing objectives, and while LLMs offer strong generative priors, directly training them on expensive experimental evaluations (taking hours or days per evaluation) is prohibitively costly.

Method: Use curriculum-based training that gradually introduces more challenging synthetic molecular design tasks to train LLMs, allowing them to learn strategies that generalize to expensive real-world lead optimization scenarios without direct training on costly evaluations.

Result: Curriculum-trained LLMs achieve strong performance that surpasses much larger frontier models on structure-based lead optimization tasks, demonstrating effective generalization from cheap synthetic tasks to expensive experimental scenarios.

Conclusion: Scaling post-training using synthetic tasks is an effective strategy for adapting LLMs to high-cost experimental scenarios in drug discovery, providing a practical solution to the cost barriers of direct training on expensive evaluations.

Abstract: Designing viable drug candidates requires searching a combinatorially large and rugged chemical space for molecules that satisfy multiple, often competing, objectives. Large language models (LLMs) provide a useful generative prior for this problem because of their representational capacity, reasoning ability, and flexibility when incorporating information from the external environment. While reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR) can be used to improve the capabilities of LLMs, many chemically relevant scoring functions require hours or even days per evaluation, making them prohibitively expensive to use directly during online training. Here, we investigate whether LLMs can learn molecular design strategies from cheaper synthetic tasks that generalize to expensive molecular lead optimization settings. We find that curriculum-based training recipes that gradually incorporate more challenging synthetic design tasks enable strong performance that surpasses that of much larger frontier models on structure-based lead optimization. Our results suggest that scaling post-training using synthetic tasks is an effective strategy for adapting LLMs to high-cost experimental scenarios that are too expensive to directly train on.

</details>


### [143] [A Fairness Audit of the Duckworth-Lewis-Stern Method: Format-Specific and Gender-Differential Bias, with an Interpretable Calibration Layer for Cricket Target Revision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04754)
*Soumyadeep Roy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper conducts the first large-scale empirical audit of the DLS method in cricket, revealing significant systematic biases including gender-based differential biases, and proposes DLS-Cal, a lightweight calibration layer that substantially reduces prediction errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite over two decades of use as the international standard for target revision in rain-interrupted cricket matches, the Duckworth-Lewis-Stern (DLS) method has never undergone a large-scale empirical audit of its prediction bias. There's a need to systematically evaluate its accuracy and fairness across different match scenarios.

Method: The researchers conducted an audit on 8,150 international matches (3,095 ODIs, 5,055 T20Is) from Cricsheet, generating 233,550 synthetic interruption scenarios with temporal splits. They identified structured biases in DLS predictions and benchmarked it against five modern machine learning alternatives (Bi-LSTM, XGBoost, enriched XGBoost, deep context-aware model, and stacking ensemble). They then proposed DLS-Cal, a lightweight interpretable calibration layer with 27K parameters that outputs state-conditioned corrections to DLS predictions.

Result: Two major structured biases were documented: 1) DLS prediction error spans a 137-run range across match-state buckets (overs-remaining, wickets-lost), and 2) a previously unquantified gender-differential bias in ODIs with mean over-prediction of +1.51 runs for men but +7.63 runs for women. DLS-Cal reduced absolute bias by 31% on ODIs and 19% on T20Is, and a gender-aware variant reduced women's ODI residual bias from +6.19 to +0.65 runs while leaving men's calibration unchanged.

Conclusion: The DLS method exhibits significant systematic biases that had not been previously quantified, particularly concerning gender fairness. The proposed DLS-Cal calibration layer provides a lightweight, interpretable solution that substantially improves prediction accuracy and fairness while maintaining the transparency of the established DLS method. The findings call for systematic auditing of sports analytics algorithms and demonstrate how machine learning can enhance traditional methods without replacing them.

Abstract: The Duckworth-Lewis-Stern (DLS) method has been the international standard for revising target scores in rain-interrupted limited-overs cricket since 1999. Despite over two decades of operational use, no large-scale empirical audit of its prediction bias has been published. We conduct such an audit on 8,150 international matches (3,095 ODIs, 5,055 T20Is) from Cricsheet, generating 233,550 synthetic interruption scenarios with temporal splits. We document two structured biases. First, DLS prediction error spans a 137-run range across (overs-remaining, wickets-lost) match-state buckets. Second, DLS exhibits a gender-differential bias on ODIs that has not previously been quantified: on the training split, mean over-prediction is +1.51 runs for men but +7.63 runs for women, a gap of +6.13 runs (F = 195.16, p < 10^-43). We benchmark DLS against five modern alternatives: Bi-LSTM, XGBoost, an enriched XGBoost variant, a deep context-aware model, and a stacking ensemble, and propose DLS-Cal, a lightweight interpretable calibration layer (27K parameters) outputting a state-conditioned correction added to DLS. DLS-Cal reduces absolute bias by 31% on ODI and 19% on T20I, and a gender-aware variant reduces women's ODI residual bias from +6.19 to +0.65 runs while leaving men's calibration unchanged. We release code, models, and data.

</details>


### [144] [Resilience Beyond Stationary Client Unavailability: Unlocking Efficient and Unbiased Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04763)
*Ming Xiang,Stratis Ioannidis,Edmund Yeh,Carlee Joe-Wong,Lili Su*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedSWE is a federated learning algorithm designed to handle intermittent client availability in dynamic environments without needing prior knowledge of client participation patterns, introducing minimal overhead while maintaining convergence guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In real-world federated learning, clients (edge devices) often have intermittent availability due to resource constraints and uncertainties. Existing algorithms struggle with non-stationary client availability dynamics, which can introduce bias and harm model performance, while prior solutions either ignore these dynamics or impose heavy computational/memory overhead.

Method: FedSWE (Federated Smooth Weighted Evolution) introduces novel algorithmic structures with three key components: (1) compensation for missed client computations, (2) stabilization and diffusion of global updates across training rounds, and (3) even mixing of local updates through implicit gossiping. The algorithm operates without prior knowledge of non-stationary availability patterns while maintaining low memory and computation overhead compared to FedAvg.

Result: Theoretical analysis shows FedSWE converges to a stationary point for non-convex objectives and achieves linear speedup in certain cases. Numerical experiments on real-world datasets with diverse client unavailability patterns demonstrate the algorithm's effectiveness in handling heterogeneous and non-stationary stochastic client availability.

Conclusion: FedSWE provides an efficient solution for federated learning in highly dynamic environments with intermittent client availability, offering provable resilience to non-stationary availability patterns while maintaining low computational overhead compared to standard approaches.

Abstract: Due to resource constraints or external and internal uncertainties, clients in real-world federated learning systems are often intermittently available edge devices. In highly dynamic environments, the parameter server lacks prior real-time knowledge of clients' availability, making it challenging to adapt traditional federated learning algorithms to be resilient to uncertainties in client availability. If not carefully addressed, complex client availability can introduce significant bias, potentially harming the performance of the trained model. Most prior work either fails to account for non-stationary client availability dynamics or demands significant memory and computational overhead. This paper aims to develop efficient federated learning algorithms that are provably resilient to heterogeneous and non-stationary stochastic client availability. We propose FedSWE, which admits novel algorithmic structures to (i) compensate for missed computations, (ii) stabilize and diffuse the global updates over rounds, and (iii) evenly mix the local updates through implicit gossiping, despite being agnostic to non-stationary dynamics. Compared with the standard FedAvg, FedSWE introduces light additional memory and computation overhead. We show that FedSWE converges to a stationary point of non-convex objectives while achieving the desired linear speedup property in certain special cases. We corroborate our analysis with numerical experiments over diversified client unavailability dynamics on real-world data sets.

</details>


### [145] [A Robust Watermark-based Fingerprint Framework for GNNs Ownership Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04772)
*Han Zhang,Yan Wang,Guanfeng Liu,Pengfei Ding,Huaxiong Wang,Kwok-Yan Lam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: REMARK is a robust watermark-based fingerprint framework for GNNs that addresses limitations in existing ownership verification methods by generating in-distribution watermark graphs and extracting fingerprints from output differences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The high training cost of GNNs raises concerns about model ownership infringement (model stealing, unauthorized misuse). Existing GNN ownership verification methods face three limitations: 1) performance degradation from OOD watermark graphs, 2) unrealistic assumption that surrogate models use watermark-containing training sets, and 3) over-reliance on specific output levels for fingerprint extraction.

Method: REMARK generates carefully crafted in-distribution watermark graphs that maximize output differences between GNN models, mitigating OOD-induced performance degradation. It then extracts robust fingerprints from these output differences for ownership verification, removing the need for surrogate models to be trained on watermark-containing datasets or expose specific output levels.

Result: Extensive experiments across widely used real-world datasets and GNN architectures demonstrate that REMARK achieves state-of-the-art ownership verification accuracy and robustness while preserving the utility of protected models.

Conclusion: REMARK provides an effective solution for GNN ownership verification by addressing key limitations of existing methods through in-distribution watermark generation and robust fingerprint extraction from model output differences.

Abstract: The high training cost of Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) has raised growing concerns regarding model ownership infringement, such as model stealing and unauthorized misuse. To verify model ownership and prevent significant economic losses, two groups of GNN Ownership Verification (OV) methods have been proposed: watermark-based methods and fingerprint-based methods. However, these methods typically face three limitations: (1) the performance degradation of protected models caused by out-of-distribution (OOD) watermark graphs with respect to the training set; (2) the unrealistic assumption that surrogate models have been trained on a watermark-containing training set; and (3) over-reliance on specific output levels for fingerprint extraction. In this paper, we propose a Robust watErMArk-based fingeRprint frameworK for GNNs, named REMARK. REMARK first generates carefully crafted in-distribution watermark graphs that maximize output differences between GNN models, thus mitigating OOD-induced performance degradation. REMARK then extracts robust fingerprints from these output differences to verify GNN ownership, thereby removing the assumptions that surrogate models must be trained on a watermark-containing dataset or expose specific output levels. Extensive experiments across widely used real-world datasets and GNN architectures demonstrate that REMARK achieves state-of-the-art OV accuracy and robustness while preserving the utility of protected models.

</details>


### [146] [Persistent Teacher Anchoring for Tool-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04773)
*Hyun Bin Park,Kyungho Song,Sangmin Lee,Du-Seong Chang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Persistent Teacher Anchoring (PTA) improves LLM tool-use distillation by ensuring teacher verification of entire tool-call turns before execution, preventing error accumulation in student rollouts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In tool-use scenarios, standard on-policy knowledge distillation suffers from teacher-student distribution gap accumulation when student rollouts enter states the teacher wouldn't visit. Since tool calls execute before teacher supervision and their observations affect later prefixes, this drift becomes particularly problematic for tool-using LLMs.

Method: Proposes Persistent Teacher Anchoring (PTA): 1) Turn-level commitment - calls only reach environment after teacher verifies entire turn, 2) Treats verified chunks as atomic generation units, 3) Introduces persistent lookahead that fills idle rollout capacity by advancing future samples and carrying unfinished ones across student updates under fixed verifier.

Result: PTA improves macro best@4 by 2.5 and 2.8 points over standard OPKD in Search-R1-style retrieval and DeepEyes-style perception RL. Persistent lookahead improves throughput by 24%.

Conclusion: PTA effectively addresses distribution drift in tool-use distillation by ensuring teacher commits to entire tool-call turns before execution, improving both performance and efficiency in downstream RL applications.

Abstract: Distillation is common in LLM post-training, where on-policy knowledge distillation (OPKD) uses student-generated trajectories to prepare the student for downstream RL. At each state, the student matches a next-token distribution supplied by the teacher. As the rollout enters states the teacher would not visit, the teacher-student distribution gap can accumulate. In tool use, this gap becomes consequential because student-written calls execute before supervision and their observations shape later prefixes. Proposer-verifier generation addresses this drift by letting the teacher decide which student-proposed text is retained during generation. Existing formulations govern text but leave tool execution outside their scope. We propose Persistent Teacher Anchoring (PTA), a student-induced but teacher-committed rollout construction. PTA retains chunk-level verification and adds turn-level commitment, allowing a call to reach the environment only after the teacher has verified the entire turn. Treating verified chunks as atomic generation units, we introduce persistent lookahead, which fills idle rollout capacity by advancing future samples and carrying unfinished ones across student updates under the fixed verifier. Across Search-R1-style retrieval and DeepEyes-style perception RL, applying PTA before downstream RL improves macro best@4 by 2.5 and 2.8 points over OPKD under the same downstream RL budget, while lookahead improves throughput by 24%.

</details>


### [147] [Dynamic Heterogeneous Graph Representation Learning: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04779)
*Huan Liu,Pengfei Jiao,Jie Yin,Hongjiang Chen,Zhidong Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A comprehensive survey on Dynamic Heterogeneous Graph (DHG) representation learning methods, introducing a unified definition, proposing a novel taxonomy, and discussing applications and future directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world AI systems are evolving heterogeneous entities with complex interactions, posing challenges to static or homogeneous modeling approaches. There's a need to systematically review and categorize the emerging field of DHG representation learning.

Method: The survey introduces a unified formal definition for DHGs from temporal granularity perspective, proposes an algorithm-centric taxonomy categorizing methods into embedding-based, GNN-based, and Transformer-based approaches, and explicitly highlights their intrinsic modeling biases.

Result: First systematic review of DHG representation learning, including comprehensive categorization of existing literature, summary of representative applications, commonly used datasets/benchmarks, and identification of modeling biases related to dynamic granularity.

Conclusion: DHG representation learning is vital for modeling real-world complex systems, and the survey provides a foundation for future research by establishing a unified framework, taxonomy, and identifying promising research directions in this evolving field.

Abstract: Graph representation learning (GRL) serves as a canonical paradigm for modeling complex networks. However, real-world AI systems inherently manifest as evolving heterogeneous entities with complex interactions, posing significant challenges to static or homogeneous modeling. To address these complexities, representation learning for Dynamic Heterogeneous Graphs (DHGs) has emerged as a vital approach for learning low-dimensional representations that simultaneously preserve structural semantics and temporal dynamics. This survey presents the first systematic review of DHG representation learning methods. We first introduce a unified formal definition that encompasses both discrete-time and continuous-time DHGs from the perspective of temporal granularity. Building upon this formulation, we propose a novel algorithm-centric taxonomy that categorizes existing literature, including early embedding-based approaches, graph neural network (GNN)-based models, and relatively recent Transformer-based DHG methods, while explicitly highlighting their intrinsic modeling biases with respect to dynamic granularity. Furthermore, we summarize representative applications of DHG representation learning, along with commonly used datasets and benchmarks. Finally, we discuss promising research directions that guide future advances in this rapidly evolving field.

</details>


### [148] [Learning-Augmented Algorithms: Guarantees, Construction Mechanisms, and System-Level Implications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04787)
*Hailiang Zhao,Peng Chen,Xueyan Tang,Jianwei Yin,Shuiguang Deng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Survey of learning-augmented algorithms covering prediction interfaces, error measures, consistency-robustness trade-offs, construction mechanisms, and theoretical vs. empirical considerations across multiple domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning-augmented algorithms combine machine learning predictions with traditional algorithmic guarantees, but the field lacks systematic synthesis of interfaces, error measures, trade-offs, and construction mechanisms across different problem domains.

Method: Synthesis approach examining prediction interfaces, error measures, consistency-robustness trade-offs, and five construction mechanisms across online optimization, caching, learned data structures, graph problems, and mechanism design. Separates theoretical guarantees from empirical evidence with explicit treatment of prediction cost, feedback, and composition.

Result: Provides comprehensive synthesis of learning-augmented algorithms field, establishing sufficient conditions for limited end-to-end reasoning. Organizes knowledge along theorem-level axis distinguishing achieved upper bounds from matched asymptotic dependence.

Conclusion: The survey identifies key open problems: cost-aware prediction, endogenous error, semantic predictors, and benchmarking. It provides a structured framework for understanding and advancing learning-augmented algorithms while separating formal guarantees from empirical evidence.

Abstract: Learning-augmented algorithms use fallible predictions while retaining formal performance guarantees. This survey synthesizes prediction interfaces, error measures, consistency--robustness trade-offs, and five representative construction mechanisms across online optimization, caching, learned data structures, graph problems, and mechanism design. An orthogonal theorem-level axis distinguishes achieved upper bounds from matched asymptotic dependence. Formal guarantees are separated from empirical systems evidence, with explicit treatment of prediction cost, feedback, and composition. The resulting synthesis states sufficient conditions for limited end-to-end reasoning and delineates open problems in cost-aware prediction, endogenous error, semantic predictors, and benchmarking.

</details>


### [149] [How Faithful Is Attribution for Sales Forecasting? A Counterfactual Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04797)
*Glib Kechyn*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a post-hoc, architecture-agnostic counterfactual interpretability layer for WaveNet-style multi-series sales forecasters that decomposes forecasts into contributions summing exactly to predicted values, with rigorous faithfulness evaluation and candid assessment of its limitations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep models for sales forecasting (like WaveNet) are accurate but opaque - when predicting sales for multiple time series, they offer no explanation for why certain forecasts are made. There's a need for interpretability in multi-series forecasting without the allocation artifacts seen in methods like SHAP attribution.

Method: The authors add a post-hoc, architecture-agnostic counterfactual interpretability layer to a multi-series WaveNet forecaster trained on the Corporacion Favorita grocery dataset (174,685 series over 1,688 days). The method decomposes each forecast into contributions that sum exactly to the predicted value, avoiding additive SHAP-style attribution artifacts. They evaluate faithfulness using deletion/insertion protocols.

Result: Statistically significant faithfulness: deletion gap 0.22 (p<0.001) and insertion gap 0.27 (p<0.01), robust across five background-sampling seeds. Attributions reflect genuine model behavior. Analysis shows heterogeneous reliance on promotion signals across series (median ratio ~1.0, ~20% show strong effect). Model captures weekly sales cycle shape (day-of-week r=0.78) but systematically under-predicts amplitude.

Conclusion: The main contribution is not improved accuracy but an interpretability layer with rigorous faithfulness evaluation and honest characterization of its limits. The method provides counterfactual explanations for multi-series forecasts while acknowledging where attribution is and isn't informative.

Abstract: Deep models for sales forecasting, such as WaveNet-style dilated convolutional networks, are accurate but opaque: when a single model predicts sales for one of many series, it offers no account of why. We add a post-hoc, architecture-agnostic counterfactual interpretability layer to a multi-series WaveNet forecaster trained on the full Corporacion Favorita grocery dataset (174,685 series over 1,688 days). The method decomposes each forecast into contributions that sum exactly to the predicted value, avoiding the allocation artifacts we observed with additive SHAP-style attribution. We evaluate faithfulness with a deletion/insertion protocol and find a statistically significant effect on both tests (deletion gap 0.22, p<0.001; insertion gap 0.27, p<0.01; robust across five background-sampling seeds), establishing that the attributions reflect genuine model behavior rather than plausible-looking artifacts.
