We then characterize, honestly, where attribution is and is not informative: reliance on the promotion signal is heterogeneous across series (median ratio approximately 1.0, with roughly 20% of series showing a strong effect), and the model captures the shape of the weekly sales cycle (day-of-week r=0.78) while systematically under-predicting its amplitude. Our contribution is not improved accuracy but an interpretability layer with a rigorous faithfulness evaluation and a candid account of its limits.

</details>


### [150] [Federated Attack Campaign Detection via Contrastive Encoding of Threat Indicators in Gradient Updates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04815)
*Manuel Röder,Bibin Babu,Frank-Michael Schleif*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedIoC is a federated learning framework that enables collaborative detection of multi-organizational cyberattack campaigns by encoding local threat indicators into gradient updates without sharing sensitive data, allowing servers to cluster coordinated attacks from gradient geometry alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional methods for detecting cross-organizational cyberattack campaigns require sharing sensitive telemetry and threat intelligence across institutional boundaries, which creates privacy and legal barriers. Federated Learning offers a solution by enabling collaborative training without direct data sharing.

Method: FedIoC uses a supervised contrastive loss where clients embed locally available structured threat indicators (IoCs) into their gradient updates. Flows matching known indicators form positive sets, with contrastive learning pulling their embeddings together and pushing non-IoC embeddings apart. The server then clusters updates by cosine similarity to recover global campaign patterns without direct IoC transmission.

Result: On two public threat-detection benchmarks distributed across FL clients, FedIoC successfully recovered cross-organizational campaign cohorts directly from gradient geometry. Clients observing only fragments of campaigns with disjoint indicator sets enabled the server to identify coordinated attack patterns.

Conclusion: FedIoC provides an effective modular framework for collaborative cyberattack detection without sensitive data sharing, with non-IID gradient structure identified as the key driver of campaign recovery. The paper establishes the open problem of designing better encoders to improve upon this gradient-based approach.

Abstract: Detecting orchestrated cyberattack campaigns that span multiple organizations traditionally requires sharing sensitive telemetry and threat intelligence across institutional boundaries and country borders, a barrier that Federated Learning removes by training shared threat detectors directly on local data. We propose FedIoC, a modular framework in which clients fold locally available structured threat indicators into their gradient updates; we instantiate the client-side encoder with a supervised contrastive loss over IoC-matched flows. Within each training batch, flows that match any known indicator pattern form the positive set; the contrastive objective pulls their learned embeddings together and pushes non-IoC embeddings away, so that campaign-relevant structure is, by design, expressed in the gradient direction. Clients sharing indicators for the same attack campaign then produce aligned gradient components, which the server clusters by the cosine similarity of their updates to recover global campaign patterns without any direct IoC transmission. We evaluate FedIoC on two public threat-detection benchmarks distributed across FL clients that each observe only a fragment of every active campaign and hold disjoint indicator sets derived from their local telemetry. In this regime the FL server recovers cross-organizational campaign cohorts directly from gradient geometry. We contribute FedIoC as a modular framework for this setting, and use it to pinpoint the non-IID gradient structure as the main driver of recovery and to define the open problem of designing encoders that improve on it.

</details>


### [151] [Communication-Efficient Personalized Federated Learning via Layer-Wise Multi-Threshold Random Sketching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04830)
*Xu Zhang,Xingyu Hou,Jiacheng Cheng,Kaiyuan Feng,Maoguo Gong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel layer-wise multi-threshold random sketching method for personalized federated learning that achieves better communication efficiency than existing one-bit compression approaches by adapting to layer-specific parameter distributions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Personalized federated learning addresses data heterogeneity but suffers from high communication costs in bandwidth-constrained systems. Existing one-bit compression methods are too simplistic - they use a single threshold for the entire model, ignoring layer-wise differences in parameter distributions and quantization sensitivities, providing only coarse binary information.

Method: Proposes a communication-efficient PFL framework using layer-wise multi-threshold random sketching. Each layer gets its own set of quantization thresholds, allowing compressed representations to adapt to layer-specific statistics while using multiple intervals to provide finer low-bit descriptions of sketched parameters. Supports bidirectional communication with compact low-bit sketches.

Result: The method improves the communication-accuracy tradeoff compared to existing one-bit compression approaches by better capturing fine-grained variations in parameter distributions through layer-specific multi-threshold quantization.

Conclusion: Layer-wise multi-threshold random sketching provides a more effective compression strategy for personalized federated learning, addressing the limitations of single-threshold one-bit methods while maintaining communication efficiency in bandwidth-constrained distributed systems.

Abstract: Personalized federated learning (PFL) is a promising paradigm for collaborative learning over distributed devices, where edge nodes collaboratively train personalized models without sharing raw data. Although PFL addresses data heterogeneity by learning client-specific models, it still suffers from substantial uplink and downlink communication costs when exchanging high-dimensional parameters in bandwidth-constrained systems. Recent one-bit methods achieve extreme compression, but they usually rely on a single thresholding rule applied to the whole model. This design has two limitations. First, it overlooks layer-wise differences in parameter distributions and quantization sensitivities. Second, a single threshold provides only coarse binary information and cannot capture fine-grained variations in parameter distributions. To address these issues, we propose a communication-efficient PFL framework via layer-wise multi-threshold random sketching. In the proposed method, each layer is assigned its own set of quantization thresholds, so that the compressed representation can adapt to layer-specific statistics while using multiple intervals to provide a finer low-bit description of sketched parameters. The proposed method supports bidirectional communication using compact low-bit sketches and improves the communication-accuracy tradeoff compared with existing one-bit compression approaches.

</details>


### [152] [PACE: Propagation-Aware Collaborative Correction for One-Shot Personalized Federated Graph Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04832)
*Ruizhe Huang,Chengran Li,Xiaochuan Shi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PACE enables personalized federated graph learning by treating collaborative knowledge as a compact correction to local models rather than replacement, using rank-r updates and convex calibration to selectively combine local and external knowledge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Client heterogeneity in federated graph learning creates tension: knowledge from other subgraphs can complement local models, but incompatible transfers can override reliable predictions. One-shot communication exacerbates this issue since incorrect server responses can't be corrected later.

Method: PACE uploads rank-r update carriers and diagonal sketches of message moments. The server constructs propagation-aware, receiver-anchored corrections while clients retain full local models. Convex negative-log-likelihood calibration selects coefficients between local and external logits using validation nodes without modifying model parameters.

Result: At Rank-6, corrections occupy only 9.6-17.6% of dense tensor bytes. The approach improves Accuracy and weighted-F1 over local models on 5 of 6 datasets, while on ogbn-arxiv it assigns zero weight to corrections, preserving local predictions exactly. These gains aren't explained by the calibration rule alone.

Conclusion: A small transported correction can effectively augment complete local models when receiver evidence supports it, while leaving local predictions unchanged when not beneficial, resolving the tension between collaborative knowledge and model reliability in federated graph learning.

Abstract: Client heterogeneity creates both an opportunity and a risk in personalized federated graph learning. Knowledge held by other subgraphs may complement a receiver's Local model, but an incompatible transfer can override reliable predictions. One-shot communication sharpens this tension because an unsuitable server return cannot be corrected later. We introduce PACE, which treats collaborative knowledge as a compact correction to a complete Local predictor rather than as its replacement. Each client uploads a rank-r update carrier and a diagonal sketch of propagated message moments. The server uses them to construct a propagation-aware, receiver-anchored correction, while the receiver retains its full Local model. Convex negative-log-likelihood calibration (CNLL) then selects one coefficient between Local and External logits using validation nodes; model parameters remain fixed and no feedback is sent. At Rank-6, personalized returns occupy 9.6-17.6% of dense tensor bytes across the six evaluated datasets. The correction receives nonzero weight and improves both Accuracy and weighted-F1 over Local on five datasets; on ogbn-arxiv, CNLL assigns zero predictive weight to the correction and preserves Local predictions exactly. Applying the same CNLL rule to matched baselines on three citation datasets does not account for these gains. The central result is therefore that a small transported correction can augment a complete Local model when receiver evidence supports it while leaving the Local prediction unchanged otherwise.

</details>


### [153] [KVMem: Virtualizing Million-Token Agent Workspaces on a Consumer GPU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04852)
*Di Chai,Leye Wang,Zeshen Su,Zhiguo Xia,Zhihang Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: KVMem is a KV-context virtualization system that handles LLM agent workspace overflow by paging KV state across GPU, host, and NVMe, using attention-space indexes to select relevant historical blocks and create query-dependent views within the model's native context window.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern LLM agents operate in persistent workspaces whose accumulated history can exceed GPU KV capacity and model context windows. Existing approaches either lose fine-grained execution evidence through summarization or inefficiently retrieve text that has already been processed.

Method: KVMem preserves overflowed workspace history as paged KV state across memory hierarchy (GPU, host, NVMe). It uses lightweight, model-native attention-space indexes to select relevant historical blocks and materializes query-dependent execution views bounded by the model's native context window.

Result: KVMem achieves higher task utility and greater inference efficiency than compaction-based approaches, improving DeepSWE test success from 43.8% to 48.4% with Qwen3.8-27B. It enables virtualizing agent workspaces up to 1M tokens (4x native context) on consumer hardware, maintaining ~50 tokens/s for interactive responsiveness.

Conclusion: KVMem provides a practical solution for long-running agents by decoupling addressable workspace size from the LLM's native context window, enabling workspaces to grow beyond the model's context limitations while maintaining performance and efficiency.

Abstract: Modern LLM agents operate in persistent workspaces whose accumulated history can exceed both GPU KV capacity and the model's native context window. Existing systems typically compact older context into summaries or retrieve it later as text, either losing fine-grained execution evidence or repeatedly prefilling content that the model has already processed. We present KVMem, a KV-context virtualization system that preserves overflowed workspace history as paged KV state across GPU memory, host memory, and NVMe. KVMem uses lightweight, model-native attention-space indexes to select relevant historical blocks and materializes a query-dependent execution view bounded by the model's native context window. Extensive evaluations on long-context agent benchmarks spanning histories up to one million tokens, including LongMemEval, MemoryAgentBench, and AgentLongBench, show that KVMem generally achieves higher task utility and greater inference efficiency than compaction-based approaches, the de facto standard for handling context overflow. In the DeepSWE long-context test with Qwen3.8-27B, KVMem improves task success from 43.8% with compaction-only context management to 48.4%.
  In our local-deployment evaluation, KVMem runs Qwen3.6/3.8-27B NVFP4 with MTP on an off-the-shelf laptop equipped with a 24\,GB RTX 5090 Laptop GPU, virtualizing agent workspaces of up to 1M tokens-four times the model's native 256K-token context window. In a single-session setting, KVMem generates $\sim$50 tokens/s, providing interactive responsiveness for local agent execution. More broadly, by decoupling addressable workspace size from the LLM's native context window, KVMem provides a practical path toward long-running agents whose workspaces can grow beyond that window.

</details>


### [154] [When Genomic Masking Priors Fail to Transfer: Strong Variant Prediction, Weak Functional Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04861)
*Susu Hu,Preetam Gattogi,Jens Lehmann,Sahar Vahdati,Stefanie Speidel,Julien Vibert*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A bidirectional discrete diffusion model (GenDA) for genomic modeling shows strong fine-tuned variant prediction but fails functional generation tests, revealing distinct validation requirements for different genomic modeling claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bidirectional discrete diffusion appears naturally suited for genomic modeling since it can reconstruct missing sequences from both flanks. The authors hypothesized that entropy-guided span placement would concentrate reconstruction pressure on compositionally complex regions, improving variant-effect prediction and functional sequence generation.

Method: Developed GenDA (Genomic Density-optimized Absorbing Diffusion) with entropy-guided span placement. Evaluated against autoregressive models and random-span variants. Conducted zero-shot functional inpainting stress tests across promoters, enhancers, exon boundaries, and intron boundaries. Used 202M-parameter model with 1-mer tokenization and training spans capped at 300 bp.

Result: GenDA achieves 0.774 AUROC on ClinVar SNV prediction, beating similar autoregressive model by 0.103, but matched random-span variant reaches 0.777, showing no benefit from entropy guidance. GenDA fails zero-shot functional inpainting tests across genomic regions and gap sizes (50-500 bp), not consistently outperforming a control that shuffles native gap while preserving 3-mer composition. Enhancer degradation worsens at longer gaps.

Conclusion: Strong fine-tuned variant prediction, plausible corruption prior, and functional generation are distinct claims requiring separate validation. Key limitations: entropy measures local complexity not functional importance, 1-mer tokenization limits context, training span caps, and high absolute fidelity can coexist with negative control-normalized restoration.

Abstract: Bidirectional discrete diffusion model appears naturally suited to genomic modeling because it can reconstruct missing sequence from both flanks. We developed GenDA (Genomic Density-optimized Absorbing Diffusion) under the additional hypothesis that entropy-guided span placement would concentrate reconstruction pressure on compositionally complex regions, improving both downstream variant-effect prediction and functional sequence generation. Our results only partially support this premise. After supervised fine-tuning, the 202M-parameter GenDA model reaches a pooled ClinVar SNV AUROC of 0.774, exceeding a similarly scaled autoregressive model by 0.103. However, a matched random-span variant reaches 0.777, providing no evidence that entropy guidance causes the ClinVar improvement. More unexpectedly, GenDA fails a zero-shot functional inpainting stress test: across promoters, enhancers, exon boundaries, and intron boundaries, it does not consistently outperform a control that shuffles the native gap while exactly preserving 3-mer composition. Failure is already present for 50--500-bp gaps, although enhancer degradation worsens at longer gaps. Diagnostics identify several boundary conditions: entropy measures local sequence complexity rather than functional importance; 1-mer tokenization limits physical context; training spans are capped at 300 bp; and high absolute AlphaGenome fidelity can coexist with negative control-normalized restoration. These results show that strong fine-tuned variant prediction, a plausible corruption prior, and functional generation are distinct claims that require separate validation.

</details>


### [155] [From Deep to Shallow: Unconstrained and Efficient Layer Merging Strategy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04881)
*Petro Shulzhenko,Gabriele Spadaro,Enzo Tartaglione*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a depth compression method for neural networks that can merge layers without existing analytical solutions and without increasing kernel size, enabling efficient inference on resource-constrained devices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep Neural Networks have high computational demands that limit their application in resource-constrained environments. Existing depth compression methods that linearize redundant activation functions face challenges: they cannot be applied to convolutions with padding (no analytical solution for merging), and they typically increase kernel size of merged layers, limiting speed-up gains.

Method: The paper proposes an efficient strategy that enables merging of layers without requiring an existing analytical solution, and also without increasing kernel size. The method addresses the limitation with convolutions involving padding where traditional analytical solutions don't exist.

Result: Validated across multiple architectures and datasets, with inference speed-up gains measured on real embedded platforms. The approach successfully enables depth compression where previous methods failed, particularly for convolutions with padding.

Conclusion: The proposed method overcomes key limitations of existing depth compression techniques, enabling more efficient neural network inference on resource-constrained devices. Code is publicly released at the provided GitHub repository.

Abstract: Although Deep Neural Networks have become foundational in many areas of Machine Learning, high computational demands limit their application in resource-constrained environments. To address this issue, depth compression methods have been proposed to identify and linearize redundant activation functions, thereby allowing for the merging of layers without intermediate non-linearities. However, these methods face two key challenges: they cannot be directly applied to convolutions with padding due to the absence of an analytical solution for merging these layers, and they typically increase the kernel size of merged layers, thus limiting speed-up gains. To overcome these limitations, we propose an efficient strategy that enables merging of layers without an existing analytical solution, and also without increasing kernel size. We validate our approach across multiple architectures and datasets, and measure inference speed-up gains on real embedded platforms. We publicly released the code at https://github.com/ShulzhenkoPetr/deep-to-shallow.

</details>


### [156] [Adaptation Interfaces for In-Context Tabular Foundation Models in Time-to-Event Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04901)
*Minh-Khoi Pham,Luca Cotugno,Dan Cernei,Alina Sirbu,Stefano Masi,Giuseppe Prencipe,Alessandro Pingitore,Patrizia Landi,Working Group on Uric Acid,Cardiovascular Risk of the Italian Society of Hypertension,Tai Tan Mai,Martin Crane,Marija Bezbradica*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Tabular foundation models can be effectively adapted for survival analysis with different methods performing better depending on data size: zero-shot works for smaller datasets, supervised adaptation for larger ones, with CoxPH being most reliable overall.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tabular foundation models (TabFMs) perform well on standard classification/regression tasks but adapting them to censored time-to-event prediction (survival analysis) is challenging due to requirements for handling censoring and event-time dynamics.

Method: Researchers link TabFMs with survival analysis methods (CoxPH and DeepHit) and revise context-resampled training. They evaluate three approaches: 1) temporal zero-shot reformulation, 2) classification-based fine-tuning, and 3) survival-head adaptation using frozen TabFM backbones on 74 single-risk datasets plus 4 competing-risk datasets.

Result: Zero-shot inference works well on smaller single-risk datasets, while supervised adaptation improves as datasets scale. CoxPH provides the most reliable performance (especially IBS on larger datasets). DeepHit is stronger for time-dependent Concordance Index than IBS. Classification fine-tuning becomes more competitive with zero-shot as datasets grow but weaker for probabilistic prediction. Cause-specific MTLR ranks highest among TabFM survival heads for competing-risk analysis.

Conclusion: Effective TabFM transfer for survival analysis depends on both the data regime (dataset size) and the statistical structure represented by the chosen adaptation interface, with CoxPH emerging as the most robust interface overall.

Abstract: Tabular foundation models (TabFMs) achieve strong performance on structured data, particularly for standard classification and regression problems. Yet, extending them to censored time-to-event prediction is challenging because it requires properly handling censoring and event-time dynamics. Building on our prior work, we further link TabFMs with CoxPH and DeepHit and revise the context-resampled training procedure. We evaluate temporal zero-shot reformulation, classification-based fine-tuning, and survival-head adaptation using frozen TabFM backbones on 74 single-risk data sets, and we additionally study 4 competing-risk data sets. Zero-shot inference is effective on smaller single-risk data sets, whereas supervised adaptation becomes increasingly advantageous as data sets scale. Cox provides the most reliably strong interface, especially for Integrated Brier Score (IBS) on larger data sets. DeepHit is relatively stronger for the time-dependent Concordance Index than for IBS, while cause-specific MTLR ranks highest among the TabFM survival heads in the four-data-set competing-risk analysis. Classification fine-tuning becomes more competitive with zero-shot inference as data sets grow but remains weaker for probabilistic prediction. Overall, our results indicate that effective TabFM transfer depends on the data regime and on the statistical structure represented by the chosen adaptation interface. The implementation scripts used for this work are available at https://github.com/kaylode/survival-fm.

</details>


### [157] [Fast Gauss Sums via Flash Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04910)
*Nicolaj Rux,Sebastian Neumayer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Flash attention can be adapted to compute Gaussian kernel sums (with arbitrary signed weights) via simple input augmentations, achieving better speed, memory efficiency, and accuracy than existing methods for feature dimensions >8 in fp16.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gaussian kernel sums are fundamental to many important kernel methods (MMDs, kernel gradient flows, SVGD), while flash attention has received extensive hardware optimization. There's an opportunity to leverage flash attention's engineering advancements for efficient Gaussian kernel computations.

Method: Two small input augmentations transform the normalized softmax reduction (used in flash attention) into an unnormalized Gaussian sum. This approach requires no custom GPU code and directly utilizes existing flash attention implementations.

Result: For feature dimensions D>8 in fp16, this flash attention-based approach outperforms compiled PyTorch code and PyKeOps kernels in speed, memory overhead, and accuracy. The method maintains linear memory scaling.

Conclusion: Flash attention provides an efficient, hardware-optimized solution for Gaussian kernel sums, enabling better performance for many kernel methods while leveraging existing, well-engineered attention implementations.

Abstract: Gaussian kernel sums are the computational core of maximum mean discrepancies (MMDs), kernel gradient flows, Stein variational gradient descent (SVGD), and many other kernel methods. At the same time, softmax attention has received an extraordinary amount of hardware-aware code engineering, culminating in flash attention. We show that Gauss kernel sums with arbitrary, signed weights can be evaluated via flash attention: two small input augmentations turn the normalized softmax reduction into the unnormalized Gauss sum, without writing a single line of custom GPU code. For feature dimension D>8 in fp16, this approach beats compiled PyTorch code as well as PyKeOps kernels (often significantly) in speed, memory-overhead and accuracy. Indeed, its memory scaling remains linear.

</details>


### [158] [Physics-Aware Random Walk Fingerprints for Scalable Power Grid Graph Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04943)
*Adnan Anwar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes MC-PA-RWF, a multi-channel physics-aware random walk fingerprint method for power grid cascading failure classification that incorporates physical edge states into graph representations, achieving competitive accuracy with GNNs while being more interpretable and lightweight.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current GNNs for power-grid cascading-failure classification require end-to-end training, model-specific tuning, and produce latent representations that are difficult to relate to physically meaningful propagation patterns. Existing Random Walk Fingerprints mainly focus on topology and node-level information, neglecting grid-relevant operational edge states.

Method: The proposed Multi-Channel Physics-Aware Random Walk Fingerprints (MC-PA-RWF) introduces physical edge states into random-walk propagation. It constructs multiple edge-weighted channels from domain-relevant attributes, extracts channel-specific fingerprints from each weighted graph, and concatenates the resulting vectors into a compact graph-level representation.

Result: Experiments on three PowerGraph benchmark systems show substantial improvements over topology-only RWF and competitive balanced accuracy against strong GNN baselines (GCN, GAT, GINE, TransformerConv). MC-PA-RWF+ achieves 98.04%-99.32% balanced accuracy and improves failure-class F1 by 1.60-5.84 percentage points over the strongest GNN baseline, with statistically significant gains across all three systems.

Conclusion: The proposed MC-PA-RWF framework provides a lightweight, scalable, and interpretable alternative to GNNs for power systems analysis, successfully incorporating physical edge states into graph representations while achieving competitive or superior performance on cascading-failure classification tasks.

Abstract: Recent benchmarks such as PowerGraph provide large collections of power-grid graphs for cascading-failure classification. Graph neural networks (GNNs) achieve strong predictive performance on this task, but typically require end-to-end training and model-specific tuning, while their latent representations can be difficult to relate to physically meaningful propagation patterns. Random Walk Fingerprints (RWF) offer a scalable and interpretable alternative, but existing variants primarily emphasise topology and node-level information, leaving grid-relevant operational edge states in the walk dynamics. We propose Multi-Channel Physics-Aware Random Walk Fingerprints (MC-PA-RWF) for power systems, a lightweight graph-level representation framework that introduces physical edge states into random-walk propagation. The method constructs multiple edge-weighted channels from domain-relevant attributes, extracts a channel-specific fingerprint from each weighted graph, and concatenates the resulting vectors into a compact representation. Experiments on three \textit{PowerGraph} benchmark systems show substantial improvements over topology-only RWF and competitive balanced accuracy against strong GNN baselines, including Graph Convolutional Networks (GCN), Graph Attention Networks (GAT), Graph Isomorphism Networks with edge features (GINE), and Transformer-based Graph Convolutional Networks (TransformerConv). At the largest evaluated settings, the node-edge extension MC-PA-RWF+ achieves around 98.04% - 99.32% balanced accuracy and improves failure-class F1 over the strongest GNN baseline by 1.60 -- 5.84 percentage points, with statistically significant gains across all three systems.

</details>


### [159] [Fractal basins trap latent reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04963)
*Jeffrey Lai,Anthony Bao,John Quinn,William Gilpin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper shows that reasoning models exhibit transient chaos due to computational complexity, with reasoning slowdowns on harder tasks resulting from being trapped near saddle points (nearly-correct solutions), making reasoning traces a new class of dynamical system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While reasoning models are known to reason longer on harder tasks, the underlying mechanism for these slowdowns is unknown. The authors aim to uncover the physical and computational principles behind reasoning slowdowns in AI models.

Method: The authors analyze diverse leading reasoning models as dynamical systems, examining their behavior across tasks like Sudoku, maze solving, visual puzzles, and mathematical logic. They show these models exhibit transient chaos and fractal basins.

Result: Reasoning models display transient chaos as a physical consequence of computational complexity. Their basins become increasingly fractal with task difficulty, with slowdowns occurring as reasoning gets trapped near saddle points representing nearly-correct attempted solutions.

Conclusion: Reasoning slowdowns are an inevitable consequence of problem hardness in modern AI models, establishing reasoning traces as a rich new class of dynamical system with physical underpinnings in transient chaos.

Abstract: Reasoning allows artificial intelligence models to revisit and correct their mistakes, enabling recent frontier advances in mathematical theorem solving, software engineering, and autonomous task planning. Reasoning models are widely observed to reason for longer on harder tasks, but the general mechanism responsible for these slowdowns is unknown. Here, we show that reasoning models exhibit transient chaos, a physical consequence of the computational complexity of difficult tasks. As a consequence, we show that diverse leading reasoning models are dynamical systems with fractal basins, with fractality increasing with task difficulty across diverse tasks like Sudoku and maze solving, visual puzzles, and mathematical logic. We show that transient chaos emerges due to reasoning becoming trapped for extended durations near saddle points, which we show correspond to nearly-correct attempted solutions of the underlying problem. Our results show that reasoning slowdowns are an inevitable consequence of problem hardness in modern artificial intelligence models, and establish reasoning traces as a rich new class of dynamical system.

</details>


### [160] [BeaconKV: Key-Value Cache Compression Guided by Beacon Queries for Efficient Large Reasoning Model Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04971)
*Janghyeon Kim,Minsoo Kim,Kyuhong Shim,Jungwook Choi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BeaconKV is a training-free KV cache compression method that uses beacon queries to anticipate attention revisiting patterns in long reasoning tasks, achieving 5.8× memory reduction while preserving accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large Reasoning Models suffer from memory bottlenecks due to KV cache growth during extended Chain-of-Thought reasoning. Existing compression methods fail for long-horizon reasoning because they assume recent queries predict future attention patterns, which breaks down when models revisit distant context tokens.

Method: BeaconKV identifies Thought Revisiting Tokens (TRTs) that re-attend to distant context. It clusters queries into similarity groups in embedding space and maintains compact "beacon queries" as representatives for each cluster. These beacons anticipate which KV pairs will be revisited without storing full query history, enabling training-free compression.

Result: Across four open-source LRMs and diverse reasoning benchmarks, BeaconKV outperforms existing compression methods, achieving up to 5.8× memory reduction while nearly preserving full cache accuracy and improving throughput by over 4.3×.

Conclusion: The assumption that recent queries reliably predict future attention patterns fails in long-horizon reasoning. By identifying and clustering TRTs into beacon queries, BeaconKV provides an effective training-free solution for KV cache compression that addresses memory bottlenecks in Large Reasoning Models while maintaining reasoning quality.

Abstract: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) achieve superior problem-solving through extended Chain-of-Thought (CoT) generation, but the resulting key-value (KV) cache grows linearly with sequence length and creates severe memory bottlenecks, often exceeding GPU capacity for long reasoning traces. Existing KV cache compression methods rely on recent queries to estimate future token importance, implicitly assuming these serve as reliable proxies for future attention patterns. We demonstrate that this assumption fails in long-horizon reasoning: certain decoding steps generate Thought Revisiting Tokens (TRT) that re-attend to distant previous context, such as task-solving plans formulated early in the trace. Through systematic analysis, we discover that queries corresponding to the TRT cluster into a small number of similarity groups in the embedding space. Based on this insight, we propose BeaconKV, a training-free KV cache compression method that maintains beacon queries, compact representatives for each global query cluster, to anticipate which KV pairs will be revisited without storing the entire query history. Across four open-source LRMs and diverse reasoning benchmarks, BeaconKV generally outperforms existing compression methods, achieving up to $5.8\times$ memory reduction while nearly preserving full cache accuracy and improving throughput by over $4.3\times$.

</details>


### [161] [Beyond Homoscedasticity: Decoupled Uncertainty Optimization for Deep Imbalanced Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04995)
*Juncheng Zhou,Jiaxi Lu,Weijing Zeng,Zhong Li,Hao Qi,Jingsong Cui*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DUO is a novel framework for Deep Imbalanced Regression that addresses heteroscedastic uncertainty and gradient coupling issues through decoupled mean-variance optimization and distribution-guided contrastive learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep Imbalanced Regression faces challenges where existing methods learn deterministic point mappings assuming uniform uncertainty, ignoring instance-wise heteroscedasticity in long-tailed data. Even heteroscedastic negative log-likelihood suffers from gradient coupling that weakens learning signals for valuable tail samples, leading to optimization inertia and tail underfitting.

Method: DUO models regression targets as conditional Gaussian distributions to characterize instance-level predictive uncertainty explicitly. It transforms uncertainty into dynamic enhancement signals for tail samples through decoupled mean-variance optimization. Additionally, it employs a distribution-guided contrastive learning mechanism that adaptively constructs positive/negative pairs based on sample distribution overlap to alleviate feature looseness and semantic entanglement.

Result: DUO achieves state-of-the-art performance across visual and biological DIR benchmarks, obtaining the best few-shot bMAE and GM metrics on IMDB-WIKI-DIR, AgeDB-DIR, and AAV2-DIR while remaining competitive on few-shot MAE.

Conclusion: DUO successfully addresses key limitations in Deep Imbalanced Regression by explicitly modeling instance-level uncertainty and decoupling mean-variance optimization, demonstrating superior performance on multiple DIR benchmarks through its uncertainty-aware framework and distribution-guided contrastive learning approach.

Abstract: Deep Imbalanced Regression (DIR) is pervasive in continuous prediction tasks across diverse modalities, such as age estimation, depth prediction, and protein mutation activity prediction, where label-scarce tail samples often carry higher practical value. However, most existing methods still learn deterministic point mappings under mean squared error or its simple variants, implicitly assuming a uniform uncertainty level across all samples and thereby overlooking the instance-wise heteroscedasticity that is widespread in long-tailed data. We further point out that even heteroscedastic negative log-likelihood suffers from a gradient coupling issue, which, under DIR scenarios, weakens the learning signal of hard tail samples and leads to optimization inertia as well as tail underfitting. To address this, we propose DUO, an uncertainty-aware long-tailed regression framework. Specifically, the proposed method models the regression target as a conditional Gaussian distribution to explicitly characterize instance-level predictive uncertainty, and transforms uncertainty into a dynamic enhancement signal for tail samples through decoupled mean-variance optimization. Furthermore, we design a distribution-guided contrastive learning mechanism that adaptively constructs positive and negative pairs based on the overlap between sample distributions, thereby alleviating feature looseness and cross-label semantic entanglement. Across visual and biological DIR benchmarks, DUO achieves the best few-shot bMAE and GM on IMDB-WIKI-DIR, AgeDB-DIR, and AAV2-DIR while remaining competitive on few-shot MAE.

</details>


### [162] [Solution-space heterogeneity shapes federated learning dynamics across partial differential equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05012)
*Ping Luo,Jiahuan Wang,Ziqing Wen,Tao Sun,Dongsheng Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a solution-space protocol called PDE-Dirichlet for quantifying and analyzing non-IID data effects in federated learning for PDEs, showing that data heterogeneity increases solution distance and optimization divergence, but generalization impact varies by task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current federated learning approaches for PDEs lack a transferable definition of non-IID data, with existing methods using equation-specific partitioning rules that are not reusable across different PDE problems.

Method: Proposed solution-space PDE-Dirichlet protocol: converts continuous PDE responses into reusable solution bins, quantifies client separation using optimal transport over solution geometry, and analyzes relationship between population heterogeneity and Dirichlet concentration.

Result: Lower Dirichlet concentration increases realized solution distance and optimization heterogeneity across all tested PDE tasks; final error degradation is task-dependent: largest effect in low-viscosity Burgers (4.157 percentage points), while additional communication or smoother dynamics can reduce final gap despite persistent parameter separation.

Conclusion: The work distinguishes between reproducible geometric mechanisms of data heterogeneity and task-dependent generalization outcomes, providing a common basis for evaluating non-IID federated PDE learning that can be applied across different PDE problems.

Abstract: Federated scientific machine learning enables institutions to train neural surrogates without centralizing local physical data, yet studies of partial differential equations (PDEs) lack a transferable definition of non-independent and identically distributed data. Existing protocols partition coordinates, coefficients, boundary conditions, or geometries according to equation-specific rules. Here, we introduce solution-space PDE-Dirichlet, a protocol that converts continuous supervised responses into reusable solution bins and quantifies the realized separation between clients through optimal transport over the geometry of these bins. We derive an exact inverse relation between population allocation heterogeneity and the Dirichlet concentration, and we establish conditions under which response heterogeneity induces gradient disagreement, local-update dispersion, and parameter divergence. Across seven controlled and public PDE tasks, three neural-operator families, and five random seeds, a lower concentration consistently increases the realized solution distance and optimization heterogeneity. The degradation in final error is task dependent: the largest effect occurs for low-viscosity Burgers, reaching 4.157 percentage points under the most heterogeneous setting, whereas additional communication or smoother dynamics can reduce the final gap despite persistent parameter separation. These results distinguish a reproducible geometric mechanism from task-dependent generalization outcomes and provide a common basis for evaluating non-IID federated PDE learning.

</details>


### [163] [Amortizing Scaling Law Construction Costs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05016)
*Abhash Kumar Jha,Diana Alexandra Onuţu,Neeratyoy Mallik,Swagatam Haldar,Sam Laing,Niccolò Ajroldi,Shiwei Liu,Joaquin Vanschoren,Aaron Klein*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Efficient scaling law construction via Bayesian optimization that matches full-grid results with 10-100x computational savings


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deriving neural scaling laws typically requires training exhaustive hyperparameter grids, which is computationally expensive. Current methods waste resources by training most configurations, even though only the best-loss frontier is needed for fitting scaling laws.

Method: Proposes a Bayesian optimization framework for efficient scaling law construction, with progressive compute budget expansion during acquisition (mimicking practical evaluation order) and augmentation of observed configurations with surrogate-fantasized evaluations to recover the broader experimental grid.

Result: The method can closely match scaling law fits obtained from full dense grids while achieving computational savings of 10-100x. Progressive compute budget expansion improves recovery efficiency, and surrogate-augmented configurations enable accurate scaling law fitting without training every configuration.

Conclusion: Efficient scaling law construction is feasible through smart Bayesian optimization strategies that selectively sample the configuration space, dramatically reducing computational costs while maintaining accuracy comparable to exhaustive grid searches.

Abstract: Scaling laws guide the design choices for training large foundation models, but deriving them involves training an exhaustive grid over hyperparameters, token budgets, and parameter counts, which is computationally expensive. Fitting a scaling law, however, only requires the best-loss frontier across compute scales, discarding most of the trained configurations. We propose a framework for efficient scaling law construction that formulates data collection as a Bayesian optimization problem, and introduce metrics for comparing scaling law fitting methods under constrained compute budgets. We find that progressively expanding the compute budget during acquisition, mirroring the compute-ordered evaluation of configurations in practice, substantially improves recovery efficiency. Augmenting the observed configurations with surrogate-fantasized evaluations then recovers the broader experimental grid, allowing accurate scaling law fitting without training every configuration. Together, these can closely match scaling law fits over a full dense grid at computational savings of up to $10\text{--}100\times$.

</details>


### [164] [Confounding-Valid Conformal Inference for Counterfactual KPIs in Wireless Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05073)
*Abdessamed Qchohi,Jessica Moysen Cortes,Matteo Zecchin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Conformal counterfactual inference for network 'what-if' analysis that combines observational and randomized telemetry to handle hidden confounding while maintaining statistical guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Network operators need to answer 'what-if' questions using logged telemetry, but hidden confounding from omitted variables in observational data invalidates statistical guarantees, while purely randomized data is scarce and produces uninformative predictions.

Method: Proposes Confounding-Valid Counterfactual Conformal Inference (CV-CCI) which combines abundant observational telemetry with limited randomized data using the General Synthetic-Powered Inference (GESPI) principle. Uses observational data for efficiency while using randomized data to guarantee finite-sample coverage under arbitrary hidden confounding.

Result: CV-CCI remains valid under hidden confounding while producing more efficient prediction sets than state-of-the-art confounding-valid baselines in experiments on two representative radio access network (RAN) control tasks.

Conclusion: The CV-CCI framework successfully addresses the challenge of hidden confounding in network counterfactual analysis by leveraging both observational and randomized data, maintaining statistical validity while improving prediction efficiency for practical network operation scenarios.

Abstract: Conformal counterfactual inference enables network operators to use logged telemetry to reliably answer 'what-if' questions about network operation. These answers typically take the form of prediction sets that contain, with a user-defined probability, the key performance indicators (KPIs) that would have been observed under alternative control actions. A key challenge is that logged telemetry may omit variables used by the controller, resulting in hidden confounding and invalidating the statistical guarantees of counterfactual analysis. In principle, this issue can be addressed using randomized telemetry, collected by assigning control actions independently of the network state. However, because such randomization may disrupt normal operation, randomized telemetry is typically scarce, causing counterfactual analysis based solely on it to produce uninformative prediction sets. To address these challenges, we propose Confounding-Valid Counterfactual Conformal Inference (CV-CCI), which combines abundant, potentially confounded observational telemetry with limited randomized data through the General Synthetic-Powered Inference (GESPI) principle. CV-CCI leverages observational data to improve efficiency while using randomized data to retain finite-sample coverage guarantees under arbitrary hidden confounding. Experiments on two representative radio access network (RAN) control tasks show that CV-CCI remains valid under hidden confounding while producing more efficient prediction sets than state-of-the-art confounding-valid baselines.

</details>


### [165] [Deep Microcompression: Structured Pruning and Bit-packed Quantization for Microcontrollers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05081)
*Opegbemi Matthias Busoye,Tolulope Matthew Busoye,Eghonghon-aye Eigbe*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Deep Microcompression (DMC) is a hardware-aware pipeline that achieves 55.8× weight compression for deep learning inference on resource-constrained microcontrollers, enabling CNN deployment on devices with only 2KB SRAM.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning deployment on bare-metal microcontrollers is challenging due to severe resource constraints (memory, compute) and lack of dependency-rich frameworks like TensorFlow Lite. There's a need for efficient CNN inference on extremely limited hardware like ATmega328P with only 2KB SRAM.

Method: DMC combines structured pruning, quantization-aware training, and fixed-length bit-packing in a hardware-aware pipeline to maximize compression. It generates dependency-free C libraries with deterministic latency optimized for microcontroller deployment.

Result: • 55.8× weight compression ratio on LeNet-5 while maintaining 98.77% accuracy
• 3× reduction in binary size versus TensorFlow Lite on RP2040 (Cortex-M0+) while matching accuracy
• First documented deployment of a standard CNN on ATmega328P (2KB SRAM)
• Dependency-free C library generation with deterministic latency

Conclusion: DMC successfully addresses the challenge of deep learning inference on severely constrained microcontrollers, proving that CNN deployment is feasible even on devices with only 2KB SRAM previously considered infeasible. The approach opens new possibilities for edge AI applications.

Abstract: This paper introduces Deep Microcompression (DMC), a hardware-aware pipeline for deep learning inference on bare-metal microcontrollers. DMC integrates structured pruning, quantization-aware training, and fixed-length bit-packing to achieve a 55.8$\times$ weight compression ratio on LeNet-5 (98.77\% accuracy), generating a dependency-free C library with deterministic latency. On the RP2040 (Cortex-M0+), DMC reduces binary size by 3$\times$ versus TensorFlow Lite while matching its accuracy. Critically, DMC enables the first documented deployment of a standard CNN on the ATmega328P, a device constrained to 2KB SRAM, previously considered infeasible for CNN inference.

</details>


### [166] [NEAT-POCKET: Pocket-Conditioned Autoregressive 3D Molecular Generation with a Neighborhood-Guided Set Transformer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05097)
*Roxane Axel Jacob,Daniel Rose,Thierry Langer,Johannes Kirchmair*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NEAT-POCKET is a pocket-conditioned autoregressive model for 3D molecular generation that builds molecules atom by atom within protein binding pockets with competitive performance and fast sampling speed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI-driven de novo molecular design can accelerate early-stage drug discovery by generating novel ligands directly within target protein binding pockets, but existing methods need improvements in efficiency and practical utility.

Method: NEAT-POCKET extends the NEAT autoregressive model to be pocket-conditioned for 3D molecular generation. It generates molecules atom by atom in protein pocket environments while preserving atom permutation invariance and explicitly modeling hydrogen atoms.

Result: On CrossDocked and SPINDR datasets, NEAT-POCKET achieves competitive structure-based generation performance while sampling substantially faster than existing baselines. It also enables pocket-conditioned fragment completion for lead optimization tasks.

Conclusion: NEAT-POCKET is a fast, flexible, and practical framework for structure-based drug design that supports both full-molecule generation and fragment completion for lead optimization.

Abstract: AI-driven de novo molecular design offers a promising route to accelerate early-stage drug discovery by generating novel ligands directly within target protein binding pockets. We present NEAT-POCKET, a pocket-conditioned extension of the autoregressive NEAT model for 3D molecular generation. NEAT-POCKET generates molecules atom by atom in protein pocket environments while preserving atom permutation invariance and explicitly modeling hydrogen atoms. Benchmarks on the CrossDocked and SPINDR datasets show that NEAT-POCKET achieves competitive structure-based generation performance while sampling substantially faster than existing baselines. Beyond full-molecule generation, NEAT-POCKET naturally enables pocket-conditioned fragment completion, a task directly relevant to lead optimization and scaffold elaboration. These results position NEAT-POCKET as a fast, flexible, and practical framework for structure-based drug design.

</details>


### [167] [A Comparative Study of Counterfactual Explainers for Graph Neural Networks Enabling Multiple Types of Graph Edit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05113)
*Maria Myrto Villia,Filippos Gouidis,Theodore Patkos,Panos Trahanias*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper compares six state-of-the-art counterfactual explanation methods for graph-structured data across various real-world and synthetic datasets, evaluating their strengths and weaknesses in terms of explanation size, coverage, and quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Counterfactual explanations for graph data need to determine minimal, realistic modifications to change model predictions. Current methods lack general efficiency and vary in quality, with different methods trading off between explanation size, coverage, and quality. A comprehensive comparison is needed to identify where each method excels and falls short.

Method: Comparative study of six SOTA counterfactual explanation methods on diverse real-world and synthetic datasets covering both binary and multi-class graph and node classification tasks. Evaluation uses diverse quantitative and qualitative metrics.

Result: The study provides a comprehensive comparison revealing the strengths and weaknesses of each method across different scenarios. Results show that existing methods exhibit different performance characteristics, often making trade-offs between explanation size, coverage, and quality.

Conclusion: The research highlights the importance of identifying where each counterfactual explanation method performs well and where it falls short, providing guidance for future research in the field of graph counterfactual explanations.

Abstract: Counterfactual explanations for graph-structured data seek to determine minimal and realistic modifications required in an input graph to alter a model's prediction to a predefined output. Although counterfactual explainers that support modifying the graph by both adding and removing edges have recently emerged, there is still a lack of general and efficient methods, especially when considering the quality of the generated explanations. Moreover, the problem remains far from solved, as existing methods exhibit different strengths and weaknesses, often trading off between explanation size, coverage and quality. For this reason, it is important to identify where each method performs well and where it falls short, so as to guide future research in the field. Thus, our study compares six state-of-the-art (SOTA) models on a diverse set of real-world and synthetic datasets, covering both binary and multi-class graph and node classification tasks, and evaluates their performance using diverse quantitative and qualitative metrics.

</details>


### [168] [Single-Query Black-Box Calibration Auditing via Logit Bias](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05125)
*Roman Plaud,Antoine Saillenfest,Matthieu Labeau,Thomas Bonald,Willem Waegeman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Researchers introduce a method to evaluate LLM calibration using only API logit_bias parameters when continuous probabilities are hidden, enabling single-query calibration error estimation for black-box models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Commercial LLM APIs increasingly hide continuous output probabilities needed for standard calibration metrics, making it difficult to assess model reliability and safety for deployment as zero-shot classifiers.

Method: The authors demonstrate that any LLM API exposing a logit_bias parameter can be mathematically manipulated to evaluate exact probability thresholds using just one query per sample. This enables construction of a provably consistent estimator of True Calibration Error for binary tasks.

Result: Developed a novel framework for auditing black-box foundation models that works efficiently with limited API access (one query per sample) while providing mathematically rigorous calibration error estimation.

Conclusion: The proposed method provides a practical solution for evaluating LLM calibration in real-world deployment scenarios where providers hide detailed probability outputs, enabling safer deployment of black-box foundation models.

Abstract: Evaluating the calibration of Large Language Models (LLMs) is critical for their safe deployment as zero-shot classifiers. Yet, commercial API providers increasingly hide the continuous output probabilities required by standard calibration metrics. To bypass this opacity, we demonstrate that any LLM API exposing a logit\_bias parameter can be mathematically manipulated to evaluate exact probability thresholds using strictly one query per sample. Leveraging this mechanism, we introduce a novel and provably consistent estimator of the True Calibration Error for binary tasks. Our approach therefore provides an efficient framework for auditing black-box foundation models.

</details>


### [169] [Coarse-Graining Hidden Representations: Unsupervised Neuron Selection via Mapping Entropy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05126)
*Margherita Mele,Andrea Castagna,Roberto Menichetti,Raffaello Potestio,Alessandro Ingrosso*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces mapping entropy as an unsupervised method to select informative neurons in overparameterized neural networks, achieving better performance than random selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overparameterized neural networks have many more hidden units than needed, raising the question of which neurons are essential and whether this distinction can be identified without labels or gradients.

Method: Neuron selection is framed as coarse-graining the hidden layer by retaining a subset of neurons. The method uses mapping entropy (ME) to measure the loss of discriminatory power when discarding neurons, then selects the subset that minimizes ME based only on hidden-activation statistics.

Result: In teacher-student networks, ME optimization recovers minimal teacher-consistent representations; on non-linear Gaussian process tasks, it selects coherent functional-class mappings; on MNIST with translation augmentation, ME-selected subnetworks outperform random subsets of equal size, especially under strong compression.

Conclusion: Mapping entropy provides an effective unsupervised approach for neuron selection that links configurational distinguishability to predictive performance, performing best under strong compression.

Abstract: Overparameterized neural networks carry far more hidden units than a task nominally requires, raising the question of which neurons are essential and whether that distinction is legible in the representation itself, without labels or gradients. We cast neuron selection as the problem of coarse-graining the hidden layer by retaining a subset of its neurons, and score each putative selection by the mapping entropy (ME). This quantity measures the loss of discriminatory power inherent in discarding part of the network neurons, and the selection that minimises the ME is taken as particularly informative. This criterion is fully unsupervised, in that it depends only on hidden-activation statistics. In teacher-student networks, ME optimisation recovers the minimal teacher-consistent representation and retains extra units in proportion to the hidden layer's residual variability; in a non-linear Gaussian process task, it selects coherent functional-class mappings whose preferred class shifts across training. On this task and on translation-augmented MNIST, ME-selected subnetworks outperform random subsets of equal size, most clearly under strong compression - linking configurational distinguishability to predictive performance.

</details>


### [170] [MomentQuant: an even more minimalist interval method with linear time complexity for time series classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05136)
*Johann Faouzi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A faster version of Quant time series classification algorithm using optimized implementation and approximate quantiles via Cornish-Fisher expansion, achieving significant speed improvements with minimal accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time series classification is important in many applications, and while the Quant algorithm achieves good accuracy and speed, there's room for further optimization. Faster algorithms are particularly valuable for real-world applications where inference happens more frequently than training.

Method: Two improvements to Quant: 1) Better optimized implementation of the original algorithm, 2) Replacing exact quantiles with approximate ones using Cornish-Fisher expansion (eliminating sorting requirements and reducing computational complexity). The new algorithm is called MomentQuant.

Result: The optimized Quant implementation is faster than the original, and MomentQuant is even faster than this optimized version. The speed improvements come at the cost of only a tiny decrease in predictive performance.

Conclusion: The proposed improvements make Quant-based time series classification algorithms more efficient, which is especially beneficial for real-life applications where fast inference is crucial.

Abstract: Time series data is very common in many real-world applications and in numerous domains, with increasing interest for automated information extraction using machine learning. One of these subfields is time series classification, which consists in assigning a label to each new, unseen time series. Many algorithms have been developed over the past decades, with the trade-off between predictive performance and computational cost being consistently discussed. Quant, an interval-based algorithm extracting quantiles from recursive, fixed, dyadic intervals, was shown to achieve high accuracy, while being very fast. We propose two changes to make this algorithm even faster. The first one is a better optimized implementation of the exact same algorithm. The second one is to derive approximate quantiles, using the Cornish-Fisher expansion, instead of exact quantiles. This change removes the necessity to sort the time series, leading to a smaller computational complexity. We call this novel algorithm MomentQuant. We provide evidence that our implementation of Quant is faster than the original one, and that MomentQuant is even faster than our implementation of Quant, at the cost of a tiny decrease in predictive performance. These improvements are especially relevant for real-life applications, where inference is performed much more often than training.

</details>


### [171] [From 80x to 385x: A Best-Matching-Unit Search at the L2 Roof, Measured Against a Symmetrically Tuned Baseline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05138)
*Andrew James Amos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A systematic tuning program for both a novel SOM algorithm (SparseBin) and its baseline (cuSPARSE) achieved dramatic speedups through four optimization levers, with SparseBin reaching 5.6-10.1x speedup and 385x over reference implementations, while cuSPARSE improved 2-3x, hitting hardware bandwidth limits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GPU algorithm comparisons are typically asymmetric - one side is optimized by its author while the other runs as-is. This creates unfair comparisons that don't reveal the true potential of baseline implementations.

Method: Implemented a tuning program that systematically optimized both the novel SparseBin algorithm (for self-organizing maps) and the baseline cuSPARSE algorithm using four optimization levers: tile size, tile-membership clustering, neuron-axis chunking, and vectorized loads.

Result: SparseBin achieved 5.6-10.1x speedup per epoch over previous configurations across map sizes from 32x32 to 512x512, improving from ~80x to ~385x over earlier implementations. cuSPARSE improved 2-3x. The tuned kernel reached 77% of L2 bandwidth peak (others at 40-65%), bounding further optimizations to ~1.3x.

Conclusion: Systematic tuning of both novel and baseline algorithms produces fair comparisons and reveals performance ceilings. The work achieved near-terminal performance by hitting hardware bandwidth limits, demonstrating that asymmetric comparisons in GPU research can be misleading.

Abstract: Comparisons between GPU implementations are usually asymmetric: one side is tuned by its author, the other is run as found. I report a programme that tuned both a novel SOM algorithm (SparseBin) and the baseline algorithm it was being compared to (cuSPARSE). The best-matching-unit search that dominates self-organizing map training was tuned through four levers - tile size, tile-membership clustering, neuron-axis chunking and vectorised loads - reaching 5.6-10.1x per epoch over the previously published configuration at map sizes from 32x32 to 512x512, and lifting the margin over the CUDA implementation behind our earlier MEDLINE atlases from ~80x to ~385x. cuSPARSE, the implementation SparseBin is compared against, received every lever with an analogue on its side, and became 2-3x faster in the process. The tuned kernel pressed the L2 bandwidth roof at 77% of peak with every other unit at 40-65%, bounding any further lever at ~1.3x - a terminal result rather than a waypoint, and every untested lever was either capped by that bound by construction or measured null.

</details>


### [172] [Beyond Stationarity in Time Series: Discovering Causal Structures and Latent Regimes via Markov Blankets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05150)
*Lei Zan,Charles K. Assaad,Emilie Devijver,Eric Gaussier*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RCBNB-MB is a novel causal discovery algorithm for time series that handles regime changes by identifying stable causal structures within different time segments, outperforming baselines in detecting both regime transitions and causal relationships.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world time series often exhibit regime changes that violate the common assumption of a single, time-consistent causal structure, which limits the applicability of existing causal discovery methods in dynamic systems.

Method: RCBNB-MB follows an iterative strategy that segments time series into latent causal regimes (subsets where stable causal structure holds) and discovers causal graphs within each regime using Markov blankets rather than direct parents for robustness.

Result: The algorithm provides theoretical guarantees for recovering regime transitions and causal graphs, and empirical validation shows it systematically outperforms baseline approaches on both simulated datasets with ground truth and real-world IT monitoring data.

Conclusion: RCBNB-MB is a robust and versatile framework for non-stationary time series analysis that effectively addresses regime changes while maintaining accurate causal discovery, positioning it as a significant advance in time series causal inference.

Abstract: This paper introduces Regime-aware Constraint-Based and Noise-Based causal discovery with Markov Blankets (RCBNB-MB), a novel causal discovery algorithm for time series that relaxes the common assumption of a single, time-consistent causal structure. Time series are typically observed at discrete time points and often exhibit regime changes that challenge the assumption of a static causal structure, a limitation in many real-world dynamic systems. To address this challenge, RCBNB-MB identifies latent causal regimes, defined as subsets of time points within which a stable causal structure holds. The algorithm follows an iterative strategy that segments the time series into regimes and discovers the causal graph within each regime. By leveraging the Markov blanket rather than direct parents, RCBNB-MB gains robustness to errors in causal discovery and preserves predictive information. We provide theoretical guarantees for RCBNB-MB's ability to recover both regime transitions and causal graphs under reasonable assumptions. Furthermore, we validate its effectiveness through extensive experiments on simulated datasets with known ground truth and real-world IT monitoring data, where taking into account regime shifts is critical. Empirical results show that RCBNB-MB systematically outperforms baseline approaches in accurately detecting regime changes and their associated causal graphs, positioning it as a robust and versatile framework for non-stationary time series analysis.

</details>


### [173] [Phase Transition Frequency as a Training Time Predictor of Test Accuracy in ResNets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05194)
*Arunan J*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The number of class-separability jumps during ResNet finetuning correlates negatively with final test accuracy on standard benchmarks, but this relationship weakens under distributional stress.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To identify simple, inexpensive training-time signals that can predict final test accuracy, particularly examining class-separability jumps during finetuning as a potential probe for in-distribution training quality.

Method: Empirical analysis across 75 experiments spanning four benchmarks (CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, TinyImageNet, CIFAR-10-C) with three ResNet architectures (ResNet-18, 50, 101) and multiple seeds per configuration. Used correlation analysis, partial correlation controlling for architecture depth, and comparison against six alternative training-curve signals.

Result: Strong negative correlations on standard i.i.d. benchmarks: r = -0.84 on CIFAR-10 and r = -0.87 on CIFAR-100. Weaker correlations under distributional stress: r = -0.45 on TinyImageNet and r = -0.19 on CIFAR-10-C. Transition count shows significant predictive power even when controlling for architecture depth, and performs competitively against alternative training-curve signals on clean datasets.

Conclusion: Class-separability jump count is a promising inexpensive in-distribution training-quality probe that can be logged during standard training, though its predictive power diminishes under distribution shift. More comprehensive comparisons with other literature metrics remain future work.

Abstract: The number of discrete class-separability jumps observed during ResNet finetuning is examined empirically as a predictor of final test accuracy. Across 75 experiments spanning four benchmarks (CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, TinyImageNet, and CIFAR-10-C) and three architectures (ResNet-18, ResNet-50, and ResNet-101), with five to ten seeds per configuration, a strong within-dataset negative correlation is obtained on standard i.i.d. classification benchmarks: \(r = -0.84\) on CIFAR-10 (\(p < 10^{-8}\), \(n = 30\)) and \(r = -0.87\) on CIFAR-100 (\(p < 10^{-5}\), \(n = 15\)). Under distributional stress, the relationship attenuates: TinyImageNet yields \(r = -0.45\), and the CIFAR-10-C corruption benchmark yields \(r = -0.19\). Two additional analyses discipline the empirical claim. A partial correlation controlling for architecture depth, treated as a linear covariate, shows that on CIFAR-100 the transition count retains statistically significant predictive power (\(r_{\mathrm{partial}} = -0.69\), \(p = 0.007\)); the corresponding result under the stricter categorical conditioning is not established at \(n = 15\). A comparison against six alternative training-curve signals shows that transition count achieved the strongest correlation among the evaluated signals on CIFAR-100 and one of the strongest on CIFAR-10, but is dominated by other signals on the two stressed benchmarks. The comparison is restricted to training-curve-level signals; comparisons against effective rank, Hessian sharpness, Fisher information, margin, and neural-collapse measures, which are the strongest competitors in the current literature, are not part of the present study and remain open. The observation is presented as an in-distribution training-quality probe among a family of candidate probes, and an inexpensive detection procedure suitable for logging alongside a standard training loop is provided.

</details>


### [174] [Dimension-Adaptive Batched Lipschitz Narrowing Without Knowing the Zooming Dimension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05214)
*Yasong Feng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents Count-Adaptive BLiN algorithm for bandit learning that removes the dependence on zooming dimension dz while achieving optimal batch complexity with O(log log T) batches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous A-BLiN algorithm depended on the zooming dimension dz, which may be unknown in practice. This limitation motivates developing an algorithm that doesn't require dz or zooming constant Cz while maintaining performance guarantees.

Method: Develops Count-Adaptive BLiN algorithm that selects next edge length based on the number of cubes surviving previous elimination, eliminating dependence on dz. Uses adaptive batch selection without requiring zooming parameters.

Result: Algorithm achieves Ōd(T^{(dz+1)/(dz+2)}) regret with Od(log log T) batches without using dz or Cz. Combined with adaptive-grid lower bound, establishes Θd(log log T) optimal batch complexity when dz is unknown.

Conclusion: Count-Adaptive BLiN successfully removes dependence on zooming dimension while maintaining optimal batch complexity, solving the problem of unknown dz in adaptive bandit algorithms.

Abstract: The Appropriately Combined Edge-length (ACE) sequence in A-BLiN depends on the zooming dimension $d_z$. This note removes that dependence. The next edge length is selected from the number of cubes that survive the preceding elimination. The resulting Count-Adaptive BLiN algorithm does not use $d_z$ or the zooming constant $C_z$, yet it attains $\widetilde{\mathcal O}_d(T^{(d_z+1)/(d_z+2)})$ regret with $\mathcal O_d(\log\log T)$ batches. Together with the adaptive-grid lower bound in Theorem 10 of the original paper, the optimal batch complexity remains $Θ_d(\log\log T)$ when $d_z$ is unknown.

</details>


### [175] [FedDRAW: Federated Dual Reputation Annealing Weighting for Heterogeneous Multi-Institutional Chest Radiograph Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05223)
*Maryam Moradpour,Anne-Christin Hauschild*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedDRAW: A federated learning aggregation method that dynamically combines data size and model similarity with dual annealing schedules to improve medical diagnosis models without centralizing data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated learning in medicine faces aggregation challenges where FedAvg weights clients by sample count alone, causing small but informative hospitals to have minimal influence while larger clients may dominate despite being less informative, potentially biasing diagnostic models.

Method: FedDRAW combines data-size prior with cosine similarity between client and global parameters using two coupled annealing schedules: inner schedule shifts client reputation from size prior towards similarity; outer deferred annealing on softmax inverse temperature keeps weighting selective early/mid rounds and relaxes to uniformity at convergence.

Result: FedDRAW achieved highest average rank among 8 methods (including 7 baselines) on 12 simulated client-partition scenarios of chest radiograph datasets under both AUC and geometric mean of sensitivity/specificity, with statistical significance confirmed by Friedman test with Nemenyi post-hoc analysis.

Conclusion: Scheduling two signals (data size and model similarity) rather than fixing weights by sample count alone could enable less biased diagnostic models in federated medical AI applications.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence models are promising for medical diagnosis, but they require large numbers of unbiased data, which in medicine are distributed across hospitals and cannot be centralized to protect patient privacy. Federated Learning (FL) addresses this, since hospitals train one shared diagnostic model while patient data remain local. Training proceeds in communication rounds, in which each hospital trains the shared model locally and returns it to the server for merging by weighted average. This aggregation weight determines whose institutional knowledge shapes the result. Federated averaging (FedAvg) sets it in proportion to local sample count, so a small but informative hospital is permanently assigned a small influence, andl argest clients could dominate the global model even when they are less informative. We propose Federated Dual Reputation Annealing Weighting (FedDRAW), a server-side aggregation method that combines a data-size prior with the cosine similarity between client and global parameters under two coupled annealing schedules. An inner schedule shifts client reputation from the size prior towards similarity. An outer, deferred annealing schedule on the softmax inverse temperature keeps the weighting selective in the early and middle rounds and relaxes it to uniformity at convergence. We evaluate FedDRAW on 12 simulated client-partition scenarios of two chest radiograph datasets (CheXpert and ChestMNIST), against seven federated baselines under identical local training settings. FedDRAW achieved the highest average rank among all eight methods under both AUC and the geometric mean (GM) of sensitivity and specificity, which a Friedman test with Nemenyi post-hoc analysis confirmed to be a statistically significant difference between the methods. Scheduling two signals, rather than fixing the weights by sample count alone, could enable less biased diagnostic models.

</details>


### [176] [Hessian-based molecular conformation augmentation for a scalable and efficient strategy of machine learning interatomic potentials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05233)
*Bumju Kwak,Jeonghee Jo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel data augmentation method for machine learning interatomic potentials that enhances Hessian prediction without architectural changes or computational overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard MLIPs are trained only on energy and forces, ignoring Hessian information crucial for vibrational analysis and transition state search.
