Existing Hessian-aware methods require architectural modifications and incur significant computational/memory overhead from higher-order backpropagation.

Method: Proposes two Hessian-derived data augmentation schemes: UniAug (isotropic Gaussian displacement) and ModeAug (normal mode-weighted displacement). Both use simple Taylor expansions to generate augmented data without changing training objectives or extending autograd graphs, enabling plug-and-play integration with existing architectures.

Result: Comprehensive evaluations across non-equilibrium and equilibrium datasets show the approach enhances model accuracy while providing practical, task-specific guidelines for implementation.

Conclusion: The proposed data augmentation methods effectively incorporate Hessian information into MLIP training without computational overhead or architectural modifications, addressing a key limitation in current approaches while maintaining compatibility with existing pipelines.

Abstract: While machine-learning interatomic potentials (MLIPs) have successfully learned potential energy surfaces (PES) and atomic forces, many practical applications, such as vibrational analysis and transition state search, rely heavily on the PES Hessian. Yet, standard MLIPs tend to be trained on energy and forces alone, leaving Hessian information largely unexploited. Meanwhile, existing methods that explicitly incorporate the Hessian into training objectives require architectural modifications and introduce significant computational and memory overheads due to higher-order backpropagation. To address these limitations, we propose two Hessian-derived data augmentation schemes: isotropic Gaussian displacement (\textbf{UniAug}) and normal mode-weighted displacement (\textbf{ModeAug}). Both methods utilize simple Taylor expansions, achieving effective augmentation without altering training objectives or extending the autograd graph. This allows seamless, plug-and-play integration with existing architectures and training pipelines. Comprehensive evaluations across non-equilibrium and equilibrium datasets demonstrate that our approach enhances model accuracy while providing practical, task-specific guidelines.

</details>


### [177] [PRICE: A Systematic Study of LLM Adaptation Choices for Bitcoin Price Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05235)
*Maryam Fakhari,Mehran Safayani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PRICE introduces a structured LLM adaptation approach for Bitcoin price forecasting, combining LoRA fine-tuning, recursive inference, integer representation, CTF prompting, and zero-temperature decoding to achieve superior forecasting accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cryptocurrency markets exhibit extreme volatility and non-stationary dynamics that challenge conventional forecasting methods. Although LLMs show promise for time series forecasting, the combined effects of adaptation choices remain largely unexplored in financial settings.

Method: PRICE is built on 4-bit quantized LLaMA-3 8B model with five key components: Parameter-efficient fine-tuning with LoRA, Recursive multi-step inference, Integer-rounded numerical representation, Context-Task-Format (CTF) prompting, and Exact zero-temperature decoding. Ablation studies validate each component's contribution.

Result: PRICE achieves the lowest forecasting errors on both validation and test sets compared to eight transformer-based and time-series foundation models. Despite using a text-pretrained model, it achieves competitive or superior performance relative to specialized foundation models, maintaining robust performance across evaluation periods.

Conclusion: Adaptation choices critically determine the accuracy and robustness of LLMs for numerical time-series forecasting. The structured approach demonstrates that properly adapted LLMs can outperform specialized models even when primarily pretrained on text rather than time-series data.

Abstract: Cryptocurrency markets exhibit extreme volatility and non-stationary dynamics that challenge conventional forecasting methods. Although Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown promise for time series forecasting, the combined effects of adaptation choices remain largely unexplored in financial settings. This study introduces PRICE, a structured approach for adapting LLMs to short-term Bitcoin price forecasting. Built on a 4-bit quantized LLaMA-3 8B model, PRICE investigates how fine-tuning, numerical representation, prompting, inference, and decoding jointly influence forecasting performance. PRICE integrates Parameter-efficient fine-tuning with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), Recursive multi-step inference, Integer-rounded numerical representation, Context-Task-Format (CTF) prompting, and Exact zero-temperature decoding. Ablation studies show that each component contributes to forecasting accuracy and reliability. LoRA enables efficient training on limited hardware, recursive inference improves accuracy, integer-rounded values reduce errors, CTF prompting outperforms Chain-of-Thought, Implicit Chain-of-Thought (iCoT), and few-shot prompting, and zero-temperature decoding improves stability during recursive forecasting. Comparative evaluation against eight transformer-based and time-series foundation models shows that PRICE achieves the lowest forecasting errors on both validation and test sets while maintaining robust performance across evaluation periods. Despite being based on a model primarily pretrained on text rather than time-series data, PRICE achieves competitive or superior performance relative to specialized foundation models. These findings demonstrate that adaptation choices critically determine the accuracy and robustness of LLMs for numerical time-series forecasting.

</details>


### [178] [GLASS: Graph-Language Alignment with Spherical Scoring for Transferable Graph-Level Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05253)
*Xudong Wang,Chris Ding,Tongxin Li,Jicong Fan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GLASS is a graph-level anomaly detection framework that achieves cross-domain transferability through graph-language alignment on a hypersphere, enabling zero-shot and few-shot anomaly detection across domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the challenge of cross-domain graph-level anomaly detection (GLAD) where models typically struggle to transfer knowledge across different domains due to domain-specific graph structures and properties. Current methods lack robust transferability and require substantial target-domain data for adaptation.

Method: GLASS builds a unified representation space by aligning a structure-aware graph encoder with an instruction-aware text embedding via multi-slice soft cosine objective. It serializes graph properties into a compact Graph Descriptor Prompt (GraphDP), uses Matryoshka representation slices for multi-scale consistency, and employs Spherical Multi-Modal Scoring with von Mises-Fisher kernel density estimators for anomaly detection in both graph and text spaces.

Result: The framework achieves the best average AUROC and rank across twelve benchmarks and three meta-domains compared with recent advanced GLAD baselines. It enables effective cross-domain transfer through zero-shot anomaly detection (encoding target domain's GraphDP without training data) and few-shot adaptation with minimal normal examples.

Conclusion: GLASS provides a principled approach for robust cross-domain graph-level anomaly detection through graph-language alignment, establishing a text-based bridge for domain-agnostic anomaly scoring and enabling practical applications with minimal data requirements.

Abstract: We introduce GLASS, a framework for graph-level anomaly detection (GLAD) that achieves robust cross-domain transferability through graph-language alignment on the unit hypersphere. GLASS builds a unified representation space by aligning a structure-aware graph encoder with an instruction-aware text embedding via a multi-slice soft cosine objective. Our framework serializes local, global, and semantic graph properties into a compact Graph Descriptor Prompt (GraphDP), creating a text bridge that enables domain-agnostic anomaly scoring. By enforcing multi-scale consistency through Matryoshka representation slices, the model captures anomalous deviations at multiple levels of granularity. For scoring, we formulate anomaly detection as density estimation on the aligned hypersphere and introduce Spherical Multi-Modal Scoring (SMS), which instantiates von Mises-Fisher kernel density estimators in both graph and text embedding spaces. This probabilistic formulation recovers angular k-nearest-neighbor scoring as a high-concentration limiting case and provides a principled fusion of structural and semantic anomaly signals. The shared text embedding space further serves as a cross-domain bridge: by encoding a target domain's GraphDP without target-domain training data, GLASS performs zero-shot anomaly detection, and with only a handful of normal examples, few-shot adaptation via reference-set calibration. Across twelve benchmarks and three meta-domains, GLASS obtains the best average AUROC and rank compared with recent advanced GLAD baselines and enables effective cross-domain transfer.

</details>


### [179] [How to Speculate about Uncertainty in Agentic Coding? A Draft-Model Gate Method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05274)
*Konstantin Grotov,Valentin Malykh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Speculative Uncertainty (SU) is a method that predicts failure likelihood for black-box LLM agents by analyzing their output tokens using a small draft model, enabling pre-execution error detection and reducing deployment costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents in software engineering often fail expensively with confident but wrong actions that are only discovered after costly execution and retry cycles, creating a need for predictive failure signals before execution.

Method: Inverts speculative decoding: uses a small open-weight draft model to score an agent's already-generated trajectory in a single forward pass, extracts phase-aware features by separating reasoning and action spans, and calibrates them against verifiable objectives to produce failure-likelihood scores.

Result: SU reduces execution error rate by 6-8 percentage points and token cost by 14-19% on software engineering agents like Qwen3-Coder-480B and Claude 3.5 Sonnet, transfers to out-of-distribution benchmarks without retraining, and generalizes across agent models.

Conclusion: Speculative Uncertainty provides an effective, actionable failure prediction signal for black-box LLM agents that can be consumed by downstream policies like routing, human intervention, or veto gates, significantly reducing deployment costs and errors while maintaining generalizability.

Abstract: LLM agents deployed for software engineering fail expensively: they act confidently wrong, and bad actions are recognized only after costly execution and retry. We present Speculative Uncertainty (SU), a method that recovers a predictive failure signal for a black-box agent from its output tokens alone, with no access to logits, weights, activations, or repeated sampling. Inverting speculative decoding, a small open-weight draft model scores the agent's already-generated trajectory in a single forward pass. From these speculative cross-likelihoods we extract phase-aware features by separating the reasoning and action spans, and calibrate them against a verifiable objective. SU produces a failure-likelihood score that any downstream policy, such as routing, human intervention, or extra test-time compute, can consume directly. To show the signal is actionable, we instantiate one such policy, a pre-execution veto gate, on software engineering agents Qwen3-Coder-480B and closed-source Claude 3.5 Sonnet, cutting execution error rate by 6-8 percentage points and token cost by 14-19% in deployment, transferring to out-of-distribution benchmarks without retraining, and generalizing across agent models.

</details>


### [180] [Learning from VAE Errors to support ECG-based Differential Diagnosis of Myocardial Scar](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05294)
*Shayan Sharifi,Riccardo Treu,Ilaria Gandin,Federico Garoia,Marco Merlo,Giulia Cisotto*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A β-VAE trained on normal ECGs can reconstruct scar-altered ECGs with higher DTW errors, providing discriminative features for LGE+ detection that complement foundation model features.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LGE cardiac MRI is a gold standard for myocardial scar detection but has limited accessibility, motivating the need for routine ECG-based screening alternatives.

Method: Compared 32-dimensional features from the foundation ECGx.AI model with those from a shallower β-VAE trained on normal PTB-XL ECGs. Evaluated downstream classification with Random Forest and Gradient Boosting, and analyzed Dynamic Time Warping (DTW)-based reconstruction errors from the β-VAE.

Result: ECGx.AI reached AUROC 0.686 with Random Forest, while the proposed β-VAE reached 0.577 with 0.775 sensitivity using Gradient Boosting. DTW reconstruction errors significantly differed between LGE+ and LGE- classes in 10/12 leads and achieved AUROC 0.643 with Logistic Regression.

Conclusion: β-VAE reconstruction errors on scar-altered ECGs provide discriminative features for LGE detection, offering a complementary approach to foundation models for ECG-based myocardial scar screening.

Abstract: Late Gadolinium Enhancement (LGE) on cardiac magnetic resonance is a key marker of myocardial scar, but its limited accessibility motivates routine ECG-based screening. We evaluated whether $β$-variational autoencoder (VAE)-derived ECG representations can discriminate LGE+ from LGE- cardiomyopathic patients in a local cohort of 300 subjects. We compared 32-dimensional features from the foundation ECGx.AI model with those from a shallower $β$-VAE trained on normal PTB-XL ECGs, evaluating downstream classification and Dynamic Time Warping (DTW)-based reconstruction errors. ECGx.AI reached an area under ROC of 0.686 with Random Forest, while the proposed $β$-VAE reached 0.577 with sensitivity of 0.775 with Gradient Boosting. Notably, DTW-reconstruction errors significantly differed between classes in 10 out of 12 leads according to Mann-Whitney U test and help in classification, leading to an area under ROC of 0.643 with Logistic Regression, supporting their potential as markers of scar-related ECG alterations.

</details>


### [181] [How Does mHC Use Its Residual Streams? Selective Routing and Near-Identity Mixing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05309)
*Pengxiang Zhao,Xing Li,Xianzhi Yu,Wei Guo,Zhenhua Dong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how DeepSeek-V4-Flash's four-stream hyper-connection architecture is actually used, finding that blocks rarely use all four streams and late mixing provides little benefit, suggesting the model doesn't fully utilize its architectural flexibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While hyper-connections (HC) and manifold-constrained variants (mHC) theoretically expand residual pathways from one stream to n streams, it's unclear how trained models actually utilize this capacity - specifically, how blocks route information across streams, how much residual mixing occurs, and whether streams maintain distinct representations.

Method: The authors examine DeepSeek-V4-Flash's four-stream residual pathway using three analytical techniques: effective stream counts, cross-stream residual weights, and inter-stream cosine similarity. They also perform targeted interventions including replacing late/early mixers with identity matrices, fixing early mixers to their diagnostic means, and pruning routing weights.

Result: Analysis shows read/write routing is concentrated (typically using ~2 streams per site) with streams remaining directionally distinct. Residual mixing is modest and mostly occurs in early layers. Late mixing provides minimal benefit - replacing late mixers with identity only increases perplexity by 1.9% and preserves task performance, while replacing early mixers causes 41% perplexity increase.

Conclusion: DeepSeek-V4-Flash doesn't fully utilize the flexibility of its four-stream mHC architecture. Individual blocks rarely require all four streams, and late residual mixing provides little measurable benefit, suggesting potential for architectural simplification or optimization.

Abstract: Hyper-Connections and their manifold-constrained variant mHC widen a residual pathway from one stream to n, yet how trained models use this capacity remains unclear: how broadly blocks read and write, how strongly the residual pathway mixes streams, and whether the streams carry distinct representations. We examine these properties in the four-stream residual pathway of DeepSeek-V4-Flash using effective stream counts, cross-stream residual weights, and inter-stream cosine similarity. Read/write routing is concentrated but varies across depth: a typical attention or FFN site effectively uses about two streams, while the dominant stream changes across layers and the representations remain directionally distinct. Residual mixing is modest and occurs primarily in early layers; in layers 22-42, the pathway mostly carries each stream forward separately. Targeted interventions establish the functional significance of these patterns. Replacing the late mixers by identity increases C4 perplexity by only 1.9% and preserves the six-task average score, whereas replacing the early mixers increases perplexity by 41%. Fixing each early mixer to its C4 diagnostic mean increases perplexity by only 0.2% and reduces the average score by 0.25 percentage points, showing that its site-specific structure matters more than its token-wise variation on the evaluated metrics. Likewise, retaining the three largest routing weights per token at every site increases perplexity by at most 2.7% and changes the average score by at most 0.4 points. Thus, the studied model realizes only part of the flexibility afforded by four-stream mHC: individual blocks rarely require all four streams, and late residual mixing provides little measured benefit.

</details>


### [182] [Optimal Rates for Agentic Networked Information Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05318)
*MohammadHossein Bateni,Zahra Hadizadeh,MohammadTaghi Hajiaghayi,Mahdi JafariRaviz,Shayan Taherijam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper closes the gap in understanding information aggregation in networked learning by proving optimal Θ(M²/D) excess error rates for regression and extending results to logistic classification, showing geometric error contraction for fixed distributions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To study information aggregation in networked learning models that capture agentic AI patterns where agents see only partial data and pass conclusions forward, building on prior work by Kearns, Roth, and Ryu that left gaps in understanding optimal error rates.

Method: Analyzes linear regression with MSE loss in DAG networks where agents see subsets of features and parent predictions. Uses sharper analysis of cyclic instances, constructs new M-covered path instances, proves geometric contraction for fixed distributions, and extends results to logistic classification via equivalence analysis.

Result: Proves the correct excess error rate is constant up to depth M² and Θ(M²/D) beyond it, closing the gap between previous O(M/√D) upper bound and Ω(M/D) lower bound. Shows regression lower bounds transfer to logistic classification, establishing optimal O(M²/D) bounds for both problems.

Conclusion: The paper provides a complete characterization of optimal error rates in networked learning for both regression and classification, demonstrating that information aggregation achieves geometric convergence for fixed distributions and establishing fundamental limits for agentic AI systems.

Abstract: Building on the pioneering paper of Kearns, Roth, and Ryu (SODA'26), we study information aggregation in a networked learning model. The model captures a central pattern in agentic AI: each agent sees only part of the data and passes on only its own conclusion. Their model considers a linear regression problem with the mean squared error (MSE) loss. Agents sit in a DAG and each sees only a subset of the features and its parents' predictions, fits a linear predictor, and passes only its prediction forward. The benchmark is the full-feature learner that sees all raw features. A path of depth $D$ is $M$-covered if every block of $M$ consecutive agents collectively sees all raw features. Kearns, Roth, and Ryu proved that the excess mean squared error of the last agent on such a path is $O(M/\sqrt D)$, and gave a cyclic instance with excess error $Ω(M/D)$ for $D<M^2$.
  We close this gap: the correct rate is constant up to depth $M^2$, and $Θ(M^2/D)$ beyond it. We first give a sharper analysis of the cyclic instance and improve its lower bound to $Ω(\sqrt{M/D})$ for $D<M^2$. We then construct, for every depth $D\ge M^2$, an $M$-covered path of depth $D$ with excess error $Ω(M^2/D)$. The same instance gives the constant lower bound for all $D < M^2$. We also show that for any fixed distribution the excess error contracts geometrically along the path, ruling out any single instance that witnesses any polynomial lower bound at every depth.
  Finally, we prove the same optimal rate for logistic classification in the logit-passing model of Bateni et al., which considers the binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss. The same improved upper bound of $O(M^2/D)$ holds, and we transfer all the regression lower bounds by showing that on those examples the logistic path follows the least-squares path up to rescaling.

</details>


### [183] [Embedded Graph Flows for Categorical Graph Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05328)
*Ethan Ma,Zihan Wang,Chris Siu Yeung Chow,Xinguo Feng,Qingqing Li,Rui Jiang,Naipeng Dong,Guangdong Bai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EGF learns continuous embeddings for graph categories and generates graphs using a permutation-equivariant transformer, achieving state-of-the-art performance on molecular benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph generators use fixed one-hot vectors for node and edge categories, which imposes artificial geometry where categories are treated as equidistant. This approach fails to capture meaningful relationships between categories and depends on node order.

Method: Embedded Graph Flows (EGF) learns continuous embeddings for node and unordered-edge categories, uses a permutation-equivariant graph transformer to transport Gaussian noise towards learned endpoints, and applies a terminal readout to map embeddings back to discrete graph categories.

Result: On QM9 benchmark, EGF achieved best results on all four metrics among three methods, with FCD of 0.150 (vs 0.717 for DiGress and 0.812 for GruM). On ZINC250k, EGF retained lowest MMD using NSPDK, indicating close agreement with reference molecules' local substructures.

Conclusion: EGF demonstrates that learning continuous embeddings for graph categories with permutation-equivariant architecture leads to superior generative performance on molecular datasets compared to fixed categorical approaches.

Abstract: Generating categorical graphs requires choosing node and edge types that form a coherent structure without depending on node order. Many graph generators encode categories as fixed one-hot vectors, which can impose an artificial geometry in which categories are equidistant. We propose Embedded Graph Flows (EGF), a generative model that learns continuous embeddings for node and unordered-edge categories and transports Gaussian noise towards these learnt endpoints using a permutation-equivariant graph transformer. A terminal readout maps the embeddings back to discrete graph categories. Across molecular benchmarks, EGF achieved competitive performance. On QM9, EGF gives the best result on all four reported metrics among the three methods, including a Fréchet ChemNet Distance (FCD) of 0.150, compared with 0.717 for the categorical-diffusion baseline DiGress and 0.812 for the bridge-based baseline GruM. When applied to larger molecules in ZINC250k, EGF retains the lowest maximum mean discrepancy (MMD) using the neighbourhood subgraph pairwise distance kernel (NSPDK), indicating close agreement with the local substructures of the reference molecules. Our code is available at https://github.com/Trusted-System-Lab/EGF.

</details>


### [184] [Variational Continuation for Double Pendulum Periodic Orbits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05337)
*Leo Yao,Ziming Liu,Max Tegmark*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A Hessian-based approach using automatic differentiation to numerically continue periodic orbits in dynamical systems, eliminating the need for hand-derived Jacobians and providing efficient detection of orbit families and bifurcations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous methods for continuing periodic orbits rely on hand-derived Jacobians, which is cumbersome and requires manual mathematical work. There's a need for an automated approach that can efficiently handle complex dynamical systems like the double pendulum while detecting important features like orbit family intersections and bifurcations.

Method: Parametrizes periodic orbits (loops) as Fourier series and defines a loss function based on deviation from physical differential equations. Uses automatic differentiation (a machine learning technique) instead of hand-derived Jacobians. Determines continuation direction via flat directions (zero eigenvalues) of the loss landscape. The method is integrator-free and can precisely initialize oscillations around unstable fixed points.

Result: Successfully demonstrates full continuations of periodic double pendulum oscillations from fixed points, showing bifurcations along orbit families and categorizing branches of periodic orbits. Finds novel periodic orbits where both pendulum masses are never simultaneously at rest, which was previously missing in literature.

Conclusion: The automated Hessian-based approach with automatic differentiation provides an efficient, guided method for searching periodic orbits, eliminating the need for manual Jacobian derivation while enabling discovery of previously undocumented orbit families in complex dynamical systems.

Abstract: We present a Hessian-based approach to numerically continue periodic orbits in dynamical systems. A loop (periodic orbit candidate) is parametrized as a Fourier series; a loss function is defined based on the deviation of the loop from the physical differential equations. Unlike previous work relying on hand-derived Jacobians, our method automates the process by leveraging automatic differentiation, a common machine learning technique. The continuation direction can be determined by the flat directions of the loss landscapes (directions with zero eigenvalues), making the search of periodic orbits efficient and guided. Our method is integrator-free, precisely initializes oscillations around unstable fixed points, and efficiently detects orbit family intersections and subharmonic bifurcations. As a demonstration, we present full continuations of periodic double pendulum oscillations from fixed points, showing bifurcations along orbit families and categorizing branches of periodic orbits. In particular, we find periodic orbits where both pendulum masses are never simultaneously at rest, which to our knowledge has been missing in the literature.

</details>


### [185] [Distill Globally, Adapt Locally: Reasoning Distillation and Product-Type Test-Time Training for Scalable Trade-Up Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05363)
*Siliang Liu,Mohammad Ghasemi,Sapan Patel,Amin Banitalebi-Dehkordi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Two-level framework distills LLM reasoning into efficient embedding classifier for trade-up recommendation, achieving 5000x speedup and 10000x cost reduction vs direct LLM inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs can reason about trade-up recommendations but are impractical for hundreds of millions of product pairs due to operational costs and computational overhead.

Method: Level 1: LLM teacher generates relation labels and rationales to supervise compact embedding-pair classifier via alignment/contrastive objectives. Level 2: Product-type test-time training uses few-shot demonstrations to optimize category-specific adapters over frozen student.

Result: 15.5M-parameter distilled student achieves AUC 0.924 vs 0.912 for label-only baseline; PT-TTT improves to AUC 0.941 and AP 0.940. 5000x faster and 10000x lower cost than direct LLM inference.

Conclusion: Two-level distillation framework enables practical, high-quality trade-up recommendation at scale by efficiently transferring LLM reasoning to lightweight models while adapting to product-type-specific criteria.

Abstract: Trade-up recommendation identifies higher-quality alternatives that preserve a customer's purchase intent while offering upgraded benefits. Large language models (LLMs) can reason about such distinctions, but applying them directly to hundreds of millions of product pairs is operationally impractical. We introduce a two-level framework that distills LLM reasoning into an efficient non-generative student and adapts its decision boundary to product-type-specific trade-up criteria. At Level 1, a retrieval-augmented few-shot LLM teacher generates structured relation labels and natural-language rationales. These rationales supervise a compact embedding-pair classifier through alignment and contrastive objectives; at inference, the student uses only two precomputed 768-dimensional product embeddings, with no LLM calls or text generation. On a fixed human-annotated benchmark of 8,352 pairs, a 15.5M-parameter four-class reasoning-distilled student achieves AUC 0.924 (95% CI [0.918, 0.929]), compared with 0.912 for the four-class label-only student. At Level 2, product-type test-time training (PT-TTT) uses few-shot demonstrations to optimize lightweight category-specific adapters over the frozen student. PT-TTT improves AUC from 0.924 to 0.941 and average precision from 0.920 to 0.940. On a 100K-pair proxy catalog, the distilled student on a single eight-GPU machine is approximately 5,000x faster and 10,000x lower in estimated cost than direct LLM inference.

</details>


### [186] [RegionFed: Federated Learning for Personalized Query Understanding in Heterogeneous Retail Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05403)
*Quoc H. Nguyen,Ali Lafzi,Abhijeet Phatak,Siddharth Pratap Singh,Rohit Upadhyay,Yogananda Domlur Seetharama,Chittaranjan Tripathy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RegionFed is a gradient-level federated learning framework that addresses data heterogeneity in retail search across diverse regions, avoiding catastrophic failure in transformers that plagues parameter-level personalization methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Retail search systems face significant data heterogeneity across geographic regions (different query patterns, vocabularies, product preferences), creating challenges for both privacy-preserving training and model personalization. Standard federated learning produces global models that sacrifice regional performance, while existing personalized FL approaches catastrophically collapse on modern transformers.

Method: RegionFed operates entirely at the gradient level, using ℓ₂ conflict between regional and global gradients as a unified signal to: 1) diagnose heterogeneity, 2) route each region to the cheapest sufficient personalization strategy, and 3) adaptively control personalization strength. It treats models as differentiable black boxes, enabling deployment on various architectures without code changes.

Result: RegionFed achieves 92.27% accuracy, closing the gap to the privacy-violating centralized upper bound (92.04%, Δ=0.23pp). It provides (ε≈0.60)-differential privacy with 𝒪(1/√T) convergence. The method works on T5-Small, T5-3B, RoBERTa, and CNN architectures, delivering large gains on transformers (where parameter-level methods collapse below 10% accuracy) and consistent improvements on CNNs.

Conclusion: RegionFed provides an architecture-robust federated learning framework that effectively addresses regional data heterogeneity in retail search systems while maintaining privacy. By operating at the gradient level rather than parameter level, it avoids catastrophic collapse in transformers and delivers near-centralized performance with provable privacy guarantees.

Abstract: Retail search systems serve diverse geographic regions with distinct query patterns, vocabularies, and product preferences, creating significant data heterogeneity that challenges both privacy-preserving training and model personalization. Federated learning offers a natural solution for privacy, but standard FL methods produce global models that sacrifice regional performance, while existing personalized FL approaches operate at the parameter level and catastrophically collapse on modern transformers (below 10\% accuracy on T5) due to tied embeddings and LayerNorm interactions. We introduce RegionFed, an \textit{architecture-robust} federated learning framework that sidesteps this failure by operating entirely at the gradient level. RegionFed uses the $\ell_2$ conflict between regional and global gradients as a unified signal that (i) diagnoses heterogeneity, (ii) routes each region to the cheapest sufficient personalization strategy, and (iii) adaptively controls personalization strength. Because it treats models as differentiable black boxes, RegionFed deploys on T5-Small, T5-3B, RoBERTa, and CNN with zero code changes, providing large gains on transformers (where parameter-level methods collapse) and consistent improvements on CNNs. Across three public datasets (Amazon ESCI, Amazon Reviews, LEAF-FEMNIST) and four architectures, RegionFed-Meta achieves 92.27\%, closing the gap to the privacy-violating centralized upper bound (Centralized + Regional Weighting: 92.04\%, $Δ$=0.23pp, within 1$σ$) while providing $(ε{\approx}0.60)$-differential privacy and $\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{T})$ convergence.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [187] [At Equal Inference Cost, Multi-Agent Structure Does Not Beat a Single Frozen Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04217)
*David Dylan,Aoife Brennan,Cian Murphy,Niamh O'Sullivan,Conor Kelly,Saoirse Walsh*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: Under equal inference cost budgets, evolving multi-agent LLM teams (Planner-Executor-Critic) doesn't provide clear benefits over evolving single agents, despite teams using more compute during evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent LLM pipelines show gains over single agents but use more inference calls per environment step, making comparisons unfair. The paper questions whether multi-agent teams still outperform single agents when total language-model calls are fixed.

Method: Introduces MA-Evolve, representing Planner-Executor-Critic teams as three evolvable role prompts optimized via per-role coordinate ascent over a shared frozen 7B backbone. Compares evolved single agents versus multi-agent teams under fixed total inference budgets.

Result: On ALFWorld: Single executor evolution significantly improves over baseline (0.769 vs 0.754), but full team is not statistically better despite using 1.8x more evaluation calls. Analysis shows value comes entirely from executor; planner and critic evolve to empty/low-impact prompts. With 2-3x extra compute, team only matches single agent. On WebShop, evolution null while team trends worse.

Conclusion: Under equal inference cost constraints, multi-agent structure adds computational overhead without clear performance benefits. The apparent gains in previous work come from unfair compute allocation rather than true architectural advantages.

Abstract: Multi-agent LLM pipelines, such as Planner-Executor-Critic teams, often report gains over single agents, but these gains usually come with higher inference cost because the team makes multiple model calls per environment step. Existing automated methods search over roles, topologies, and prompts, but typically compare teams against single agents at equal environment rollouts, giving the team extra compute. We instead fix the total number of language-model calls and ask whether evolving a multi-agent team still beats evolving a single agent under the same budget.
  We introduce MA-Evolve, which represents a Planner-Executor-Critic team as three evolvable role prompts and optimizes them by per-role coordinate ascent over a shared frozen 7B backbone. On ALFWorld, evolving a single executor significantly improves over the unevolved agent, while the full team achieves the highest mean but is not statistically better than the single agent: 0.769 versus 0.754, p = 0.80, despite using 1.8 times more evaluation calls. Leave-one-in analysis shows that the realized value comes entirely from the executor; the planner and critic evolve to empty or low-impact prompts and rarely change the executor's action. With 2-3 times free compute, the team only matches the single agent, and on WebShop evolution is null while the team trends worse. Under equal inference cost, multi-agent structure adds cost without clear benefit.

</details>


### [188] [Multi-dimensional Bias in Modeling Multi-dimensional Preferences: Evaluating the Ability of Synthetic Agents to Replace Human Participants in Conjoint Experiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04243)
*Ho Ting Hung,Nachiket Midha,Victor Y. Wu,Yiwen Zhang*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: LLMs show limited effectiveness in replicating human conjoint experiment results; synthetic agents can mimic some aggregate patterns but fail to fully capture multi-dimensional human preferences needed for robust substitution in survey experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is growing interest in using LLMs to reduce costs and add robustness to survey experiments, but their effectiveness in conjoint designs—popular in political science—remains underexplored. The paper addresses this gap by investigating whether synthetic agents can reproduce the multi-dimensional human preference patterns that conjoint experiments are designed to capture.

Method: The study replicates published conjoint experiments and compares results from synthetic agents with original human data across three dimensions: representational correspondence (choice distributions), inferential correspondence (statistical similarity of estimates), and procedural stability. It evaluates alignment of choice distributions and statistical/substantive similarity of estimates.

Result: Results are uneven across dimensions and replicated studies. Synthetic agents show some alignment in aggregate outputs (reproducing figures, obtaining sign agreement) but fail to consistently reproduce multi-dimensional human preference patterns. The validity of synthetic participants appears claim-dependent and hierarchical rather than universally applicable.

Conclusion: Synthetic agents cannot robustly substitute human samples in conjoint experiments. The discipline must first map the boundaries of this innovation across various levels before considering LLMs as valid replacements for human respondents. Reproducing aggregate outputs is insufficient evidence for replacing human participants.

Abstract: Despite growing interest in using LLMs to add robustness or reduce data-collection costs in survey experiments, their efficacy in conjoint design---an increasingly popular method in political science---remains underexplored. This paper addresses that gap by investigating whether synthetic agents can reproduce the multi-dimensional human preference patterns that conjoint is designed to capture. It replicates published conjoint studies and compares the results generated by synthetic agents with original human data along three dimensions: representational correspondence, inferential correspondence, and procedural stability. Our analysis evaluates the alignment of choice distributions as well as the statistical and substantive similarity of estimates, and the results are uneven across these dimensions and studies replicated. This implies that the validity of synthetic participants should be considered claim-dependent and hierarchical. Reproducing a figure or obtaining strong sign agreement is evidence of similar aggregate outputs, but not enough to support replacing human respondents. Our results suggest that the discipline as a whole must first map this innovation's boundaries across various levels before considering synthetic agents a robust substitute for human samples.

</details>


### [189] [EVOHARNESSBENCH: Can Your Agents Keep Pace with an Evolving Harness?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04280)
*Zixuan Ke,Vaidehi Patil,Haizhou Shi,Yang Li,Ye Liu,Sarath Shekkizhar,Anurag Koul,Jiayu Wang,Xuan Phi Nguyen,Semih Yavuz,Mohit Bansal,Shafiq Joty*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: EVOHARNESSBENCH is a new benchmark for evaluating LLM agents as their tool/skill/agent harness evolves, revealing three gaps: harness-induced forgetting, inconsistent adaptation benefits, and tension between retention and adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents operate with tool harnesses that continually evolve as new capabilities are added, but existing benchmarks test task non-stationarity while keeping harness fixed. There's a need to evaluate agents under controlled harness evolution.

Method: Created EVOHARNESSBENCH benchmark with 17 multi-stage harness streams from verifier-based benchmarks (802 tasks, 520 tools, 42 skills, 62 agents). Evaluated two settings: deployment evaluation (retention as harness expands) and self-evolving adaptation (utility of accumulated experience with new capabilities).

Result: Three persistent gaps identified: 1) Harness expansion alone degrades performance on previously solved tasks (harness-induced forgetting), 2) Gains from self-evolving adaptation inconsistent across evolution stages/capability axes/environments, 3) Retention and adaptation pull in different directions (preserving competence doesn't improve adaptation to new capabilities).

Conclusion: Harness evolution presents a distinct challenge for building agents that can keep pace with evolving capabilities while preserving effective behavior, requiring new approaches beyond current continual learning benchmarks.

Abstract: Modern LLM-based agents operate through a harness of tools, reusable skills, and specialist agents that shapes what they observe and what they can do. In practice, this harness continually evolves as new capabilities are added. We introduce EVOHARNESSBENCH, a benchmark for evaluating agents under controlled harness evolution across three axes (tools, skills, and agents). Unlike existing continual-learning benchmarks for agents, which typically place non-stationarity (i.e., what changes over time) in the task stream while keeping the harness fixed, EVOHARNESSBENCH places non-stationarity in the externally supplied harness itself. It contains 17 multi-stage harness streams constructed deterministically from verifier-based benchmarks, comprising 802 tasks, 520 tools, 42 skills, and 62 agents. We evaluate two complementary settings corresponding to the central challenges of harness evolution: deployment evaluation, which isolates retention of previously accessible competence as the harness expands, and self-evolving adaptation evaluation, which tests whether accumulated experience remains useful as new capabilities are introduced. Our results reveal three persistent gaps. First, harness expansion alone can degrade performance on previously solved tasks, producing harness-induced forgetting. Second, gains from self-evolving adaptation remain inconsistent across stages of harness evolution, capability axes, and environments. Third, retention and adaptation can pull in different directions: preserving earlier competence does not necessarily improve adaptation to newly introduced capabilities, and vice versa. These results establish harness evolution as a distinct challenge for building agents that can keep pace with an evolving harness while preserving previously effective behavior.

</details>


### [190] [Abstraction Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04303)
*Boning Li,Longbo Huang*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: A zero-shot pipeline using LLMs to automatically create game abstractions from natural language descriptions without domain-specific evaluators, training data, or game-tree traversal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional game abstraction methods require domain-specific evaluators (like hand-strength calculators) that need expert knowledge and engineering effort, making them unavailable for less-studied games.

Method: Four-phase pipeline: 1) LLM discovers continuous strategic features from game descriptions using calibration anchors, 2) batched private-state scoring, 3) correlation-based feature selection, and 4) k-means clustering to create abstraction buckets.

Result: Reduces lifted-strategy exploitability by up to 62% vs expected-hand-strength baseline on Texas hold'em turn endgames; beats scalar rank baseline on ROVER Trials; transfers to Omaha, Mahjong with discovered features tracking recognized strategic concepts.

Conclusion: LLMs can convert implicit strategic knowledge into explicit numerical features for game abstraction without domain-specific evaluators, enabling automatic abstraction construction for diverse games.

Abstract: Information abstraction, which groups strategically similar private states into a tractable number of buckets, is essential for scaling game-solving algorithms to large imperfect-information games. Constructing effective abstractions, however, has traditionally required domain-specific evaluators such as hand-strength calculators or equity estimators, which demand expert knowledge and engineering effort and are unavailable for most less-studied games. We propose the Abstraction Agent, a zero-shot pipeline that uses a large language model (LLM) to discover continuous strategic features from a natural-language game description, score private states on these features, and cluster them into abstraction buckets, without any game-specific evaluator, training data, or game-tree traversal during abstraction construction. The pipeline runs in four phases: feature discovery with calibration anchors, batched private-state scoring, correlation-based feature selection, and $k$-means clustering. The resulting abstractions reduce lifted-strategy exploitability by up to 62% relative to an expected-hand-strength baseline on heads-up no-limit Texas hold'em (HUNL) turn endgames, and beat a scalar rank baseline at every granularity on ROVER Trials, an original game absent from any pretraining corpus. Beyond these quantitative benchmarks, the pipeline transfers with unchanged prompts to four-card Pot-Limit Omaha, HUNL preflop and flop, and Riichi Mahjong, where the discovered features track each game's recognized strategic concepts. This is structured knowledge elicitation: converting implicit strategic knowledge in LLM parameters into explicit numerical features for downstream algorithmic computation. The code is available at https://github.com/lbn187/AbstractionAgent.

</details>


### [191] [Online Change-point Detection for Cooperative Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05298)
*Fatemeh Saberi Khomami,Julita Vassileva*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: PPR is a lightweight, algorithm-agnostic change-point detector for MARL that uses reward patterns to identify significant environmental shifts during training, balancing detection speed with alarm stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cooperative MARL systems need to recognize when environment or task objectives change during training so agents can adapt, as past experience becomes unreliable under non-stationary conditions.

Method: Patterns of Past Rewards (PPR) - an algorithm that smooths agents' return streams, highlights recent changes, and applies statistical drift detection to flag significant shifts using reward-derived signals.

Result: PPR offers balanced performance: smoother-return baselines detect earlier but produce many repeated alarms, while raw return detectors often miss shifts. PPR limits redundant detections while still identifying controlled shifts.

Conclusion: PPR is an effective lightweight, reward-based monitoring tool that enables cooperative MARL systems to reliably identify major changes during training, addressing the trade-off between detection speed and alarm stability.

Abstract: Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) systems rely on past experience for learning coordinated behaviour, but this experience may become unreliable if the environment or task objective changes during training. In such cases, agents first need a way to recognize that the situation has changed before deciding how to adapt. This paper studies online change-point detection for cooperative MARL using reward-derived signals. We propose \emph{Patterns of Past Rewards} (PPR), a lightweight algorithm-agnostic detector that smooths agents' return streams, highlights recent changes, and applies a statistical drift detector to flag significant shifts. We evaluate PPR in a custom Speaker-Listener environment based on the Multi-Agent Particle Environment under two controlled non-stationarity scenarios. Our results show a trade-off between detection speed and alarm stability. A smoothed-return baseline detects earlier but produces many repeated alarms. In contrast, applying the detector directly to raw returns often misses the shift. PPR offers a more balanced approach by limiting redundant detections while still identifying the controlled shifts. These findings highlight PPR as a lightweight, reward-based monitoring tool that enables cooperative MARL systems to reliably identify major changes during training.

</details>


### [192] [Trust-Aware Adaptive Disclosure for Inference Privacy Preservation in Multi-Agent Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05340)
*Puspanjali Ghoshal,Tobias J. Oechtering*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: A trust-aware privacy control framework for multi-agent systems that protects agents' latent goals from adversarial inference while maintaining consensus performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent systems are increasingly used in critical applications like healthcare and smart grids where agents have sensitive latent goals that need protection from adversarial observation and inference attacks.

Method: Proposed Trust-Aware Privacy Control framework that uses dynamic trust relationships between agents to adapt message disclosure through a trust-dependent stochastic policy, balancing information release for consensus against privacy preservation.

Result: The method reduces adversarial goal inference accuracy compared to baseline approaches while maintaining competitive consensus utility, demonstrating effective privacy-performance tradeoff.

Conclusion: Trust-aware mechanisms are effective for privacy preservation in multi-agent systems, enabling protection against goal inference attacks without significantly compromising consensus performance in information-critical applications.

Abstract: Agent based systems are increasingly deployed in information critical systems including healthcare management systems, and smart grids. In this paper, we consider a multi-agent system where each agent has a latent goal that needs to be kept hidden from observing adversaries. More specifically, this paper studies privacy-preserving consensus in networked multi-agent systems under goal inference attacks. We propose a Trust-Aware Privacy Control framework that adapts message disclosure based on the dynamic trust relationships between agents. The proposed method controls information release using a trust-dependent stochastic policy. This enables a tradeoff between consensus performance and privacy preservation. Experiments demonstrate that the proposed method reduces adversarial goal inference accuracy compared to representative baselines, while maintaining competitive consensus utility, thereby highlighting the effectiveness of trust-aware mechanisms in privacy preservation of the agents in multi-agent systems.
